# Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ (Chapter 26) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah of Poets and Prophets**

Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ — "The Poets" — is the twenty-sixth chapter of the Noble Qur'an and takes its name from verse 224, where the poets (*shuʿarāʾ*) of pre-Islamic Arabia are addressed. It is a Makkan sūrah according to the majority of scholars, though some commentators, including Ālūsī, al-Qurṭubī, and al-Ṭabarī, held that a small number of verses at its end (vv. 224–227) may have been revealed in Madinah, while others included verse 197 among the Madinan verses. The sūrah contains 227 verses and sits in the fifteenth juzʾ of the Qur'an.

**A Chapter Built on Repetition and Refrain**

One of the most striking formal features of al-Shuʿarāʾ is its rhythm. After the opening verses — which address the Prophet's ﷺ grief over his people's rejection, affirm the Quran's divine origin, and invite reflection upon the signs of God in the natural world — the sūrah launches into an extended sequence of prophetic narratives. These narratives are linked together by a recurring refrain that appears at the end of each prophetic account:

> *"Truly in that is a sign, but most of them are not believers. And truly thy Lord is the Mighty, the Merciful."*

This refrain appears seven times: after the stories of Moses (vv. 67–68), Abraham (vv. 103–104), Noah (vv. 121–122), Hūd (vv. 139–140), Ṣāliḥ (vv. 158–159), Lot (vv. 174–175), and Shuʿayb (vv. 190–191). The refrain is not mere ornamentation; it performs a structural and theological function. Structurally, it binds the individual narratives into a single arc — one long argument that the message has always been the same, the rejection has always followed the same pattern, and the outcome has always confirmed the same truth. Theologically, the refrain holds two truths in tension: the sign is clear (*a sign*), yet most do not believe (*but most of them are not believers*); and God is simultaneously overwhelming in power (*the Mighty*) and inexhaustible in compassion (*the Merciful*). The pairing of these two Names — al-ʿAzīz and al-Raḥīm — at the close of every prophetic cycle tells the reader that judgment and mercy are not rivals but partners in the divine economy.

**The Prophetic Patterns Within the Sūrah**

Each prophet's story follows a recognizable pattern: the prophet is sent to his people as a "trustworthy messenger" (*rasūl amīn*); he calls them to reverence God (*ittaqu Llāh*) and obey him; he reminds them that he seeks no reward from them, for his reward lies only with the Lord of the worlds; the people reject him, often with the same stock objections — "you are but a human being like us," "you are bewitched," "we found our fathers doing so"; the prophet disowns their actions and turns to God; and God delivers the believers and destroys the deniers. This pattern is not repetitive out of laziness in the text; it is repetitive because the point is precisely that the pattern never changed. Every prophet brought the same core message; every entrenched elite responded with the same reflexes; and the same God administered the same justice. The repetition is a pedagogical hammer-stroke.

**Why the Stories of Prophets in a Makkan Sūrah?**

Makkan sūrahs are typically concerned with the fundamentals of faith: God's oneness, the reality of resurrection, the accountability of the soul. Narrative might seem out of place in such a setting, yet al-Shuʿarāʾ devotes the majority of its verses to recounting the stories of Moses, Abraham, Noah, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Lot, and Shuʿayb. The wisdom is multilayered. First, the stories comfort the Prophet ﷺ, who is grieving over his people's rejection (vv. 3–4): he is shown that every prophet before him faced the same denial, and the same God delivered them. Second, the stories warn the Quraysh: the fate of past nations is not ancient history but a mirror. Third, the stories teach the mechanics of prophecy itself — that the message is one, that rejection follows predictable patterns, and that God's justice operates across time with perfect consistency. Fourth, the stories demolish the accusation that the Qur'an was fabricated: no single human could have composed such a coherent and detailed narrative cycle of events that occurred across centuries and lands.

**The Poets and the Final Section**

After the prophetic narratives conclude, the sūrah shifts to a different register. Verses 192–212 affirm the divine origin of the Qur'an: it was brought down by the Trustworthy Spirit (Gabriel) upon the Prophet's heart, in clear Arabic, consistent with the scriptures of old, and confirmed by the knowledge of the learned among the Children of Israel. The satans did not bring it; it is beyond their capacity and their nature. Then, in the final verses (221–227), the sūrah turns to the poets — figures whose words in pre-Islamic Arabia were often believed to carry a supernatural or even demonic inspiration. The Qur'an distances itself from the poets whose words wander without purpose and who say what they do not do — but it carves out an exception for believing poets who remember God much and defend the truth after having been wronged. This ending is not incidental: it is the sūrah's final argument about the source of speech. The Qur'an came from the Lord of the worlds through the Trustworthy Spirit; the poets' words came from elsewhere. The reader is invited to judge which source deserves his soul.

**How to Read What Follows**

The commentary that follows treats each of the 227 verses as an independent discourse, in strict verse-by-verse order. Every verse is quoted individually, then expanded through Arabic language analysis, the classical commentators (al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī, al-Zamakhsharī, and others), authenticated Prophetic reports with their collections identified, the logic of its teaching, and its bearing upon modern life. Verses that the source commentary treats in groups are here separated and given full individual treatment. Nothing is compressed because of the chapter's length.

---

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:1

> **Ṭā. Sīn. Mīm.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Disconnected Letters and the Rhetoric of Surprise**

Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ opens with three isolated letters: *Ṭā. Sīn. Mīm.* — Ṭāʾ, Sīn, Mīm. These belong to the family of *al-ḥurūf al-muqaṭṭaʿāt*, the "disconnected letters" that appear at the beginning of twenty-nine sūrahs of the Qur'an. The letters do not form a word in Arabic; they are recited by their individual names, each standing alone. This opening is not a sentence — it is an interruption. The listener expecting narrative or command receives instead the raw alphabet itself, as if language were being stripped to its foundations before the edifice of meaning is raised upon it.

**The Letters as a Silent Challenge**

The most widely accepted interpretation among the classical exegetes — reported from Ibn ʿAbbās and developed at length by al-Ṭabarī — holds that these letters serve as a tacit challenge to the Arabs' eloquence. Al-Rāzī explains the logic: these letters are the Arabs' own alphabet, the building blocks of every poem and oration they composed. By opening with them, the Qur'an effectively says: "Here are the very materials of your own language — and yet you cannot produce anything like this Book." The challenge is made explicit elsewhere:

> *"Say: If mankind and the jinn were to gather in order to produce the like of this Qur'an, they could not produce the like of it, even if they supported one another."* (Qur'an 17:88)

The letters stand at the threshold like a gatekeeper's challenge: you know these sounds, you use them every day — but can you build from them what God has built?

**Symbolic Interpretations from the Sufi Tradition**

Alongside the publicly argued interpretations, the commentators preserve richer symbolic readings. Al-Qushayrī and other Sufi commentators report interpretations in which each letter represents a divine attribute or spiritual station. Some say *Ṭāʾ* stands for *ṭarab* — the joy of the hearts of those who know God; *Sīn* for *surūr* — the happiness of the lovers of God; and *Mīm* for *munājah* — the intimate discourse of those who desire God. Others say *Ṭāʾ* represents God's Might (*ṭawl*), *Sīn* His Resplendence (*sināʾ*), and *Mīm* His Sovereignty (*mulk*). Yet others suggest they stand for Divine Names: al-Ṭāhir (the Pure), al-Salām (Peace), and al-Majīd (the Glorious). Al-Ṭabarī notes that some considered them a name for this sūrah itself, or a name of the Qur'an, or an oath that God swears by them.

**The Wisdom of Ambiguity**

Why does God open with letters whose meaning He has chosen not to specify? Al-Zamakhsharī offers a perceptive answer: if the letters had a fixed, publicly known meaning, the Arabs could claim to understand the alphabet of the Qur'an and thus imagine they could replicate it. By keeping the meaning concealed, the mystery deepens — and with it, the recognition that the Qur'an operates on a level of language beyond human manufacture. Furthermore, the ambiguity itself is a form of invitation: every generation of readers encounters these letters fresh, without the exhaustion that comes from full explanation. They are an open door that never closes.

**Reverence Before the Recitation Begins**

On a practical level, the disconnected letters serve as a pause — a moment of recalibration before the weighty content that follows. Just as one pauses before entering a mosque, the listener pauses at these letters. The alphabet is the most basic unit of literacy; by beginning with it, God reminds the reader that everything that follows — prophecy, history, law, warning, glad tidings — is constructed from the simplest possible elements, yet arranged in a way that no human assembly could match. The lesson is one that applies far beyond Arabic: the most profound truths are often carried in the simplest vessels.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:2

> **These are the signs of the clear Book.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From Letters to Signs: The Transition**

Verse 2 immediately follows the disconnected letters and establishes what they point toward: "These are the signs (*āyāt*) of the clear Book (*kitāb mubīn*)." The word *āyāt* — literally "signs" — is the same word used throughout the Qur'an for individual verses. Each verse of the Qur'an is thus simultaneously a unit of speech and a sign pointing beyond itself. The word *kitāb* — "Book" — implies something written, compiled, and authoritative: not a scattered collection of sayings but a coherent, preserved document with binding force. The adjective *mubīn* — "clear" — derives from the root *b-y-n*, meaning to make manifest, to distinguish, to clarify. The Book is not obscure or encrypted; its message is accessible to anyone who approaches it with sincerity.

**The Clarity That Is Both Gift and Test**

That the Book is *mubīn* — clear — is a mercy: God does not hide His message in codes or riddles. But clarity is also a test. When something is unmistakably clear, there is no excuse for misunderstanding. The disbelievers of Makkah could not plead confusion; the message was plain. This is why the Qur'an so frequently pairs the quality of clarity with the reality of rejection: the problem is never with the message but with the hearts that receive it.

> *"We have sent it down as an Arabic Qur'an so that you may understand."* (Qur'an 12:2)

The clarity of the Book is further reinforced by the use of *bayyina* — the same root — as a common description of the Qur'an's verses throughout the text (10:15; 22:16; 24:34; 28:36; 57:9; 65:11). The Book makes things clear; and those who encounter it are themselves made clear — their true states exposed, as light reveals what darkness concealed.

**The Word "These" and the Referent**

The demonstrative pronoun "These" (*tilka*) refers back to the disconnected letters of verse 1 — or, according to another reading, forward to the verses of the sūrah that follow. Either way, the message is the same: what you are about to hear is not ordinary speech. It is composed of signs, each one pointing to something larger than itself. The letters are signs of the Book; the verses are signs of the truth; the stories are signs of God's pattern with humanity; and the entire Book is a sign of God's mercy to the worlds.

**The Book as an Ongoing Reality**

The word *kitāb* — Book — in the Qur'anic usage carries more than the meaning of a physical volume. It denotes a settled, authoritative, divinely ordained communication — a standard by which judgment is made. Al-Rāzī observes that the description "clear Book" functions as a description of the entire revelatory enterprise: the truth has been inscribed, preserved, and made accessible. It is not a secret reserved for the elite; it is a public document addressed to all who have ears to hear. The task of the reader is not to decode but to receive — and having received, to act.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:3

> **Perhaps thou dost torment thyself that they are not believers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prophet's Grief and God's Compassion**

Verse 3 is among the most tender verses in the Qur'an. God addresses His Prophet ﷺ directly, acknowledging the pain that the Prophet's deep concern for his people has caused him. The word translated as "torment" is *bākhiʿ*, from the root *b-kh-ʿ*, which literally means to kill, to destroy, or to fight. It conveys the idea of a grief so consuming that it borders on self-destruction. The Prophet ﷺ was not indifferent to his people's rejection; he did not say, "They chose their path; let them perish." Rather, he was so consumed by concern for their guidance that he was literally harming himself with sorrow. God's response is not to scold him for caring too much, but to gently redirect that caring.

**The Psychology of the Messenger's Burden**

The psychological dimension of this verse is profound. The Prophet ﷺ is described in the Qur'an elsewhere with similar tenderness:

> *"There has come to you a Messenger from among yourselves; grievous to him is your suffering, solicitous for you, compassionate and merciful to the believers."* (Qur'an 9:128)

The word *ḥarīṣ* — "solicitous" — carries the same emotional weight as *bākhiʿ*. The Prophet's pain was not weakness; it was the pain of one who sees clearly what others refuse to see, and who loves them enough to grieve their blindness. This is the burden of every true reformer, every parent who watches a child make destructive choices, every teacher whose students squander their potential. The Qur'an acknowledges this pain as real, valid, and human — but then asks the Prophet to place it in the right context.

**The Limits of Human Responsibility**

The theological principle embedded here is one of the most important in Islamic thought: no person is ultimately responsible for the guidance of another. The Prophet's task was to deliver the message — clearly, completely, compassionately — but the acceptance or rejection of that message was between each individual soul and God. This principle is repeated throughout the Qur'an:

> *"Thou guidest not whom thou lovest; but God guides whom He wills."* (Qur'an 28:56)

> *"Thou art not a keeper over them."* (Qur'an 88:22)

> *"If God had willed, they would not have been idolaters."* (Qur'an 6:107)

The logic is merciful: it frees the messenger from a burden that no human being was designed to carry. The Prophet ﷺ was a man, not a savior. He could warn, invite, and exemplify; he could not force hearts open. That task belonged to God alone.

**A Comfort for Every Caller to Truth**

This verse is not only about the Prophet ﷺ; it is a standing comfort for everyone who calls others to truth and meets with rejection. The teacher whose students refuse to learn, the parent whose children reject guidance, the preacher whose congregation remains unmoved — all of them can find in this verse the same reassurance: your task is delivery, not outcome. The results belong to God. Al-Qurṭubī notes that this verse was intended to console the Prophet ﷺ and to remind him that the state of the souls of those he addressed was a matter for God to determine, not his. The verse is a masterclass in setting boundaries between what is in your control and what is not — a principle that modern psychology calls the "dichotomy of control" and that Islamic spirituality has taught for fourteen centuries under the name of *tawakkul* — trusting the outcome to God after doing your part.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:4

> **If We will, We will send down a sign from Heaven and their heads would remain bowed before it in humility.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Power God Could Deploy But Choses Not To**

God reminds the Prophet ﷺ — and through him, the reader — of a point that is both awesome and restrained: if God willed, He could send down a visible, undeniable sign from the heavens that would force every head to bow. The word for "sign" here is *āyah* — the same word used for verses of the Qur'an — but in this context it refers to a miraculous, visible event. The word *aʿnāq* — translated as "heads" — literally means "necks," and the image is of necks bowed in submission. In Arabic, the neck is the seat of pride: a person who "cranes his neck" is displaying arrogance; a person whose neck is bowed has been humbled. The image is of the most stubborn, arrogant deniers physically compelled to submit — their necks bent under the weight of an undeniable sign.

**The Wisdom of Not Forcing Belief**

Why does God not deploy this overwhelming sign? The question is one that runs through the entire Qur'an. The answer, given repeatedly, is that God created human beings with the capacity to choose — and that choice is only meaningful when it is not coerced. A faith extracted by overwhelming visual proof is not faith; it is capitulation. The Qur'an's argument throughout is that the signs already present — the Qur'an itself, the natural world, the human conscience, the stories of past nations — are sufficient for those who approach with sincerity. For those who do not, even the most dramatic miracle would not suffice:

> *"Even if We opened to them a gate of Heaven and they continued to ascend through it, they would say, 'Our eyes have been dazzled; rather, we are a people bewitched.'"* (Qur'an 15:14–15)

The problem is not with the evidence; it is with the hearts. And hearts are not changed by spectacle alone.

**The Multiple Meanings of "Necks"**

Al-Rāzī and al-Qurṭubī note that *aʿnāq* carries multiple meanings that enrich the verse. Beyond "necks" and "heads," it can signify the leaders or notables of a group — the "top brass" — in which case the verse means their most powerful figures would be humbled. It can also signify "groups" or "communities," in which case entire tribes and clans would be humbled together. Each reading adds a dimension: not only individuals but also the powerful, and not only the powerful but entire societies — all brought low before the reality they had denied.

**A Lesson in Divine Pedagogy**

The broader lesson is about how God educates humanity. He does not overwhelm with brute force; He invites with signs that are clear enough for the sincere but ambiguous enough to leave room for choice. The Qur'an itself is the supreme example of this pedagogy: it is a book that can be read, studied, and understood — but it can also be dismissed by those who choose to dismiss it. This is not a flaw in the Book; it is a feature of the test. The test requires that the evidence be sufficient for the honest seeker and insufficient for the dishonest denier. Verse 4 reminds us that God could have removed the ambiguity at any moment — and that His choice not to do so is an act of mercy, not neglect.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:5

> **Yet no new reminder comes to them from the Compassionate, but that they turn away from it.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Freshness of Each Revelation**

The word *muḥdath* — "new" — carries a richness that the English translation only partially captures. It means "recent," "freshly brought," and also "something that has come into being at a particular moment." This is precisely the quality of the Qur'an's gradual revelation: it did not descend all at once as a single volume but came in installments, each one a fresh *dhikr* — reminder — meeting the community at its current moment of need. Al-Rāzī explains that this gradualness was deliberate: each new portion of revelation addressed current circumstances, answered pressing questions, and renewed the urgency of the message in the hearts of believers and deniers alike.

**The Pattern of Turning Away**

The verse describes a behavioral pattern: every time a new reminder arrives, they turn away (*aʿraḍū*). The verb *aʿraḍa* means to turn one's face away, to avert, to disregard. It is the physical gesture of someone who does not want to see or hear — the turning of the cheek, the avoidance of the eye. This is not thoughtful disagreement; it is reflexive avoidance. The deniers of Makkah did not engage with the Qur'an's arguments and find them wanting; they simply turned their faces away whenever it was recited. Al-Ṭabarī reports that this pattern was visible in Makkah: when the Qur'an was recited, the disbelievers would cover their ears, raise their voices in mockery, or simply walk away.

> *"And those who disbelieve say, 'Do not listen to this Qur'an and make noise during its recitation, so that perhaps you will prevail.'"* (Qur'an 41:26)

**The Theological Significance of "From the Compassionate"**

The verse specifies that the reminder comes "from the Compassionate" (*al-Raḥmān*). This is not incidental. The Most Compassionate is the Name of God that encompasses all mercy — it is the Name from which all created mercy derives. To turn away from a reminder that comes from the source of all mercy is to turn away from the very thing one needs most. It is like a patient turning away from the physician who has come to heal him. The irony is devastating: the more they turn away, the more they deprive themselves of the mercy they were designed to receive.

**Muḥdath as a Technical Term**

Al-Rāzī notes that *muḥdath* would later become a technical term in Islamic theology and philosophy, signifying the fact of something being created in time — something that came into existence after not existing. In this theological sense, the verse carries a subtle metaphysical claim: each new portion of revelation is a fresh act of creation, a new manifestation of God's speech arriving at a specific moment in history. The Qur'an is not a static document that happened once; it is a living communication that engaged its recipients moment by moment. This is one of the reasons the Qur'an describes itself as being "sent down" (*munazzal*) — the verb is in the continuous form, implying ongoing descent.

**The Modern Echo of Turning Away**

This verse describes a phenomenon that is universal and timeless. Human beings have an extraordinary capacity to avoid truths that are inconvenient. The modern equivalent is the person who scrolls past articles that challenge his worldview, who changes the channel when an uncomfortable truth appears on screen, who avoids conversations that might force him to reconsider his life. The mechanism is the same: turning away (*iʿrāḍ*) is the soul's default defense against change. The Qur'an names this defense for what it is and invites the reader to notice it in himself.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:6

> **Indeed, they have denied it, but there shall come to them the tidings of that which they used to mock.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Denial and Its Inevitable Consequence**

The verse moves from the behavior of turning away to the deeper reality behind it: denial (*kadhdhabū*). The root *k-dh-b* means to declare something false, to reject as a lie. The disbelievers did not merely ignore the Qur'an; they actively declared it false. And yet God's response is not an immediate punishment but a promise: "there shall come to them the tidings" (*yaʾtīhim anbāʾu*). The word *anbāʾ* — "tidings" — carries the weight of news that is certain, comprehensive, and ultimately unavoidable. The deniers mocked what they did not understand; the "tidings" are the reality of what they mocked, arriving in full.

**The Irony of "Tidings"**

The word "tidings" (*anbāʾ*) normally connotes good news. Here it is used with devastating irony: the news that comes to the deniers is not glad tidings but the terrible confirmation of everything they denied. Al-Qurṭubī draws attention to this rhetorical device: what the deniers treated as a joke will become their reality. The mockery itself becomes part of the punishment, because it represents the attitude with which they met the truth — not ignorant rejection but deliberate contempt. Contempt is worse than ignorance, because ignorance can be cured by education, while contempt poisons the very capacity to receive.

**The Scope of the Coming News**

Al-Rāzī explains that the "tidings" can refer to events in this world — the military defeats the Quraysh would suffer at Badr and elsewhere — or to the events of the Hereafter — the full revelation of the truth on the Day of Judgment. Both fulfillments occurred. In this world, the Quraysh saw their most powerful leaders killed at Badr, their plans thwarted, and their civilization ultimately transformed by the very message they mocked. In the Hereafter, every soul will see the full reality of what it denied. The verse thus operates on two time-scales simultaneously, a feature common in Qur'anic eschatological language: the punishment is both imminent (for those who will see it in their lifetimes) and ultimate (for the final accounting).

**The Psychology of Mockery**

Mockery is one of the most common defenses against uncomfortable truth. It allows the mocker to feel superior without engaging with the substance of what is mocked. The Quraysh mocked the Prophet ﷺ, his message, and the very idea of resurrection — not because they had refuted it, but because acknowledging it would require them to change their lives. Modern psychology calls this "defensive ridicule," and it operates exactly as the Qur'an describes: as a way of avoiding engagement with truth by reducing it to absurdity. The verse promises that mockery does not neutralize truth; it only delays the encounter with it — and when that encounter comes, the mocker will find that what he laughed at is the most serious thing he ever faced.

**A Warning That Extends Beyond Makkah**

The verse's principle is universal. Every generation produces its mockers — those who treat sacred truths as jokes, who scoff at warnings of accountability, who dismiss the reality of death and judgment with irony and humor. This verse reminds them that mockery changes nothing about the reality mocked. The news will come. The question is only whether the recipient will be ready for it.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:7

> **Have they not considered the earth, how much of every noble kind We have caused to grow therein?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Earth as a Sign Book**

After discussing the disbelievers' rejection, the sūrah pivots to one of the Qur'an's most characteristic arguments: look at the natural world. The word "considered" (*yanẓurū*) means to look, to observe, to reflect upon. The command is to look at the earth — not abstractly but concretely, at the specific reality of what grows from it. "Every noble kind" (*zawj karīm*) means every excellent, generous, beautiful species of plant. The word *karīm* — "noble" — is normally used for persons of high character, but here it is applied to plants, elevating the natural world by attributing to it a quality usually reserved for human virtue. This is not mere poetry; it is a theological statement: everything God created carries within it a mark of His generosity.

**The Meaning of "Kind" as "Pair"**

Al-Rāzī and other commentators observe that *zawj* also means "pair" — and in this meaning, the verse can be read as a reference to Adam and Eve, "the most noble pair," from whom all prophets and saints descend. Sufi commentators draw from this the spiritual teaching that human beings themselves are among the "noble kinds" that God has caused to grow from the earth: Adam was created from clay, and from him all humanity propagated. The earth is thus not only the source of vegetation but the source of human life itself — and every human being is a noble kind, a pairing of body and soul, clay and spirit.

**Al-Rāzī on the Hidden Benefit of All Growing Things**

Al-Rāzī makes a remarkable observation: every growing thing has some benefit, even if human beings are not yet aware of it. Modern science has confirmed this insight: plants that were once considered useless weeds have been discovered to contain medicinal compounds; ecosystems that appeared barren have been found to perform critical functions in the web of life. The verse invites a posture of humility toward the natural world: the fact that you do not yet understand a plant's benefit does not mean it has none. The Creator's wisdom exceeds human knowledge, and the earth is full of signs for those who look carefully.

**The Agricultural Metaphor for the Soul**

Sufyān al-Thawrī is reported to have said: "Human beings are among the growing things of the earth; whosoever enters the Garden is noble (*karīm*), and whosoever enters the Fire is wretched." This is a profound spiritual reading: the same earth that produces noble vegetation also produces thorns and bitter plants. Similarly, the human soul — cultivated by the same divine nurture — produces either the noble fruits of faith and good deeds or the bitter harvest of disbelief and sin. The difference is not in the soil or the rain but in the seed — and the seed is the state of the heart.

**The Invitation to Observation**

For the modern reader, this verse is an invitation to what might be called "contemplative science" — looking at the natural world not merely to exploit it but to learn from it, to see in its diversity and generosity a reflection of the Creator's attributes. Every time a botanist discovers a new use for a plant, every time a farmer marvels at the generosity of the soil, every time a person pauses to appreciate the beauty of a flower — they are, whether they know it or not, engaging in the act the Qur'an commands: considering the earth and recognizing the noble kinds that grow within it.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:8

> **Truly in that is a sign, but most of them are not believers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sign Hidden in Plain Sight**

This verse is the first occurrence of a refrain that will punctuate the sūrah at regular intervals, appearing seven times in total — once after each prophetic narrative. The refrain consists of two clauses: first, an assertion that what has just been recounted constitutes a sign (*āyah*); second, the observation that most people do not believe. The "sign" in this verse refers to the earth's vegetation described in verse 7 — the diversity, beauty, and generosity of what grows from the soil. But the word *āyah* is deliberately ambiguous: it means both the individual botanical miracle and the broader lesson to be drawn from it. The sign is not merely the plant; the sign is what the plant tells you about its Creator.

**"Most of Them" — A Recurring Diagnosis**

The phrase "most of them are not believers" is not a statement of despair but of realistic diagnosis. It appears throughout the Qur'an (7:10; 12:103; 34:28; and repeatedly in this sūrah) and serves multiple functions. First, it normalizes the experience of the believer who finds himself in a minority: you are not alone; most people in most ages have not believed. Second, it prevents the believer from assuming that majority opinion is a reliable guide to truth: the majority is often wrong. Third, it comforts the Prophet ﷺ by showing him that his experience is not unique — every prophet before him faced a majority that did not believe.

**The Logic of the Sign**

The verse's structure is an argument: "Truly in that is a sign." The word "in" (*fī*) is important: the sign is *within* the phenomenon described, not external to it. The sign is not a separate miracle bolted onto the natural world; it is embedded in the natural world itself. The earth's vegetation — its diversity, its reliability, its generosity — is itself the sign. This is a distinctive feature of the Qur'anic mode of argument: it does not ask you to believe in something beyond nature; it asks you to see nature itself as the evidence. The miracle is not that God occasionally suspends the natural order; the miracle is that the natural order exists at all, functions with such precision, and produces such beauty.

**The First Occurrence of the Refrain**

That this is the first of seven occurrences of the refrain is structurally significant. It establishes a pattern that the sūrah will repeat: narrative → sign → observation about belief. This pattern teaches the reader a method of reading: every story you encounter in this sūrah is not merely history but a sign — something to be reflected upon, not merely consumed as information. The refrain is the sūrah's way of slowing the reader down: pause here. Think about what you just read. The sign is in it — if you are among those who see.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:9

> **And truly thy Lord is the Mighty, the Merciful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Pairing of Might and Mercy**

The second clause of the refrain names two of God's attributes: *al-ʿAzīz* — the Mighty — and *al-Raḥīm* — the Merciful. These are not random selections from the ninety-nine Names; they are a deliberate pairing that captures a central tension of the Qur'anic message. God is absolutely powerful — nothing can resist Him, nothing can thwart His will, nothing can escape His reach. And God is absolutely merciful — His compassion exceeds His wrath, His forgiveness outstrips human sin, His grace precedes human deserving. The two Names together tell the complete story: power without mercy would be tyranny; mercy without power would be impotence. God is both, and the combination is what makes His judgment just and His forgiveness meaningful.

**Why This Pairing at the End of Every Prophetic Story**

The choice of this specific pairing as the refrain that concludes each prophetic narrative is deeply significant. After each story of a prophet's rejection and his people's destruction, the reader might be left with a sense of overwhelming divine severity — nations wiped out, populations drowned, cities overturned. The refrain corrects this potential misreading: yes, God is Mighty enough to destroy those who persistently reject the truth; but He is also Merciful — He sent prophets in the first place, He gave time for repentance, He delivered the believers. The destruction was not His first choice; it was His last resort after every other avenue had been exhausted.

**The Comfort to the Prophet**

For the Prophet ﷺ, the refrain is also a personal comfort. After recounting the stories of prophets who were rejected, mocked, and threatened — stories that mirror his own experience — the Prophet is reminded that his Lord is both powerful enough to protect him and merciful enough to care for him. The Mighty will not let the message fail; the Merciful will not abandon the messenger or the believers who follow him.

**The Theological Balance**

Al-Rāzī observes that the pairing of Might and Mercy in the refrain reflects a deeper theological principle: the Qur'an consistently refuses to let the reader rest in a one-sided image of God. After descriptions of punishment, it introduces mercy; after descriptions of grace, it introduces accountability. This is not inconsistency; it is completeness. The God of the Qur'an is not a God of wrath only, nor a God of love only — He is the God who is both, and who calls human beings to hold both realities simultaneously. The refrain teaches this balance in seven repetitions, ensuring that no reader can leave the sūrah with a distorted image of the Divine.

**The Refrain as a Liturgical Pulse**

From a literary perspective, the refrain functions like a liturgical pulse — a recurring heartbeat that gives the sūrah its rhythm. Just as the reciter of a long prayer finds comfort in the repeated phrases that structure his worship, the reader of al-Shuʿarāʾ finds in the refrain a moment of rest, reflection, and recalibration. After the intensity of each narrative — the confrontation with Pharaoh, the destruction of ʿĀd, the flooding of Noah's people — the refrain provides a moment to breathe, to remember who God is, and to continue reading with the right frame of reference.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:10

> **And [remember] when thy Lord called out to Moses, "Go to the wrongdoing people,"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Transition from Signs to Stories**

Verse 10 marks a major structural transition in the sūrah. The opening section — which addressed the Prophet's grief, affirmed the Qur'an's divine origin, invited reflection on natural signs, and established the refrain — now gives way to the first and longest of the prophetic narratives: the story of Moses and Pharaoh. This narrative will occupy verses 10–66, the longest single prophetic account in the sūrah. Its length is not accidental: Moses' confrontation with Pharaoh is the archetypal story of truth facing entrenched power, and it is the story most directly relevant to the Prophet's ﷺ own situation in Makkah — a single messenger standing before an arrogant, powerful establishment that controlled the religious and political order.

**"Thy Lord Called Out"**

The phrase "thy Lord called out" (*nādā rabbuka*) uses the verb *nādā*, which connotes calling from a distance, raising the voice to reach someone far away. This is not the intimate whisper of inspiration but the formal commissioning of a prophet — a public, authoritative summons. Al-Ṭabarī explains that this "calling out" refers to the event at the burning bush, described in greater detail in Sūrah Ṭā Hā (20:9–48) and Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ (28:29–35), where Moses saw a fire on Mount Ṭūr, approached it, and heard God's voice commissioning him to go to Pharaoh. The detail is important: Moses was not seeking prophecy; he was traveling with his family, saw a fire, and went toward it expecting to bring back news or warmth. The divine call came to him in the course of an ordinary journey — a reminder that God's most extraordinary commissions can arrive in the most ordinary moments.

**"The Wrongdoing People"**

God describes Pharaoh's people as "the wrongdoing people" (*al-qawm al-ẓālimīn*). The word *ẓālimūn* — "wrongdoers" — is a careful description. They are not called "the disbelieving people" or "the sinful people" but "the wronging people" — people who have wronged others. This is significant because Pharaoh's oppression was not merely theological rebellion; it was concrete, systemic injustice. He enslaved the Children of Israel, killed their newborn sons, and maintained his power through terror and exploitation. The Qur'an's choice to frame the conflict as one between a messenger and "wrongdoers" — rather than between a prophet and "disbelievers" — highlights the social dimension of the story: this is not only about belief but about justice. God sends His messenger not only to correct theology but to dismantle oppression.

**The Relevance to the Prophet's Situation**

The parallel between Moses' situation and the Prophet Muhammad's ﷺ situation is striking. Both were messengers sent to their own people, both faced an entrenched and powerful elite, both were accused of sorcery and madness, and both were ultimately vindicated by God. The story of Moses is thus not only a historical account but a mirror held up to the Prophet ﷺ: what happened to Moses will happen to you; what God did for Moses, He will do for you. Al-Rāzī observes that the sūrah places the Moses story first among the prophetic narratives precisely because it is the most directly relevant to the Prophet's ﷺ situation — a confrontation with the most powerful oppressor of the age, resolved not by military force but by divine signs and unwavering faith.

**The Grammar of Commission**

The imperative "Go" (*an idhhab*) is direct and unadorned — no preamble, no qualification. This is the grammar of divine commission: God does not negotiate with the one He has chosen; He sends him. The prophet's task is not to evaluate whether the mission is feasible or whether the people are likely to respond; his task is to go. The outcome belongs to God; the movement belongs to the prophet. This principle — that the messenger's responsibility is delivery, not outcome — will recur throughout the sūrah in every prophetic narrative.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:11

> **the people of Pharaoh. Will they not be reverent?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Specifying the Wrongdoers**

Verse 11 specifies who the "wrongdoing people" are: "the people of Pharaoh" (*qawma Firʿawn*). The construction is significant: they are defined by their relationship to Pharaoh — they are his people, his followers, his constituency. This is not merely a geographic or ethnic designation; it is a moral one. To be "the people of Pharaoh" is to be aligned with his system of oppression, to benefit from his injustice, and to share in his worldview. The Qur'an elsewhere makes clear that Pharaoh's people were complicit in his crimes:

> *"And he stirred his people to complacency, and they obeyed him. Truly they were a transgressing people."* (Qur'an 43:54)

The lesson is that oppression requires a constituency — people willing to follow, benefit, and remain silent.

**The Question "Will They Not Be Reverent?"**

God's command to Moses includes a question: "Will they not be reverent?" The word translated as "reverent" is *yattaqūn*, from the root *w-q-y*, meaning to guard, to protect, to be mindful. *Taqwā* — often translated as "fear of God" — is more accurately described as a state of vigilant self-protection: a conscious awareness of God that causes a person to avoid what harms him spiritually. Al-Rāzī explains that the question is rhetorical — God knows they will not be reverent, but the question is posed to highlight the irrationality of their position: they have every reason to be reverent and no valid reason not to be.

**Taqwā as Mindfulness of Punishment**

Some commentators understand the question as meaning "Will they not be mindful of the punishment?" — that is, will they not take precautions against the consequences of their actions? In this reading, *taqwā* is not only a spiritual state but a practical one: it is the rational recognition that wrongdoing carries consequences, and the wise person protects himself by avoiding wrongdoing. This interpretation makes the question even more pointed: Pharaoh's people are being asked not to do something spiritually sublime but something practically rational — to protect themselves. Their refusal is not only disobedience; it is stupidity.

**The Parallel to the Quraysh**

Just as Pharaoh's people were asked "Will they not be reverent?" through Moses, the Quraysh were being asked the same question through the Prophet ﷺ. The parallel is exact: an entrenched elite, comfortable in its power and privilege, being called to account by a messenger whose message threatens their position. The question is always the same: will you not protect yourselves? Will you not see where your path leads? The fact that the question appears at the opening of the first and longest prophetic narrative sets the tone for the entire sequence: every story that follows will illustrate what happens when the answer is "no."

**The Rhetorical Force of a Divine Question**

When God asks a question, it is not because He lacks information. It is because the question itself is the lesson. "Will they not be reverent?" is a question designed to make the hearer pause and reflect — to recognize that the answer should obviously be "yes" and to feel the weight of the fact that it so often is not. It is the same rhetorical strategy a parent uses when asking a child, "Don't you know better?" — not to elicit information but to awaken conscience.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:12

> **He said, "My Lord, truly I fear that they will deny me."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Moses' Honest Fear**

Moses' response to God's commission is not the confident acceptance one might expect from a prophet. Instead, he expresses fear: "My Lord, truly I fear that they will deny me." This honesty is not a deficiency in Moses' faith; it is a mark of his humanity and his realism. He knows Pharaoh's people. He grew up among them. He knows their arrogance, their power, and their cruelty. His fear is not cowardice; it is clear-eyed assessment. The Qur'an does not present its prophets as superheroes devoid of human emotion; it presents them as human beings who feel fear and yet proceed in obedience to God.

**The Fear of Denial Specifically**

Moses does not say "I fear that they will harm me" — though that fear is legitimate and will be expressed in the next verse. His first stated fear is that they will "deny me" (*yukadhdhibūn*) — declare him a liar. The root *k-dh-b* means to reject as false, to call a lie. This is the core injury of the prophetic experience: not physical harm but the rejection of one's truthfulness. Every prophet's deepest wound is the accusation that he is lying about God. Al-Rāzī observes that it is precisely this fear — the fear of being called a liar — that causes the breast to straiten and the tongue to falter, as expressed in the next verse. The pain of being declared false is more than physical pain; it strikes at the prophet's identity and mission.

**The Psychology of Fear in Divine Service**

This verse has important implications for understanding the psychology of divine service. God does not rebuke Moses for his fear; He listens to it and responds to it. This tells us that fear is not incompatible with faith — it is a natural human response to dangerous situations, and God does not expect His servants to be fearless. What He expects is that they bring their fears to Him, voice them honestly, and then proceed with His support. The Prophet Muhammad ﷺ himself experienced fear — before Badr, before Ḥunayn, in the cave during the Hijrah — and the Qur'an records these moments without diminishing his stature. Courage, in the Qur'anic sense, is not the absence of fear but the decision to act despite it.

**The Pattern of Prophetic Dialogue with God**

Moses' exchange with God at the burning bush is one of the most extended prophetic dialogues in the Qur'an. It appears across multiple sūrahs (7:103–108; 20:9–48; 27:7–12; 28:29–35; and here in 26:10–33). Each account highlights different aspects of the exchange. This sūrah focuses on Moses' fears and God's reassuring responses — a pattern that teaches the reader how to approach God with honest concerns rather than with performed confidence. Prayer, in this model, is not the suppression of human feeling but its honest expression before the One who already knows it.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:13

> **And my breast will be straitened, and my tongue will not be unfettered; so send unto Aaron.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Straitened Breast**

Moses continues to articulate his fears with striking physical imagery: "my breast will be straitened" (*wa-yanṭabiqu ṣadrī*). The verb *inṭabaqa* means to close tight, to constrict, to compress. The image is of a chest so tight with anxiety that breathing becomes difficult. This is the somatic language of anxiety — the physical tightness that every person who has faced a daunting task recognizes. Moses is not describing a spiritual deficiency; he is describing a human body responding to a terrifying prospect. Al-Rāzī notes that it is precisely the fear of being denied and called a liar that causes this constriction — the psychological pressure of facing an audience that has already decided to reject you.

**The Unfettered Tongue**

Moses adds: "my tongue will not be unfettered" (*wa-lā yanṣillu lisānī*). The verb *anṣalla* means to become loose, free, fluent — to be untied from a knot. Moses is describing a speech difficulty: his tongue does not flow freely. The Qur'an does not specify the nature of this difficulty, but the reference in Sūrah Ṭā Hā (20:27) where Moses prays "Untie a knot from my tongue" (*fukḥud ʿuqdatan min lisānī*) suggests a physical impediment. Many Muslim commentators, drawing on both the Qur'anic text and the Israelite tradition recorded in Exodus, understood this as a form of stuttering or stammering. However, in light of the Qur'an's consistent use of bodily faculties in spiritual rather than purely biological senses (the heart as the seat of understanding in 2:7; the eyes as the organ of spiritual perception in 22:46), it is also plausible that "tongue" here refers to an inward or psychological obstacle — a difficulty not in articulation but in the ability to express himself effectively before a hostile audience.

**The Request for Aaron**

Moses' solution is practical: "so send unto Aaron." Aaron is his brother, his ally, the one who can speak on his behalf when his own tongue falters. In Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ (28:34), Moses explains the reason more fully: "And my brother Aaron — he is more eloquent in speech than I; so send him with me as a helper." The request is not for someone to do the work for Moses; it is for a partner, a support, a complementary presence. God will grant this request — as recorded in 20:29–35 — and Aaron will accompany Moses throughout his mission.

**Aaron's Prophetic Appointment**

Al-Rāzī and al-Qurṭubī note that Moses' request "send unto Aaron" can be understood in two ways: either Moses is asking God to send Gabriel to inform Aaron of his mission, or Moses is asking God to bestow prophethood upon Aaron. Either way, Aaron's role is divinely sanctioned — he is not merely a family favor but a prophetic partner. The Qur'an confirms this elsewhere:

> *"And We gave him of Our mercy, and We granted him his brother Aaron as a helper."* (Qur'an 25:35)

The principle that God's servants can be supported by companions and helpers is one that applies throughout the prophetic tradition and beyond it. No one is expected to carry the burden alone.

**The Prophet's Humanity and God's Accommodation**

The most profound lesson of this verse is the way God accommodates human limitation. The Creator of the universe does not say to Moses, "I chose you; deal with it." He listens to Moses' concerns, addresses each one, and provides the support needed. This is a model for how God deals with all who serve Him: He does not demand superhuman performance; He accepts human effort augmented by divine support. The Prophet Muhammad ﷺ had Abu Bakr, ʿUmar, ʿAlī, and the other Companions; Moses had Aaron. Every caller to truth needs partners, and asking for help is not weakness — it is wisdom.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:14

> **And they have a crime against me; thus I fear that they will slay me."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Unresolved Past**

Moses now names a specific, concrete fear: he killed an Egyptian. The word for "crime" here is *dhanb*, which means a fault, a sin, or a crime — something for which one can be held accountable. The incident is described in Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ (28:15–21): Moses entered the city unnoticed and found an Israelite fighting with an Egyptian. He struck the Egyptian to help the Israelite, but the blow killed the Egyptian. It was not premeditated murder — it was a spontaneous act of intervention that had fatal consequences — but it was still a death on Moses' hands, and it was the reason he fled Egypt for Midian.

**The Fear of Retaliation**

Moses' fear is rational: under Egyptian law, killing an Egyptian — even accidentally — was a capital offense, especially for a non-Egyptian. Pharaoh's regime was built on the subjugation of the Israelites; an Israelite who killed an Egyptian would be considered doubly guilty. Moses fled to Midian to escape this consequence (28:21), and now God is asking him to return to the very place where he is wanted for a capital crime. The fear of being slain (*an yaqtulūn*) is not paranoia; it is a realistic assessment of the danger.

**Theological Sensitivity: Can a Prophet Commit a Crime?**

The question of whether Moses committed a "crime" (*dhanb*) or a "sin" touches on the sensitive theological issue of prophetic inerrancy (*ʿiṣmah*). Most Muslim theologians held that prophets are protected from sin — but there is disagreement about whether this protection applies before the prophetic mission begins or only after. Some theologians, as al-Rāzī notes, admitted the possibility that a prophet could commit a small error before being invested with his mission but maintained that thereafter he is protected. Others argued that Moses' act was not a sin in the technical sense but a mistake (*zallah*) — a well-intentioned act that had unintended consequences. Al-Rāzī explains that Moses struck the Egyptian not with the intention of killing but to "discipline" him, and the fatal outcome was unintended. In either case, the Qur'an presents Moses' account with unflinching honesty — he does not deny what happened; he names it and moves on.

**The Vulnerability of the Prophet**

This verse strips away any remaining illusion that the prophetic mission is comfortable or risk-free. Moses is being sent back to the scene of his greatest vulnerability, to confront the most powerful man on earth, with a capital crime on his record and a speech impediment in his tongue. By any rational calculation, this mission is impossible. And that is precisely the point: the prophetic mission is never about human capability; it is about divine commission backed by divine support. God does not choose the most qualified; He qualifies the chosen. Moses' fear is legitimate, but so is God's promise in the next verse: "We shall be with you, listening."

**A Lesson for Every Daʿī**

For anyone called to a difficult task in God's service, Moses' honesty is instructive. He does not pretend to be fearless; he does not inflate his courage; he names his fears one by one — the fear of rejection, the fear of speechlessness, the fear of violence. And God meets each fear with a specific provision. This is the pattern of divine support: it does not eliminate the fear; it accompanies the fear. The messenger goes forward not because he is no longer afraid but because he knows that God is with him in the fear.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:15

> **He said, "Nay! Go forth, then, with Our signs. We shall be with you, listening."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**God's Reassurance: "Nay!"**

God's response to Moses' cascade of fears is a single, powerful word: "Nay!" (*kallā*) — an emphatic negation that means "not so," "never," "absolutely not." It is the same word God uses in Sūrah al-ʿAlaq (96:6) to rebuke the attitude of self-sufficiency: "Nay! Truly man is rebellious." Here it functions as a divine override: all of Moses' fears are acknowledged, but none of them will prevail. God is not dismissing Moses' concerns as trivial; He is declaring that they will not prevent the mission from succeeding. The divine response is not a detailed argument against each fear but a simple, authoritative statement: the fears are real, but I am greater than them.

**"Go Forth with Our Signs"**

God commands Moses to go — the same command as before, but now augmented: "with Our signs" (*bi-āyātinā*). The "signs" are the miracles that Moses will deploy: the staff that becomes a serpent, the hand that turns white, and other proofs of his divine commission. The signs are not Moses' own power; they are God's signs entrusted to Moses. This distinction is crucial: the prophet is a vessel, not a source. The miracles authenticate the message, not the messenger's personal abilities. Al-Ṭabarī explains that "Our signs" refers specifically to the nine signs mentioned in Sūrah al-Isrāʾ (17:101): the hand, the staff, the flood, the locusts, the lice, the frogs, the blood, the splitting of the sea, and the drowning of Pharaoh.

**"We Shall Be With You, Listening"**

The divine promise reaches its climax: "We shall be with you, listening" (*innamā maʿakum samiʿūn*). The word *samiʿūn* — "listening" or "hearing" — carries multiple layers of meaning. It means that God will hear everything Moses' opponents say — their arguments, their threats, their mockery — and nothing will escape His awareness. It means that God will listen to Moses' prayers and respond to them. And it means that God's presence will be with Moses and Aaron throughout the confrontation — not distant, not disengaged, but actively listening and responding. Al-Rāzī explains that "We shall be with you" means God will be on their side, supporting them against Pharaoh, and "listening" means that every word spoken in the confrontation will be heard and weighed by God.

**The Structure of Divine Support**

God's response to Moses follows a clear structure: first, the negation of fear ("Nay!"); second, the provision of tools ("with Our signs"); third, the promise of presence ("We shall be with you"); fourth, the guarantee of attention ("listening"). This structure is a model for understanding divine support in general: God does not merely tell His servants to be brave; He equips them, accompanies them, and attends to every detail of their situation. The prophet goes into the confrontation not alone but with signs in his hand, God's presence at his side, and God's ears tuned to every word.

**The Application to Modern Life**

For anyone facing a daunting task — a difficult conversation, a professional challenge, a call to stand for truth in a hostile environment — this verse offers a pattern: acknowledge your fears honestly (as Moses did), then proceed with the tools God has given you, knowing that He is present and attentive. The outcome is not yours to guarantee; your responsibility is to go. As the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ said when facing the overwhelming odds at Ḥunayn:

> *"I am the Prophet, without lie; I am the son of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib."*

Confidence not in numbers but in mission, not in self but in God — this is the prophetic model.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:16

> **So go to Pharaoh and say, 'Verily we are sent by the Lord of the worlds,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Specific Target**

After the general command to "go forth," God now specifies the target: Pharaoh. The name *Firʿawn* — Pharaoh — is not a personal name but a title, like "Caesar" or "Khosrow." It is the title of the rulers of ancient Egypt, and in the Qur'an it always refers to the specific ruler who confronted Moses. The Qur'an's repeated use of the title rather than a personal name is significant: it universalizes the figure. Every tyrant who claims divine status for himself is a "Pharaoh"; every people who submit to such a claim are "the people of Pharaoh." The confrontation is not merely historical; it is archetypal.

**The Message: "Sent by the Lord of the Worlds"**

God instructs Moses in the precise words to deliver: "Verily we are sent by the Lord of the worlds" (*innā ilayka rasūlū rabbi l-ʿālamīn*). The plural "we" includes both Moses and Aaron — the mission is shared. The title "Lord of the worlds" (*rabb al-ʿālamīn*) is deliberately chosen. Pharaoh claimed to be the lord of Egypt at least, and according to some reports, he claimed universal lordship (43:51). By instructing Moses to announce the "Lord of the worlds," God is directly challenging Pharaoh's claim: you may be lord of a patch of land, but I am Lord of everything that exists — seen and unseen, past and present, human and jinn, earth and heavens.

**The Implication of "Sent"**

The word "sent" (*mursalūn* — from *arsalnā*, "We sent") carries the weight of divine authority. Moses and Aaron are not going on their own initiative; they are emissaries, ambassadors, messengers dispatched by a higher authority. In diplomatic terms, an attack on an ambassador is an attack on the sending power. When Pharaoh rejects Moses, he is not merely rejecting a man; he is rejecting the One who sent him. This is why the encounter between Moses and Pharaoh is so consequential: it is not a personal dispute but a collision between a human claim to absolute power and a divine claim to absolute lordship.

**The Brevity of the Message**

Notice the economy of the message: Moses is not instructed to deliver a long theological argument or a detailed list of demands. The core message is compressed into a single statement: "We are sent by the Lord of the worlds." Everything else — the demand to release the Israelites, the challenge to Pharaoh's claims, the offer of guidance — flows from this single declaration. The Qur'an's own message is similarly concentrated: the core of the prophetic call is always the affirmation of God's lordship and the messenger's status as His emissary. Complexity comes later, in the detailed implications; the foundation is always simple and clear.

**The Courage to Stand Before Power**

For the reader, this verse is a lesson in the courage required to stand before worldly power with a divine message. Moses is not approaching Pharaoh as an equal; he is approaching him as the representative of the true sovereign. The power dynamic is inverted: the slave who grew up in Pharaoh's household, the fugitive who fled Egypt with a price on his head, now stands before the most powerful man on earth and declares, "I come from your Lord." This inversion — the seemingly powerless carrying the ultimately powerful message — is the pattern of every prophetic mission, and it is the pattern that the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ would reenact in Makkah and Madinah.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:17

> **that you may send with us the Children of Israel.'"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Specific Demand**

The mission's objective is now stated clearly: "that you may send with us the Children of Israel" (*an arsil maʿanā banī isrāʾīl*). The verb *arsil* — "send" or "release" — means to let go, to set free, to allow to depart. Moses is not asking for concessions, reforms, or better treatment; he is asking for complete liberation. The Children of Israel are to leave Egypt entirely and go to the land of Palestine. This is not a negotiation; it is a demand for freedom — the freedom of an enslaved people to leave the land of their oppression.

**The Historical Context of Enslavement**

The background to this demand is the centuries-long enslavement of the Israelites in Egypt. According to the Biblical narrative (which the Qur'an presupposes and supplements), the Israelites came to Egypt during the time of Joseph, who rose to a position of high authority. After his death, a new Pharaoh arose who "did not know Joseph" (Exodus 1:8) and enslaved the Israelites out of fear of their growing numbers. The enslavement included forced labor, oppression, and — most horrifying — the killing of newborn male children (2:49; 14:6; 28:4). Moses' demand is thus not merely political but moral: an end to slavery, an end to infanticide, an end to systemic oppression.

**"With Us" — The Communal Nature of Liberation**

The phrase "with us" (*maʿanā*) is significant: Moses does not say "let them go" as an abstract policy; he says "send them with us." He identifies himself with his people. He is not a detached advocate; he is part of the community he represents. His fate is their fate; their liberation is his liberation. This communal identification is a hallmark of the prophetic mission: the prophet does not save himself and leave others behind; he goes with his people, shares their suffering, and leads them to freedom.

**The Echo in the Prophet Muhammad's Mission**

The demand to "send with us" the oppressed echoes the Prophet Muhammad's ﷺ own situation. The early Muslims in Makkah were a persecuted minority — tortured, boycotted, some killed. The Prophet's demand was not for personal power but for the liberation of his community from persecution. When he migrated to Madinah, it was not a flight from danger but the establishment of a community where the oppressed could live in freedom and dignity. The parallel with Moses' exodus is deliberate and instructive: liberation from oppression is a divine mandate, and the messenger's role is to lead his people from bondage to freedom.

**The Justice of the Demand**

Al-Rāzī observes that Moses' demand is just on every level. Pharaoh had no legitimate claim over the Israelites — they were a free people who had been unjustly enslaved. The demand for their release is not an act of aggression but an act of justice. This principle — that the demand for liberation from oppression is always just — is one of the Qur'an's most important social teachings. It applies not only to the historical case of the Israelites in Egypt but to every situation in which a people are enslaved, colonized, or systematically oppressed. The messenger's role is to speak truth to power on behalf of the powerless.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:18

> **He said, "Did we not raise you among us as a child, and did you not stay among us for years of your life?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Pharaoh's First Response: Appeal to the Past**

Pharaoh's response to Moses' demand is revealing. Rather than addressing the substance of the message — the claim that Moses is sent by the Lord of the worlds — Pharaoh reaches for a personal argument: "Did we not raise you among us as a child?" The implication is: "You owe us. You were nothing — an infant cast into the river — and we took you in, raised you in our palace, and gave you a life of privilege. And now you turn against us?" This is the classic move of the oppressor who cannot refute the argument: redirect to personal indebtedness.

**The Irony of "Raised Among Us"**

The irony is thick. Pharaoh claims credit for raising Moses, but the reason Moses was in the river in the first place was Pharaoh's own decree to kill all Israelite male infants (2:49; 28:4). Moses was not a random orphan; he was a baby whose life was threatened by Pharaoh's own policy. Pharaoh's "benevolence" in raising Moses was, in reality, the unwitting fulfillment of God's plan: God returned Moses to Pharaoh's household so that he could be raised and prepared for his mission (20:39: *"that you might be raised upon My eye"*). Pharaoh thought he was doing Moses a favor; in reality, he was being used by God to prepare the very instrument of his downfall.

**"Years of Your Life" — The Weight of Time**

The phrase "for years of your life" (*libth min ʿumrika*) — or in some readings "periods of your life" — emphasizes the length of Moses' stay in Pharaoh's household. According to most accounts, Moses lived in Pharaoh's palace for approximately forty years before fleeing to Midian. Pharaoh's point is: you spent a significant portion of your life benefiting from our generosity. How can you now oppose us? This argument rests on the assumption that material benefits create an unbreakable obligation — that receiving someone into your home gives you permanent authority over them.

**The Fallacy of Obligation**

Al-Rāzī identifies the logical flaw in Pharaoh's argument: material benefits do not create an obligation to accept oppression. Even if Pharaoh raised Moses with kindness, that does not give Pharaoh the right to enslave the Israelites or to claim that Moses should remain silent about the injustice. Gratitude for personal kindness does not require complicity in systemic evil. The principle is universal: no benefit received — however great — can obligate a person to remain silent in the face of injustice. This is why the Qur'an consistently separates the personal from the political: you can be grateful to an individual while still opposing the system he represents.

**The Avoidance of Substance**

Notice what Pharaoh does not do: he does not engage with Moses' claim that he is sent by the Lord of the worlds. He does not argue about theology or challenge the miracles. He redirects to a personal, emotional argument — one designed to shame Moses rather than refute his message. This is a common tactic of entrenched power: when you cannot defeat the argument, attack the person. When you cannot address the principle, redirect to the biography. The Prophet Muhammad ﷺ experienced the same tactic: the Quraysh, unable to refute the Qur'an's message, attacked the Prophet personally — calling him a poet, a sorcerer, a madman. Pharaoh's response is the archetype of every attempt to silence truth by shaming the truth-bearer.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:19

> **And you did your deed which you did, and you are one of the ingrates."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Accusation of Ingratitude**

Pharaoh presses his advantage: "And you did your deed which you did, and you are one of the ingrates." The word "ingrates" translates *kāfirūn*, from the root *k-f-r*, which means to cover, to conceal, to deny. A *kāfir* is one who covers over blessings and acts as though they were never received. In this context, Pharaoh is accusing Moses of ingratitude — of repaying Pharaoh's kindness (raising him in the palace) with betrayal (killing an Egyptian and now demanding the release of the Israelites).

**The Ambiguity of "Kāfir"**

The word *kāfir* ordinarily means "disbeliever" in the Qur'an, but here it carries the additional meaning of "ingrate" — one who denies a specific blessing received from another person. Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī explain that some commentators dismiss the idea that Pharaoh would use *kāfir* in its theological sense of "disbeliever," since Pharaoh's concern was not with theology but with his own authority and pride. The more natural reading is that Pharaoh is calling Moses an ingrate in the worldly sense: you received our favors and now repay us with opposition. Some commentators note that the phrase could also be read adverbially: "while you were an ingrate" — meaning that the act of killing was done in a state of heedlessness or ingratitude.

**The "Deed Which You Did"**

The vague phrase "your deed which you did" (*faʿalta faʿlataka*) is deliberately non-specific. Pharaoh does not say "you killed" — perhaps because the killing was not widely known, or perhaps because the vagueness itself is a rhetorical device: the deed is so obviously wrong that it needs no specification. The deed, of course, is Moses' killing of the Egyptian (28:15–21), which was the reason he fled Egypt. Pharaoh is reminding Moses of his vulnerability: you are a fugitive with blood on your hands. How dare you come back and make demands?

**The Strategy of Redefining the Narrative**

Pharaoh's strategy is clear: reframe Moses from "messenger of the Lord of the worlds" to "ungrateful fugitive murderer." If he can shift the narrative from theology to biography, from God's claim to Moses' past, he can neutralize the prophetic challenge without having to address it. This is a timeless tactic: when truth confronts power, power's first move is to discredit the truth-bearer rather than engage with the truth. The Qur'an records this tactic in full view, teaching every future generation of truth-bearers to expect the same response and to remain unmoved by it.

**Moses' Answer in the Next Verse**

The beauty of the Qur'anic narrative structure is that Moses' response follows immediately — and it is devastating. He does not deny the deed; he acknowledges it. But he recontextualizes it: he was "among those who are astray" — ignorant, not willfully rebellious. And then he turns the tables on Pharaoh: "And that blessing you recount to me is that you have enslaved the Children of Israel." The exchange is a masterclass in how to respond to personal attacks while maintaining the moral high ground.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:20

> **He said, "I did it then, when I was one of those who are astray."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Moses' Admission: "I Was Among the Astray"**

Moses' response to Pharaoh's accusation is remarkably candid: he does not deny the deed. He says, "I did it then, when I was one of those who are astray" (*fa-ʿaltuhū idhan wa-anā min al-ḍāllīn*). The word *ḍāllīn* — "those who are astray" — is understood by most commentators to mean "those who are ignorant" or "those who act without proper knowledge." Moses is not claiming that he was a sinner in the theological sense; he is saying that the killing was an error — an act done without the intention to kill, driven by a spontaneous desire to help the oppressed Israelite, but resulting in unintended death.

**Al-Rāzī on the Nature of the Act**

Al-Rāzī provides a careful analysis: Moses struck the Egyptian not with the intention of killing him but in order to "discipline" him — to stop the fight and protect the Israelite. The fatal outcome was unintended. In Islamic legal terms, this would be classified as manslaughter rather than murder — an act that caused death without the intention to kill. The distinction matters because it affects both the moral assessment and the legal consequences. Moses is not admitting to murder; he is admitting to a tragic mistake.

**The Theological Question: Can a Prophet Err?**

This verse touches on one of the most debated questions in Islamic theology: can a prophet commit an error before his mission begins? The majority position, held by most Ashʿarī and Māturīdī theologians, is that prophets are protected from sin (*maʿṣūm*) at all times — before, during, and after their mission. A minority position, reported by al-Rāzī, admits the possibility that a prophet could commit a small error before being invested with prophethood but maintains that after prophethood, the attribute of *ʿiṣmah* (inerrancy) is permanent. Under this minority view, Moses' killing of the Egyptian occurred before his prophetic mission began at the burning bush, and thus the possibility of error existed.

**The Honesty of the Prophetic Character**

Regardless of the theological resolution, the literary and moral point is clear: the Qur'an presents its prophets with unflinching honesty. Moses does not spin the story; he does not claim innocence where there was at least responsibility. He acknowledges that the act was done in a state of error — and this acknowledgment itself is a mark of his integrity. The prophets are not presented as flawless angels; they are presented as human beings who, despite their high station, are capable of mistakes — but who are also capable of acknowledging those mistakes with honesty and turning to God in repentance.

**The Distinction Between Error and Rebellion**

It is essential to distinguish between Moses' admission and the kind of willful rebellion that characterizes Pharaoh. Moses' "astray" is the astray of error — a good intention with a bad outcome. Pharaoh's "astray" is the astray of arrogance — a clear truth with a willful rejection. The two are not comparable, and the Qur'an makes sure the reader sees the difference. Moses' error led to repentance and growth (as shown in 28:16–17, where he says, "My Lord, I have wronged myself, so forgive me"); Pharaoh's arrogance led to destruction.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:21

> **So I fled from you when I was afraid of you. Then my Lord granted me judgment and placed me among the messengers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Flight to Midian**

Moses continues his account: he fled Egypt out of fear. The word "fled" (*farartu*) is the same root used in the Qur'an for fleeing from battle, from danger, from death. Moses is not ashamed of his fear; he states it plainly. The flight was to Midian (28:22), where he would spend approximately ten years — working for Shuʿayb, marrying his daughter, and living the life of a shepherd far from the palaces of Egypt. This period of exile was not wasted time; it was preparation. The prince who grew up in Pharaoh's household needed to become the humble shepherd who could lead an stubborn people through the wilderness. God's preparation of His messengers always includes a period of obscurity and humility.

**"My Lord Granted Me Judgment"**

The word "judgment" (*ḥukm*) carries multiple meanings. It can mean prophethood — the authority to judge and guide. It can mean wisdom and understanding — the intellectual capacity to discern truth from falsehood. It can mean knowledge and intelligence — the practical ability to make decisions. Al-Ṭabarī records that some commentators understood it as knowledge and understanding rather than full prophethood (since the phrase "placed me among the messengers" follows as a separate clause). Under any interpretation, the point is the same: God took the frightened fugitive and transformed him into a man of judgment and authority — not through his own effort alone but through divine bestowal.

**"Placed Me Among the Messengers"**

The final clause completes the transformation: from fugitive to messenger. The progression is remarkable: Moses describes himself in three stages — (1) the erring youth who killed in error, (2) the frightened fugitive who fled for his life, and (3) the prophet who now stands before Pharaoh with God's authority behind him. The transformation is entirely God's doing: "My Lord granted me... placed me." Moses takes no credit for his elevation; he attributes it entirely to God's grace.

**The Pattern of Divine Preparation**

The pattern of Moses' life — privilege, error, flight, obscurity, preparation, return, confrontation — is a template that recurs throughout prophetic history and beyond it. God does not usually elevate people directly from comfort to authority; He sends them through a wilderness first. The Prophet Muhammad ﷺ spent years in relative obscurity before his mission became public. Many of the great figures of Islamic history — scholars, reformers, leaders — went through periods of exile, failure, or obscurity before they were ready for their tasks. The lesson is not to despise the wilderness periods but to trust that God is preparing through them.

**The Contrast with Pharaoh**

The contrast between Moses and Pharaoh at this point in the narrative is stark. Pharaoh has been sitting on his throne, accumulating power, surrounding himself with flatterers. Moses has been through the fire — literally and figuratively — and has come out the other side transformed by God. The man who returns to Egypt is not the same man who fled it. He has been humbled, strengthened, and commissioned. Pharaoh, by contrast, has not changed at all — he is still the same arrogant tyrant he always was. The Qur'an's implicit message is clear: the one who has been through difficulty with God is stronger than the one who has been comfortable without Him.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:22

> **And that blessing you recount to me is that you have enslaved the Children of Israel."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Moses Turns the Tables**

After acknowledging his past error, Moses now addresses Pharaoh's claim of "blessing" with devastating rhetorical force. The word "blessing" translates *mann* — from a root that carries a rich range of meanings including giving, bestowing, counting, and also reminding others of one's charity in a way that implies superiority. Pharaoh has been recounting his "benefaction" to Moses — raising him in the palace, giving him status and comfort. Moses' response reframes the entire narrative: "That blessing you recount to me — is it because you enslaved the Children of Israel?"

**The Logic of the Reframing**

The rhetorical structure is brilliant. Moses is saying: "You claim you blessed me by raising me. But what was my situation before I was raised?
