The person who appears most opposed may be the one closest to recognition. Your task is to present the truth clearly; the transformation is God's to accomplish.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:49

> **He said, "Do you believe in Him before I give you leave? He is indeed your chief, who has taught you sorcery. You will surely know! I shall surely cut off your hands and your feet from opposite sides, and I shall surely crucify you all!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Pharaoh's Rage**

Pharaoh's response is a torrent of rage and threat. The structure of his outburst reveals his psychological state: first, the accusation ("before I give you leave?"); second, the rationalization ("he is your chief who taught you sorcery"); third, the threat of extreme violence (amputation and crucifixion). The speed and intensity of the reaction show how deeply the sorcerers' conversion has shaken him. These were his instruments — and they have turned against him in the most public way possible.

**"Before I Give You Leave?"**

Pharaoh's first objection is not theological but political: how dare you make a decision of this magnitude without my permission? This is the logic of totalitarianism: the ruler claims authority not only over actions but over beliefs. You may not even change your faith without the state's approval. Al-Rāzī notes the absurdity: faith is an internal matter between the soul and God — what does it mean to need Pharaoh's "permission" to believe? But this is the nature of tyranny: it seeks to control not only behavior but thought, not only action but conviction.

**"He Is Indeed Your Chief"**

Pharaoh's rationalization is transparently desperate. He cannot accept that his sorcerers recognized genuine divine power, so he invents a conspiracy: Moses must be their chief, their teacher, their mastermind. The whole thing was staged. This is the same accusation the Quraysh leveled against the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ: that he was taught by a human teacher (16:103; 25:4). When the evidence cannot be explained within the existing framework, the tyrant invents a conspiracy to preserve the framework.

**The Threat of Cruel Punishment**

Pharaoh threatens the most extreme punishment in the ancient penal repertoire: cutting off hands and feet "from opposite sides" — meaning the right hand and the left foot (or vice versa) — which renders the victim unable to walk or fight, a living helplessness. Then crucifixion — public display of the broken body as a warning to others. This is not justice; it is terror. Pharaoh is not punishing a crime; he is making an example. The punishment is designed not only to inflict pain but to create a public spectacle of suffering that will deter anyone else from following the sorcerers' example.

**The Irony of the Tyrant's Fear**

The irony is palpable: Pharaoh, the most powerful man on earth, is afraid of a group of sorcerers who have just laid down their tools and prostrated in prayer. They are not armed; they are not organizing a rebellion; they have simply declared faith. And yet Pharaoh responds with the maximum violence at his disposal. This reveals the truth about tyranny: it is not strong; it is terrified. Every act of independent thought, every declaration of allegiance to a higher authority, threatens the tyrant's illusion of total control. The more total the claim, the more fragile the foundation.

**The Sorcerers' Response — Courage Under Torture**

The sorcerers' response to these threats, which follows in the next verses, is one of the most courageous in the Qur'an. They accept the punishment with equanimity, knowing that the suffering is temporary and the reward with God is eternal. Their courage is the proof of their faith's authenticity: they believed, and they were willing to pay any price for that belief. This is the pattern that will recur throughout Islamic history — from the early Muslims tortured in Makkah to the scholars imprisoned by tyrannical caliphs to the believers who face persecution in every age.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:50

> **They said, "It is no harm. Truly unto our Lord do we turn."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Defiance of Faith**

The sorcerers' response to Pharaoh's threats is breathtaking in its calm: "It is no harm" (*lā ḍayr*). They look at the threat of amputation, crucifixion, and public torture, and they say: it does not matter. This is not bravado; it is the settled conviction of people who have seen something greater than anything Pharaoh can inflict. They have seen the staff of Moses devour their craft; they have seen the Lord of the worlds manifest His power; they have prostrated before the One who created everything. Compared to that, what are hands and feet? What is a body on a cross?

**"Truly Unto Our Lord Do We Turn"**

The sorcerers' statement "unto our Lord do we turn" (*innā ilā rabbinā munqalibūn) is a declaration of ultimate orientation. Their return is not to Pharaoh but to God. Whatever Pharaoh does to their bodies in this world, their ultimate destination is with their Lord — and with their Lord is the account of everything. This is the faith that makes tyranny irrelevant: when your orientation is toward God, the threats of a mortal tyrant lose their power. He can hurt your body, but he cannot touch your return.

**The Theology of Suffering**

The sorcerers' calm in the face of extreme suffering reflects a profound theology: this world's pain is temporary; the Hereafter's reward is permanent. What Pharaoh does to them in the next few hours will be over quickly; what God does for them in the Hereafter will last forever. This calculus — which the Qur'an repeats in dozens of verses — is the key to understanding how believers can face torture and death with equanimity. It is not masochism; it is perspective. From the vantage point of eternity, the tyrant's punishment is a moment.

**The Pattern of the First Converts from the Oppressor's Camp**

The sorcerers are the first Egyptians to believe — the first to defect from Pharaoh's camp. Their story establishes a pattern that recurs throughout prophetic history: the first converts from the oppressor's own community are often the ones who pay the highest price. Because they know the system from the inside, their defection is the most threatening. Because they were once part of the establishment, their punishment is the most severe. The sorcerers are the prototype of every person who leaves the camp of power to join the camp of truth — and pays for it with their body, their status, and their comfort.

**The Lesson About the True Meaning of "Harm"**

The sorcerers redefine "harm." Pharaoh thinks he is threatening them with harm; they say there is no harm. Why? Because the real harm is not what Pharaoh can do to their bodies; the real harm is what they would do to their souls by returning to disbelief. Physical suffering is temporary; spiritual corruption is eternal. The sorcerers have learned in a single moment what the wise learn over lifetimes: the body's pain is real but limited; the soul's destruction is the only harm worth fearing.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:51

> **Truly we hope that our Lord will forgive us our sins for our having been the first of the believers."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Hope for Forgiveness**

The sorcerers express their hope (*ṭamaʿ*) that God will forgive their sins — specifically, the sins of their past career as sorcerers in Pharaoh's service. The word *ṭamaʿ* — "hope" — carries a sense of eager expectation, not mere wishful thinking. They have seen enough of God's mercy in their immediate experience — the way He manifested His sign to them, the way He opened their hearts to faith — to be confident that He will forgive their past.

**"The First of the Believers"**

The sorcerers identify themselves as "the first of the believers" (*awwal al-muʾminīn). This phrase has multiple layers of meaning. Al-Ṭabarī records that it can mean: (1) the first of the Egyptian people to believe; (2) the first to believe in this particular sign; (3) the first of the sorcerers to believe. Under any interpretation, the sorcerers are claiming the honor of precedence — the special status of being first. In Islamic theology, the "first" (*sābiqūn*) have a special rank:

> *"And the first forerunners in the faith — those are the ones brought near to God."* (Qur'an 56:10–11)

**The Forgiveness That Comes Through Sacrifice**

The sorcerers' hope for forgiveness is linked directly to their status as the first believers — and their willingness to pay the price of that belief. In Islamic theology, sincere repentance erases past sins, and the trials endured in the path of God serve as expiation. The sorcerers are about to endure amputation and crucifixion for their faith — and in the Qur'anic calculus, this suffering, borne with patience and faith, wipes away everything that came before.

> *"And those who have believed and whose descendants followed them in faith — We will join with them their descendants, and We will not deprive them of anything of their deeds."* (Qur'an 52:21)

**The Principle: Past Sins Do Not Disqualify**

The sorcerers' story teaches one of the most important principles in Islamic theology: past sins do not disqualify a person from faith or from God's forgiveness. The sorcerers were professional practitioners of sorcery — a grave sin in Islamic law. They had spent their careers using their skills to serve tyranny and oppose truth. And yet, the moment they recognized the truth and submitted to it, their past was forgiven. This is the mercy of God made concrete: no sin is too great for forgiveness when the repentance is genuine and the subsequent commitment is real.

**The Modern Application**

This verse speaks directly to every person who feels that their past disqualifies them from faith or from God's acceptance. The sorcerers were sorcerers — experts in the art of opposing divine truth — and yet they became the first believers of their nation. If God can forgive sorcerers who turn to Him, He can forgive anyone who turns to Him. The only disqualification is the refusal to turn.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:52

> **Then We revealed unto Moses, "Set forth with My servants by night; you shall indeed be pursued."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Divine Command to Depart**

After the confrontation with the sorcerers and the events that followed (including the plagues described in other sūrahs), God commands Moses to lead the Children of Israel out of Egypt. The command "Set forth with My servants by night" (*an asri bi-ʿibādī*) contains two crucial details: first, the Israelites are described as God's "servants" (*ʿibād*) — they belong to God, not to Pharaoh; their departure is not an escape but a liberation by their true Master. Second, the departure is to be "by night" — under cover of darkness, in secrecy, to gain a head start before Pharaoh can mobilize his forces.

**The Divine Foreknowledge: "You Shall Indeed Be Pursued"**

God informs Moses that they will be pursued. This is not a warning to discourage but a preparation to strengthen. God has already planned the outcome — the parting of the sea, the drowning of Pharaoh — but Moses needs to know that the pursuit is part of the plan, not a deviation from it. The Israelites may feel terrified when they hear the chariots behind them; Moses must know in advance that this terror is within God's design and will lead to their deliverance.

**The Pattern of Divine Liberation**

The pattern of liberation through difficulty is a recurring theme: God does not always remove the difficulty; He often leads His servants through it. The Israelites are not magically transported from Egypt to freedom; they must walk, they must face the terror of pursuit, they must stand at the edge of the sea with enemies behind them. Faith does not eliminate the journey; it gives the journey its destination.

**The Exodus in Broader Scripture**

The Exodus is one of the most significant events in the history of religion, commemorated in Judaism as Passover, referenced in Christianity, and recounted multiple times in the Qur'an (7:136–137; 10:90; 20:77–79; 44:23–25). In each account, the emphasis is on God's power to liberate the oppressed from the most powerful tyrant, using means that appear inadequate (a staff, a group of slaves) but are backed by divine will.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:53

> **Then Pharaoh sent marshalers to the cities [saying],**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Pharaoh's Mobilization**

When Pharaoh learns of the Israelites' departure, his response is immediate mobilization. He sends "marshalers to the cities" — the same kind of agents he sent earlier to gather sorcerers (v. 37), now deployed to assemble a military pursuit force. The parallelism is deliberate: the same infrastructure Pharaoh used to oppose God's signs is now deployed to pursue God's servants. The tyranny's machinery is efficient — but it is being used for destruction.

**The Persistence of the Tyrant**

Even after the sorcerers' conversion, even after the signs, Pharaoh does not relent. His arrogance is so deep that no evidence can penetrate it. This is the Qur'anic portrait of the hardened heart: not a heart that has evaluated the evidence and found it lacking, but a heart that has decided never to accept, regardless of the evidence. Al-Rāzī observes that Pharaoh's persistence in denial, despite everything he has witnessed, demonstrates that the problem is not intellectual but volitional — he chooses not to believe.

**The Military Pursuit as the Final Confrontation**

The military pursuit represents the final escalation. Pharaoh has tried argument (failed), sorcery (defeated), and now he resorts to military force. This is the tyrant's last card: violence. When words fail, when tricks fail, the tyrant reaches for the sword. But the sword, too, will fail — because the God who parted the heavens and the earth can certainly deal with a collection of chariots.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:54

> **"These indeed are a small band,"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Pharaoh's Dismissal**

Pharaoh describes the Israelites as "a small band" (*shirdhimah*) — a word that denotes a remnant, a small group, a minority. The dismissal is deliberate: he is belittling his opponents to maintain the morale of his forces. If the Israelites are just a "small band," then the mighty Egyptian army should have no difficulty pursuing and recapturing them.

**The Arithmetic of Tyranny**

Pharaoh's calculation is purely quantitative: the Israelites are few, his army is many. In the mathematics of worldly power, numbers determine outcomes. But the Qur'an repeatedly subverts this mathematics:

> *"How many a small company has overcome a large company by God's leave."* (Qur'an 2:249)

The Battle of Badr, where 313 Muslims defeated approximately 1,000 Quraysh, is the Prophetic echo of this same principle. God's help does not depend on numerical superiority; it depends on faith, truth, and divine will.

**The Deception of Appearances**

Pharaoh sees a "small band"; God sees His servants. Pharaoh counts heads; God weighs hearts. The tyranny's assessment is always based on visible quantities — numbers, weapons, wealth, status. It cannot see the invisible realities — faith, divine support, the power of truth. This blindness to the unseen is the tyrant's fundamental weakness.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:55

> **and indeed they have enraged us,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Tyrant's Anger**

Pharaoh declares that the Israelites "have enraged us" (*wa-innahum lanā la-ghāʾiẓūn). The word *ghāʾiẓūn* — "those who enrage" — reveals the emotional core of the pursuit. This is not merely strategic; it is personal. Pharaoh is angry — angry that his slaves have escaped, angry that his authority has been challenged, angry that his gods (himself included) have been proven powerless. The pursuit is driven not only by political calculation but by rage.

**The Anger of the Powerless**

Al-Rāzī observes the irony: Pharaoh, the most powerful man on earth, is described as "enraged" by a group of fleeing slaves. True power is not disturbed by the actions of the weak; only false power is rattled. Pharaoh's rage reveals the fragility of his position: if a few thousand escaped slaves can shake him this deeply, how powerful is he really?

**The Declaration of Enmity**

By saying "they have enraged us," Pharaoh is declaring open enmity. This is no longer a matter of policy; it is a matter of personal vendetta. The commentators note that the rage encompasses both the immediate offense (the escape) and the accumulated grievances (the plagues, the sorcerers' conversion, the challenge to Egyptian religion).

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:56

> **and we are a host at the ready!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Confidence in Military Power**

Pharaoh completes his rationale: "we are a host at the ready" (*wa-innā la-jamīʿun ḥādhirūn). The word *ḥādhirūn* — "at the ready" — means equipped, prepared, and assembled. Pharaoh is confident in his military superiority: his host is gathered, armed, and ready to pursue. What can a "small band" of escaped slaves do against the full might of the Egyptian army?

**The Hubris That Precedes Destruction**

This verse captures the essence of Pharaonic hubris: the conviction that worldly resources are sufficient for every challenge. Pharaoh does not consider the possibility that God is protecting the Israelites; he considers only the balance of military forces. This is the fundamental error of tyrants throughout history: they count their armies but do not count God.

**The Irony of "At the Ready"**

The Egyptian army is "at the ready" — but they are riding into a trap of God's making. Their preparation is real; their readiness is genuine; their confidence is understandable. But they are preparing for the wrong battle. They think they are pursuing escaped slaves; they are actually riding into the sea that will drown them. The readiness that should have been directed toward repentance is instead directed toward destruction.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:57

> **So We expelled them from gardens and springs,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**God Makes the Egyptians Leave**

The verse describes God causing the Egyptians to leave their homes and possessions: "We expelled them from gardens and springs." The word "expelled" (*akhrajnā*) is the same word used for the Israelites' departure — but with a crucial difference. The Israelites left with God's help toward freedom; the Egyptians were expelled by God's will toward destruction. The same verb describes two very different journeys: one toward life, one toward death.

**The Loss of Paradise**

The "gardens and springs" evoke the abundance and luxury of Egyptian civilization — the Nile's fertility, the agricultural wealth, the comfort of settled life. The Egyptians are being expelled from their earthly paradise because they used its blessings to oppress others. This is a Qur'anic principle: blessings that are not accompanied by gratitude and justice become the very things that are taken away.

**The Reversal of Fortunes**

The expulsion represents a complete reversal: the oppressors become the expelled; the masters of Egypt become refugees from their own land. The lesson is universal: no position of worldly power is permanent. Those who sit on thrones today may flee from their palaces tomorrow — if the One who placed them decides to remove them.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:58

> **and treasures and a noble station.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Full Scope of What Was Lost**

The verse continues listing what the Egyptians left behind: "treasures and a noble station" (*kunūz wa-maqāmin karīm). The "treasures" are the accumulated wealth of the greatest civilization of the ancient world — gold, silver, precious objects, the material resources built up over centuries. The "noble station" refers to the high rank, honor, and prestige that Egypt held among the nations. All of it is left behind as the Egyptians ride to their destruction.

**The Futility of Worldly Accumulation**

Al-Rāzī reflects on the vanity of accumulation: Pharaoh spent his life amassing treasures and building monuments, believing that wealth and status would make him immortal. When God's command came, none of it saved him. The treasures were left behind; the station was abandoned; the man who called himself "your supreme lord" died in the mud at the bottom of the sea. This is the Qur'an's perennial lesson: worldly accumulation without spiritual orientation is not investment; it is merely the accumulation of things you will leave behind.

**The Meaning of "Noble Station"**

The word *karīm* — "noble" — applied to Pharaoh's station is deeply ironic. How "noble" was a station built on the enslavement and murder of an entire people? The Qur'an uses Pharaoh's own self-description against him: he claimed nobility, but his nobility was a facade built on oppression. True nobility, as the Qur'an teaches elsewhere (49:13), comes from taqwā — from reverence for God and righteous conduct — not from accumulated wealth or inherited status.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:59

> **So it was, and We made the Children of Israel to inherit them.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Transfer of Possession**

God declares: "We made the Children of Israel to inherit them" (*wa-awrathnāhā banī isrāʾīl). The word "inherit" (*awrathnā*) is significant: the Israelites did not conquer Egypt through military force; they inherited its wealth through divine decree. The oppressor's possessions became the property of the oppressed — not through theft but through a just transfer ordained by God. This is the Qur'anic model of how God deals with tyrants: He does not merely destroy them; He transfers their blessings to those they oppressed.

**The Justice of Inheritance**

Inheritance is a lawful transfer — the rightful passing of possessions from one party to another. By describing the Israelites' acquisition as "inheritance," the Qur'an legitimizes it: this was not plunder but justice. The wealth that Egypt had accumulated through the forced labor of the Israelites was, in a real sense, always the Israelites' property — they were merely reclaiming what had been taken from them.

**The Pattern for All Oppressed Peoples**

The principle extends beyond the historical event: every oppressed people who are patient and faithful will, in time, inherit what their oppressors have accumulated. This is not a promise of immediate revenge but of ultimate justice — which may be fulfilled in this world, in the Hereafter, or in both.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:60

> **Then they pursued them at sunrise.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Timing of the Pursuit**

The Egyptians pursued at sunrise — the time when light reveals the landscape and the fleeing Israelites can be spotted. The timing suggests urgency: Pharaoh wanted to catch them as quickly as possible, before they could put distance between themselves and the Egyptian border. The sunrise also adds dramatic effect: the Israelites, camped by the sea, see the Egyptian army approaching at dawn — the light that should bring hope instead reveals the approaching threat.

**The Dramatic Tension**

The narrative builds tension deliberately: the Israelites are between the sea ahead and the army behind. There is no escape route. The sunrise, instead of bringing deliverance, brings the revelation of their apparently hopeless situation. This is the moment of maximum trial — when faith is tested most severely.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:61

> **And when the two hosts saw one another, the companions of Moses said, "We are overtaken!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Crisis of Faith**

The moment of maximum tension arrives: the two hosts face each other — the sea ahead, the army behind. The Israelites cry out: "We are overtaken!" (*innā lamudrakūn). The word *mudrakūn* — "overtaken" — means caught, trapped, with no escape. This is the moment when the human calculus says: there is no way out. The sea cannot be crossed; the army cannot be fought. The situation is, by every rational measure, hopeless.

**The Test of the Believers**

The Israelites' cry reveals the state of their faith: it is not yet mature enough to trust God when the situation appears hopeless. They see the physical reality — sea and army — and conclude that they are trapped. They cannot yet see what Moses sees: that God is already working on their behalf. This is the perpetual gap between the believer's knowledge and the believer's faith: they know God is powerful, but in the moment of crisis, they panic.

**The Universal Human Experience**

Every person who has faced an apparently impossible situation recognizes this cry: "We are overtaken!" — "There is no way out." The feeling of being trapped between two impossibilities is universal. The Qur'an records it here to teach the response: when you feel overtaken, remember that God's options are not limited by your geography.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:62

> **He said, "Nay! Truly my Lord is with me; He will guide me."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Moses' Unshakeable Certainty**

Moses' response to the crisis is one of the most powerful statements of faith in the Qur'an: "Nay! Truly my Lord is with me; He will guide me" (*kallā inna maʿiya rabbī sayahdīn). The word *kallā* — "Nay!" or "Not so!" — is an emphatic rejection of the premise that they are trapped. Moses does not accept the Israelites' conclusion. Where they see a dead end, he sees God. Where they see impossibility, he sees guidance.

**"My Lord Is With Me"**

The phrase "my Lord is with me" (*maʿiya rabbī) echoes God's own promise to Moses in verse 15: "We shall be with you, listening." Moses has internalized the divine promise; he is not panicking because he knows — not hopes, but knows — that God is present. This is the difference between faith as information and faith as lived reality: Moses is not reciting a doctrine; he is standing on a promise that has been confirmed in his experience.

**"He Will Guide Me"**

The word "guide" (*yahdīn) carries the sense of showing the way, opening a path, providing direction. Moses does not know exactly how God will deliver them — he does not yet know about the parting of the sea — but he knows with certainty that God will show the way. This is the essence of *tawakkul* (trust in God): not knowing the outcome but being certain that God will guide.

**The Prophet as the Steadfast One**

In every crisis, the prophet is the one who remains steady while others panic. This is not because the prophet is emotionally immune to fear — Moses himself expressed fear earlier in the narrative — but because the prophet's relationship with God has reached a level where divine presence outweighs worldly circumstances. The pattern repeats with the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ in the cave during the Hijrah, when Abu Bakr panicked and the Prophet said:

> *"What do you think of two, when God is their third?"* (Qur'an 9:40)

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:63

> **Then We revealed unto Moses, "Strike the sea with thy staff!" and it parted, and each part was as a great mountain.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Miracle of the Parting Sea**

The climactic miracle arrives: God commands Moses to strike the sea with his staff, and the sea parts. The description is vivid and awe-inspiring: "each part was as a great mountain" (*fa-kullu firqin ka-l-ṭawdi l-ʿaẓīm). The image is of walls of water standing on either side like enormous mountains — a visual that captures both the power required to hold the water back and the awe of those who witnessed it.

**The Nature of the Miracle**

Al-Rāzī observes that the miracle lay not merely in the water receding — since water can disappear naturally through evaporation or currents — but in the water standing upright like mountains. Water's natural tendency is to flow downward and spread out; making it stand vertically requires a complete suspension of its natural properties. This is not a natural phenomenon amplified; it is nature itself overridden. The commentators emphasize that the water was not merely pushed back by wind (as some naturalistic explanations suggest) but was made to stand by divine command — each portion frozen in place like a mountain of crystal.

**The Staff as the Instrument**

Once again, the staff is the instrument of the miracle. The same ordinary piece of wood that Moses used for walking and shepherding is now the means by which the sea is parted. The lesson is consistent throughout the Moses narrative: God uses the most ordinary things to demonstrate the most extraordinary power — so that no one can claim the miracle was inherently present in the instrument. The staff is wood; the power is God's.

**Cross-References and Corroboration**

The parting of the sea is mentioned in multiple Qur'anic passages (2:50; 7:136; 10:90; 20:77; 44:24) and is also recorded in the Biblical account (Exodus 14:21–22). The consistency of the account across traditions testifies to its historical significance. In Islamic tradition, the body of water is generally identified as the Red Sea, though the Qur'an does not specify.

**The Symbolism of the Sea**

The sea in the Qur'an is often a symbol of overwhelming power — something that human beings cannot control. By parting the sea, God demonstrates His absolute mastery over the most powerful force in the natural world. The same sea that threatens to drown becomes the pathway of deliverance. This symbolism applies to every overwhelming challenge in life: what appears as an obstacle can, with God's help, become a path.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:64

> **And thither We brought the others nigh.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Egyptians Drawn Toward Their Fate**

God says: "And thither We brought the others nigh" (*wa-azlafnā thamma al-ākharīn). The word "others" (*al-ākharīn) refers to Pharaoh and his army. The verb *azlafnā* — "We brought nigh" — is deliberately ambiguous: it means both that God caused the Egyptians to approach the place where the sea would close over them and that God brought them closer to their death. Al-Rāzī notes the double meaning: they were literally approaching the scene of their destruction, and they were also being brought nearer to their appointed end.

**The Divine Agency in the Outcome**

The verse emphasizes that it was God who "brought them nigh." The Egyptian army was not merely following their own military logic; they were being guided — by divine plan — to the exact place and time of their destruction. Their pursuit, which they believed was an act of their own will, was actually the fulfillment of God's decree. This is the Qur'anic concept of *istidrāj* — God giving the wrongdoers rope until they are so far advanced in their wrongdoing that the punishment, when it comes, is total and inescapable.

**The Warning to Every Pursuer of the Oppressed**

The verse is a standing warning to every power that pursues the oppressed: the pursuit itself may be the mechanism of your destruction. Pharaoh thought he was chasing slaves; he was actually riding into the trap God had prepared for him. Every tyrant who pursues the believers should remember: the road you are riding may lead not to your victory but to your drowning.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:65

> **And We delivered Moses and those with him all together.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Complete Deliverance**

God declares: "We delivered Moses and those with him all together" (*wa-njaynā mūsā wa-man maʿahu ajmaʿīn). The word *ajmaʿīn* — "all together" — emphasizes that not a single believer was left behind. The deliverance was complete and total: every man, woman, and child among the Israelites who followed Moses made it across the sea safely. This completeness is a mark of divine mercy: God's deliverance does not leave stragglers.

**The Collective Salvation**

The salvation was collective — Moses AND those with him. This is the Qur'anic model of salvation: it is not merely individual but communal. The prophet and his followers are saved together. This does not negate individual responsibility — each person is accountable for their own faith — but it does describe the experience of deliverance as a shared event. The believers cross together; they arrive together; they inherit together.

**The Faithfulness of God's Promise**

The deliverance fulfills God's promise to Moses in verse 15: "We shall be with you, listening." God was with Moses; God delivered him and his companions; God kept every word of His promise. The faithfulness of God to His promises is one of the most consistent themes in the Qur'an — and one of the most important foundations for the believer's trust.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:66

> **Then We drowned the others.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Drowning of Pharaoh and His Army**

The verse states with devastating brevity: "Then We drowned the others" (*thumma aghraqnā al-ākharīn). The word *thumma* — "then" — indicates a sequence: first the deliverance, then the destruction. There is no ambiguity, no qualification, no partial escape: the Egyptian army was drowned. All of them. Pharaoh, his generals, his soldiers, his chariots — all swallowed by the sea that had momentarily parted for God's servants.

**The Brevity as Rhetorical Force**

The verse's power lies partly in its brevity. After the extended description of Pharaoh's power, his arrogance, his mobilization, and his pursuit — the account of his destruction is a single word: drowned. The empire that took centuries to build, the army that was "a host at the ready," the civilization that dominated the ancient world — all of it ends in a single act of drowning. This brevity is the Qur'an's way of saying: what took pages to build took a moment to destroy. The disproportion between the buildup and the end is the lesson.

**The Justice of the Drowning**

The drowning is not arbitrary; it is the precise consequence of Pharaoh's crimes. He had drowned the Israelite children by throwing them into the Nile (2:49; 28:4); now he himself is drowned. He had used water as a weapon of oppression; now water becomes the instrument of his destruction. The Qur'anic principle of proportionate justice — where the punishment fits the crime — is perfectly embodied here.

**Pharaoh's Final Moment**

Other Qur'anic passages describe Pharaoh's final moment: as the water closed over him, he declared faith (10:90: "I believe that there is no god but Him in whom the Children of Israel believe"). But it was too late: "Was it now? When you had been disobedient before and one of the workers of corruption?" (10:91). Faith at the moment of death, when the punishment is visible, does not avail. The drowning of Pharaoh is thus both a historical event and a perennial warning: do not wait until the water is closing over you to acknowledge the truth.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:67

> **Truly in that is a sign, but most of them are not believers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain Returns**

The first occurrence of the refrain that structures the sūrah returns here after the completion of the Moses narrative. "Truly in that is a sign" — the entire story, from Moses' commission to Pharaoh's drowning, constitutes a *āyah* — a sign to be reflected upon, not merely a story to be consumed. The sign is not in any single event but in the pattern: the commissioning, the confrontation, the signs, the rejection, the deliverance, the destruction. Together, these elements constitute a comprehensive lesson about how God deals with truth and falsehood.

**"But Most of Them Are Not Believers"**

The refrain's second clause reminds the reader that despite the clarity of the sign, most people will not believe. This is not a statement of despair but of realism: the sign is sufficient for the sincere, but sincerity is rare. The majority will always find reasons to dismiss what they do not want to accept.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:68

> **And truly thy Lord is the Mighty, the Merciful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Closing Pair**

The refrain concludes with the pairing of Might and Mercy: "your Lord is the Mighty, the Merciful." After the story of Pharaoh's destruction, "the Mighty" reminds the reader that God's power is absolute — no tyrant can escape it. After the story of the Israelites' deliverance, "the Merciful" reminds the reader that God's compassion is inexhaustible — no believer is abandoned. The pairing holds both truths together: the God who destroyed Pharaoh is the same God who saved Moses. Might and Mercy are not contradictions; they are complements.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:69

> **And recite unto them the story of Abraham,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Transition to Abraham**

The sūrah now transitions from the Moses narrative to the story of Abraham — the second prophetic account and the second longest in the sūrah. The command "recite unto them" (*wa-tlu ʿalayhim) instructs the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ to tell the story of Abraham to the Quraysh — the people who, like Pharaoh's people, are rejecting a messenger from God. The Abraham story is particularly relevant to the Quraysh because they claimed descent from Abraham through his son Ishmael and considered themselves the custodians of the Kaʿbah, which Abraham and Ishmael had built (2:127).

**The Ḥanīf — The Pure Monotheist**

Ibn ʿAjībah notes that the Abrahamic story foregrounds the ideal of the *ḥanīf* — the follower of a pure, primordial monotheism untainted by sectarian tribalism. Abraham renounces all lords but God and names as enemies those things that occupy the heart in place of God. The Quraysh, who had abandoned Abraham's monotheism for the idolatry of their ancestors, needed this story more than most.

**The Relevance to the Prophet's Mission**

The story of Abraham is also deeply personal for the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ. Abraham was his spiritual ancestor, the one who established the monotheistic tradition that Muhammad was restoring. By reciting Abraham's story, the Prophet is not only warning his people but reconnecting them to their own spiritual roots — the roots that idolatry had severed.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:70

> **when he said to his father and his people, "What are you worshipping?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fundamental Question**

Abraham opens his confrontation with his people with the most fundamental question a human being can ask: "What are you worshipping?" (*mā taʿbudūn). This question is not merely about religious practice; it is about the orientation of the heart, the ultimate loyalty of the soul, the thing that occupies the center of a person's devotion. Everything else in life flows from the answer to this question: what you worship determines what you value, what you pursue, what you fear, and what you love.

**The Question to His Father First**

Abraham addresses his father first — a detail that reveals both the intimacy and the difficulty of the confrontation. His father (identified in other sūrahs as Āzar, 6:74) was not merely a parent but the family's spiritual authority. For Abraham to challenge his father's worship was to challenge the foundation of the family's entire identity. This is the cost of monotheism in a polytheistic family: the truth-bearer must confront not only the culture but the home.

**The Question to "His People"**

The question extends beyond his father to "his people" — the broader community. Abraham is not keeping his monotheism private; he is publicly challenging the entire community's worship. This is the prophetic pattern: the truth is not a private possession but a public proclamation. Abraham asks the question openly because he expects an open answer — and because the answer, once articulated, will reveal the absurdity of what they are doing.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:71

> **They said, "We worship idols, and we remain ever devoted to them."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Honest Confession**

The people's response is remarkably candid: "We worship idols, and we remain ever devoted to them" (*naʿbudu awthānan wa-naẓallu lahā ʿākifīn). They do not claim that the idols have power, that they created them, or that they benefit from them. They simply state the fact of their worship and their devotion to it. The word *ʿākifīn* — "ever devoted" — carries the sense of persistent, unwavering attachment: they are not occasional worshippers but permanent devotees.

**The Devotion Without Reason**

What makes the confession so damning is its emptiness. They worship idols — and they remain devoted to them — but they offer no reason why. There is no argument, no justification, no claim of benefit. It is pure habit, pure tradition, pure attachment. This is what the Qur'an identifies as the root of most idolatry: not reasoned belief but unexamined attachment. People worship what they have always worshipped because they have never asked why.

**The Modern Form of Unexamined Devotion**

The modern equivalent is the person who is "devoted" to a worldview, a lifestyle, or an ideology without ever having examined its foundations. The devotion is real; the reasoning behind it is absent. Abraham's question — "What are you worshipping?" — is as relevant today as it was then: it invites every person to examine what occupies the center of their devotion and to ask whether that devotion is based on reason or on habit.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:72

> **He said, "Do they hear you when you call?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Abraham's Logical Challenge**

Abraham's response is a devastating logical challenge: "Do they hear you when you call?" (*hal yasmaʿūnakum idh tadʿūn). The question is designed to expose the fundamental absurdity of idol worship: the idols cannot hear. They have no consciousness, no perception, no awareness. The devotees cry out to them, beg them, praise them — and the idols are as silent as the stone they are made from. If worship is a relationship — a communication between the worshipper and the worshipped — then worship that is not heard is not worship at all; it is monologue directed at a stone.

**The Criterion of Responsiveness**

Abraham's implicit argument is that a true object of worship must be capable of responding to the worshipper. God hears; God responds; God answers prayers. The idols do none of these things. By what standard, then, can they be considered gods? The criterion of responsiveness — can the worshipped hear, see, and respond? — is a simple but devastating test. It eliminates not only the physical idols of Abraham's time but every modern substitute that cannot hear, see, or respond.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:73

> **Or do they benefit or harm you?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Criterion of Benefit and Harm**

Abraham continues his challenge: "Or do they benefit or harm you?" The question targets the practical justification for worship: people worship because they believe the object of worship can help them or protect them from harm. But the idols cannot do either. They cannot heal the sick, feed the hungry, protect from danger, or punish the wrongdoer. They are, by definition, powerless. If the purpose of worship is to seek benefit or avoid harm, then worship directed at something that can neither benefit nor harm is not merely irrational — it is futile.

**The Logic of Worship**

Abraham's argument rests on a clear logic: worship, by its nature, implies that the object of worship has certain attributes — the ability to hear, to benefit, to harm, to respond. If the supposed object of worship lacks all of these attributes, it is not a legitimate object of worship by any rational standard. The argument is not merely theological; it is practical: what good does it do you to worship something that cannot do anything for you?

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:74

> **They said, "Nay, but we found our fathers doing so."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Appeal to Ancestral Practice**

Confronted with Abraham's devastating logic, the people have no answer — except to fall back on the oldest justification in human history: "We found our fathers doing so" (*lā bal wajadnā ābāʾanā kadhālika yafʿalūn). The word "Nay" (*lā bal) means "not exactly" — they do not claim that the idols hear or benefit; they simply repeat that their fathers did it. This is the argument from tradition pure and simple: we do it because they did it. The validity of the practice is not established by evidence but by precedent.

**The Rejection of Taqlīd**

Al-Rāzī identifies this as the Qur'an's most fundamental argument against *taqlīd* — blind conformity with authority. The people are not following their fathers' practice because they have examined it and found it rational; they are following it because their fathers did it. Abraham's response (in the following verses) is a systematic demolition of this argument: error, whether recent or entrenched, is still error. The authority of precedent does not transform falsehood into truth.

**The Universality of the Argument**

This verse describes the most common form of religious adherence across human history: doing what was done before, without examination. The Qur'an's challenge to this pattern is radical: it demands that every person take responsibility for their own beliefs, rather than inheriting them unexamined from their ancestors. This is not a rejection of tradition per se — the Qur'an affirms the value of prophetic tradition — but a rejection of tradition that substitutes for independent reasoning.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:75

> **He said, "Have you considered what you worship,"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Invitation to Reflection**

Abraham shifts from logical argument to a direct invitation: "Have you considered what you worship?" (*a-raʾaytum mā kuntum taʿbudūn). The word *raʾaytum* — "you considered" or "you have seen" — is an invitation to look carefully, to examine, to evaluate. Abraham is saying: stop and really look at what you are worshipping. Don't just accept it as given; examine it. What is it? What can it do? What can it not do? Is it worthy of your devotion?

**The Method of Critical Examination**

This is the method of critical thinking applied to religious belief: do not accept; examine. Do not follow; evaluate. Abraham is not asking his people to abandon their faith; he is asking them to subject it to honest scrutiny — confident that honest scrutiny will reveal its emptiness. This is the Qur'anic model of engagement with belief: the truth can withstand examination; falsehood cannot.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:76

> **you and your ancestors?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Including the Ancestors in the Examination**

Abraham extends the examination to include not only the current generation but their ancestors as well: "you and your ancestors?" The implication is: not only is your worship irrational; the people you inherited it from were equally irrational. The tradition has no foundation in any generation — it is a chain of error passed from one unexamining generation to the next. The length of a tradition does not add to its validity; it only increases the number of people who failed to think critically about it.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:77

> **For they are all enemies unto me, save the Lord of the worlds,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Abraham's Declaration of Enmity Toward Idols**

Abraham makes his position absolutely clear: the idols are his enemies, and only the Lord of the worlds is his object of worship. Some commentators understand "they" as referring to the idols themselves: these lifeless objects, far from being objects of devotion, are actually enemies to Abraham — things that occupy space in the heart that should belong to God alone. Others understand "they" as referring to the worshippers: those who worship idols are, by their worship, placing themselves in opposition to Abraham and his monotheism.

**At the Spiritual Level**

At a spiritual level, the verse teaches that one must make the things of this world one's enemy — in the sense of refusing to give them the devotion that belongs to God alone — before one can truly love God. This is the principle of detachment: the heart has limited capacity, and every attachment to a false object of worship is an attachment that displaces the true object. Al-Rāzī explains that the idols are "enemies" because they take up the space in the heart that should be filled with God's love.

**The Exclusive Worship of the Lord of the Worlds**

Abraham's declaration is exclusive: the Lord of the worlds alone is worthy of worship; everything else is either an enemy or irrelevant. This is the essence of tawḥīd: the absolute, undivided worship of the one God, with no partners, no intermediaries, and no substitutes.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:78

> **Who created me, and guides me,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The First Attribute: Creation**

Abraham begins his description of the God he worships with the most fundamental attribute: "Who created me" (*alladhī khalaqan). God is the Creator — the originator of Abraham's existence, the One who brought him from non-existence into being. This is the foundation of all worship: you worship the One who made you, because without Him you would not exist to worship anything. The logic is simple and irrefutable: the created owes its existence to the Creator, and the appropriate response to being created is gratitude — expressed through worship.

**The Second Attribute: Guidance**

Immediately after creation, Abraham mentions guidance: "and guides me" (*wa-huwa yahdīn). God not only created Abraham; He continues to guide him — showing him the right path, correcting his errors, leading him toward truth. Guidance (*hidāyah*) is the ongoing dimension of God's relationship with His creatures: He does not create them and abandon them; He creates them and shows them the way. This is why worship is rational: the God who created you is also the God who tells you how to live. To worship anything other than the Creator-Guide is to reject both your origin and your direction.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:79

> **and Who feeds me and gives me drink,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Provider of Sustenance**

Abraham continues: "and Who feeds me and gives me drink" (*wa-huwa yuṭʿimunī wa-yasqīn). God is the source of all sustenance — the food that nourishes the body, the water that sustains life. Every meal, every drink, every moment of physical sustenance is a gift from God. The logic of worship continues: the One who created you, guides you, and sustains you at every moment — you owe Him your devotion. The idols cannot create you, guide you, or feed you; therefore, the idols are not worthy of worship.

**The Spiritual Dimension of Feeding**

Al-Rāzī and the Sufi commentators note that these verses have a spiritual dimension alongside the physical: God feeds the soul with faith, guides it with revelation, and quenches its thirst with knowledge and love. The physical sustenance is a sign of the spiritual sustenance — both come from the same Source.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:80

> **and Who, when I am ill, heals me,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Divine Physician**

Abraham attributes healing to God alone: "Who, when I am ill, heals me" (*wa-idhā mariḍtu fa-huwa yashfīn). Notice the subtle shift in grammar: creation, guidance, and sustenance are described with active verbs — God creates, guides, feeds, gives drink. But healing is described conditionally: "when I am ill, He heals me." The attribution of illness is to Abraham himself (I am ill); the attribution of healing is to God (He heals me). This is a profound theological point: illness may come through natural causes or through human experience, but healing comes from God.

**The Saying of Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq**

A saying attributed to Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq captures the spiritual dimension: "When I am ill through sin, He heals me with repentance." In this reading, the "illness" is spiritual — the sickness of the soul that comes from wrongdoing — and the "healing" is the repentance that God enables and accepts. The physical healing is a sign of the spiritual healing; both come from the same Source.

**The Logic of Worship Through Healing**

The logic continues: the idols cannot heal you. When you are sick, you do not turn to them; you turn to medicine, to treatment, to the One who made the remedies and the bodies they heal. If healing comes from God, then God alone is worthy of worship.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:81

> **and Who causes me to die, then gives me life,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Lord of Death and Life**

Abraham attributes to God the power over death and life: "Who causes me to die, then gives me life" (*wa-alladhī yumītunī thumma yuḥyīn). This is the ultimate power — the power to end life and to restore it. The sequence "causes me to die, then gives me life" refers to the resurrection: death in this world, then life in the Hereafter. The idols cannot kill you, and they certainly cannot bring you back to life. The power over death and resurrection belongs to God alone — and this power is the ultimate grounds for worship.

**Al-Thaʿlabī's Summary**

Al-Thaʿlabī summarizes the spiritual dimensions: God brings death through justice and life through grace; death through disobedience and life through obedience; death through separation and life through meeting Him; death through ignorance and life through knowledge. Each pair describes a different dimension of the divine power over the living and the dead.

**The Resurrection as the Foundation of Accountability**

The resurrection — "then gives me life" — is the foundation of all accountability. Without resurrection, death would be the end, and there would be no justice for the oppressed, no reckoning for the oppressor, and no fulfillment of the promises God has made to His servants. The resurrection is not only a theological doctrine; it is the guarantee that justice will ultimately prevail.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:82

> **and Who I hope will forgive me my sins on the Day of Judgment.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Hope for Forgiveness**

Abraham concludes his description of God with the attribute that matters most to every human being: forgiveness. "Who I hope will forgive me my sins on the Day of Judgment" (*wa-alladhī aṭmaʿu an yaghfira lī khaṭīʾatī yawma l-dīn). The word *aṭmaʿu* — "I hope" — expresses a confident expectation, not a vague wish. Abraham hopes for forgiveness because he knows God's nature: God is forgiving, merciful, and just.

**The Theological Question**

The commentators debate the meaning of Abraham's attribution of "sins" (*khaṭīʾāt*) to himself. If prophets are sinless, what sins is Abraham hoping to have forgiven? Some say the "hope" is Abraham's way of teaching his descendants to hope for forgiveness — he is modeling the proper posture of a servant before God. Others say the "sins" refer to minor lapses or mistakes — not major sins but the small imperfections that even the most righteous commit. Mujāhid says it could refer to the incident where Abraham "lied" about the idol (21:63) or looked at the stars (37:89) — actions that, while not sinful in the ordinary sense, represented a slight departure from the highest standard of truthfulness.

**The Day of Judgment**

Abraham's hope is specifically anchored in "the Day of Judgment" (*yawm al-dīn) — the day when every soul is held accountable for its deeds. This is the day that Abraham's entire description of God points toward: the God who created, guided, fed, healed, killed, and will resurrect — is the same God who will judge. The forgiveness Abraham hopes for will be granted on that day, when the accounting is complete and the mercy of God is fully manifested.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:83

> **My Lord! Grant me judgment, and bind me to the righteous!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prayer for Wisdom**

Abraham now turns from description to supplication: "My Lord! Grant me judgment" (*rabbi hab lī ḥukm). The word *ḥukm* — "judgment" — can mean wisdom, understanding, the ability to discern truth from falsehood, or prophethood. Abraham is asking God to give him the capacity to judge rightly — to see things as they are and to act accordingly. This prayer is relevant to every person who faces decisions: the ability to judge correctly is not a human achievement but a divine gift.

**"Bind Me to the Righteous"**

Abraham continues: "and bind me to the righteous" (*wa-alḥiqnī bi-l-ṣāliḥīn). The word *alḥiqnī* — "bind me" or "join me" — means to attach, to connect, to place in the company of. Abraham is asking to be in the company of the righteous — in this world and in the Hereafter. The "righteous" (*ṣāliḥīn) can refer to the prophets, the inhabitants of Paradise, or those who are content in all states and conditions. The prayer is for companionship with the best of people — because companionship shapes character.

**The Connection Between Wisdom and Company**

Abraham's two requests — wisdom and righteous company — are connected. Wisdom is easier to attain and maintain when one is surrounded by the wise and the righteous. The prayer acknowledges a fundamental principle of spiritual growth: you become like those you spend time with. Abraham asks not only for personal wisdom but for the environment that will sustain it.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:84

> **And make for me faithful renown among later generations.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prayer for a Good Legacy**

Abraham asks God to "make for me faithful renown among later generations" (*w-ijʿal lī lisāna ṣidqin fi l-ākhirīn). The word *lisān* — "tongue" — signifies "word," "remembrance," or "renown." The word *ṣidq* — "faithful" or "true" — means that the renown will be genuine, not exaggerated or false. Abraham is praying that he will be remembered well by those who come after him — that his name will be associated with truth and righteousness.

**The Fulfillment of the Prayer**

The prayer has been fulfilled beyond anything Abraham could have imagined. Thousands of years after his death, Abraham is remembered — by name, in prayer, with love and reverence — by billions of people across three major world religions. Every Muslim who sits in prayer recites the Abrahamite blessing; every Jew remembers him as their father; every Christian honors him as the friend of God. The "faithful tongue" Abraham prayed for has spoken his name across every continent and every century.

**The Lesson About Legacy**

The prayer teaches an important principle: a good legacy is not achieved by seeking fame but by living in truth. Abraham did not ask to be famous; he asked to be remembered faithfully — for truth. The difference is crucial: fame seeks attention; faithfulness seeks substance. The person who lives for God and truth will be remembered faithfully; the person who lives for self and display will be remembered — if at all — with contempt.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:85

> **And place me among the heirs of the Garden of bliss.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prayer for Paradise**

Abraham asks to be "among the heirs of the Garden of bliss" (*w-ajʿalnī min warathati jannati l-naʿīm). The word "heirs" (*warathah) implies that Paradise is an inheritance — something that belongs to the faithful by right, not merely as a gift. The word *naʿīm* — "bliss" — evokes tranquility, ease, comfort, and blessing — the complete satisfaction of every desire. Abraham is asking to be among those who inherit this state of perpetual bliss after death.

**The Meaning of "Bliss"**

The garden of bliss is mentioned elsewhere in the Qur'an (5:65; 10:9; 22:56; 31:8; 37:43; 56:12; 68:34). It is a garden of perfect peace — where every want is fulfilled and every sorrow is absent. Abraham's prayer for this bliss is the prayer of every believer: not merely to avoid punishment but to attain the positive state of perpetual contentment in God's presence.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:86

> **And forgive my father—he is one of those who are astray.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prayer for Abraham's Father**

Even after declaring the idols to be his enemies and separating himself from his father's idolatry, Abraham still prays for his father's forgiveness: "And forgive my father—he is one of those who are astray" (*w-ighfir li-abī innahu kāna mina l-ḍāllīn). This prayer reflects Abraham's compassion for his father, even though his father has chosen a path that leads away from God. The father is described as "one of those who are astray" — not willfully rebellious but genuinely lost.

**The Quranic Clarification**

The Qur'an later clarifies that Abraham ceased praying for his father when it became clear that his father was an enemy of God (9:114): "Abraham's plea for forgiveness of his father was only due to a promise he had made to him. But when it became clear to him that he was an enemy of God, he repudiated him." This does not diminish Abraham's compassion; it shows that even the most compassionate prayer has its limits when the person being prayed for has definitively chosen opposition to God.

**The Lesson About Praying for Others**

Abraham's prayer teaches that it is appropriate and commendable to pray for the guidance and forgiveness of those we love — even those who have not yet accepted the truth. The prayer is an act of compassion and an expression of hope. But the prayer does not override the other person's free will: if they choose to remain in error despite clear guidance, the prayer cannot force them to accept.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:87

> **And disgrace me not on the Day they are resurrected,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fear of Disgrace**

Abraham's concern shifts to the Day of Resurrection: "And disgrace me not on the Day they are resurrected" (*wa-lā tukhzinī yawma yubʿathūn). The word *tukhzinī* — "disgrace me" — refers to the shame that will be felt on the Day of Judgment when a person's deeds are exposed. For Abraham, the fear of disgrace is not about major sins — it is about any shortcoming that might cause shame before God and before all of humanity on that Day.

**The Spiritual Dimension of Disgrace**

Al-Qurṭubī and other commentators note that disgrace on the Day of Judgment is considered worse than physical punishment. The shame of having one's sins exposed before all of creation — the loss of dignity, the awareness of having fallen short — is a suffering unique to that Day. Abraham's prayer reflects an awareness that even the most righteous have shortcomings, and that only God's mercy can shield them from the disgrace of exposure.

**The Hadith About Abraham and His Father**

Some commentators cite a tradition in which Abraham sees his father on the Day of Resurrection in a wretched state covered in dust. When Abraham asks God, "Didst thou not promise me that Thou wouldst not disgrace me on the day they are resurrected?" God replies, "I have forbidden the Garden to the disbelievers." This tradition illustrates the limits of Abraham's prayer for his father: the promise of no disgrace applied to Abraham himself, not to his father who chose disbelief.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:88

> **the Day when neither wealth nor children avail,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Uselessness of Worldly Resources**

Abraham describes the Day of Judgment as "the Day when neither wealth nor children avail" (*yawma lā yanfaʿu mālun wa-lā banūn). The verse states a fundamental Qur'anic principle: on the Day of Judgment, the things that give a person status, security, and comfort in this world — wealth and children — will be completely useless. Wealth cannot buy exemption from judgment; children cannot intercede for their parents. Every person stands alone before God.

**The Reversal of Worldly Values**

In this world, wealth and children are sources of strength: wealth provides security, options, and influence; children provide support, legacy, and emotional fulfillment. On the Day of Judgment, all of these advantages disappear. The billionaire stands in the same line as the pauper; the parent of ten faces the same accounting as the childless. The reversal is deliberate: it teaches that the values of this world are not the values of the next.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:89

> **save for him who comes to God with a sound heart."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sound Heart**

The only thing that avails on the Day of Judgment is "a sound heart" (*illā man atā Llāha bi-qalbin salīm). The word *salīm* — "sound" — means free from defects, healthy, whole. A "sound heart" is one that is free from polytheism, doubt, hypocrisy, and spiritual disease. It is the heart that has been purified through faith, repentance, and righteous conduct.

**The Multiple Meanings**

The commentators offer multiple dimensions of the "sound heart": it is the heart free from doubts and certain of God's reality (Q); the heart free from polytheism and sincere in its devotion (IK); the heart free from ignorance, vices, and attachment to this world (R); the heart that is spiritually healthy rather than sick (Th); the heart empty of all but its Lord and happy with Him alone (Su). Each interpretation adds a dimension: the sound heart is one that has been cleansed of everything that is not God.

**The Association with Abraham**

The "sound heart" is specifically associated with Abraham elsewhere in the Qur'an (37:84): "When he came to his Lord with a sound heart." Abraham is the exemplar of the sound heart — the one whose devotion to God was so complete that nothing else occupied the space that belonged to the Creator. His prayer in verse 89 is not merely a wish; it is a description of the state he has been cultivating throughout his life — and will bring with him to the meeting with God.

**The Modern Application**

For the modern reader, the verse is a challenge: what is the state of your heart? Is it sound — free from the diseases of doubt, hypocrisy, worldly attachment, and spiritual negligence? Or is it sick — infected by the things that displace God from the center of your being? The Day of Judgment will reveal the answer; the question is whether you will address it before then.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:90

> **And the Garden will be brought nigh unto the reverent,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Garden Approaches**

After describing the sound heart, Abraham's vision shifts to the outcome: "And the Garden will be brought nigh unto the reverent" (*wa-uzzilati l-jannahu li'l-muttaqīn). The word *uzzilati* — "will be brought nigh" — conveys the idea of the Garden being moved close, drawn near, presented before the righteous. This is not merely the righteous entering the Garden; it is the Garden coming to them — an honor that implies the Garden's eagerness to receive its inhabitants as much as the inhabitants' worthiness to enter it.

**The Joy of Seeing the Garden**

Al-Rāzī notes that part of the joy of the reverent will be seeing the Garden when they are brought near to it — experiencing its beauty, its fragrance, its vastness — before they even enter. This anticipation is itself a form of delight. The Garden is not hidden; it is made visible, its splendor displayed before those who are about to inherit it. The Qur'an elsewhere describes this:

> *"And the Garden will be brought near to the reverent, not far away."* (Qur'an 50:31)

**The Reverent — Al-Muttaqīn**

Those for whom the Garden is brought near are "the reverent" (*al-muttaqīn) — those who cultivated taqwā, the vigilant God-consciousness that protected them from sin. Abraham's prayer for a sound heart has been answered: the sound heart produced taqwā, and taqwā produces the Garden. The chain is complete: faith → sound heart → taqwā → Garden.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:91

> **and Hellfire will become apparent to the errant.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fire Made Visible**

In contrast to the Garden's approach to the righteous, "Hellfire will become apparent to the errant" (*wa-burrizati l-jaḥīmu li'l-ghāwīn). The word *burrizati* — "became apparent" or "was made visible" — means the Fire is exposed, uncovered, displayed in full. Just as the Garden is brought near to the righteous, the Fire is exposed to the errant. The symmetry is deliberate: both destinations are made visible to their respective inhabitants.

**The Double Suffering**

Al-Rāzī explains that the errant will suffer from both the sight of the Fire and its reality. Seeing it before entering it adds a dimension of psychological torment — the anticipation of punishment is itself a form of punishment. The errant see what is coming and cannot avoid it; the knowledge of what awaits them compounds their suffering.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:92

> **And it will be said unto them, "Where is that which you worshipped"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Interrogation of the Errant**

The errant are addressed with a question that cuts to the heart of their error: "Where is that which you worshipped?" (*ayna mā kuntum taʿbudūn). This is not a genuine question — God knows where the idols are — but a rhetorical exposure. On the Day of Judgment, the false objects of worship will be exposed for what they are: powerless, silent, absent. The questioner is asking: where are they now? Can they help you? Where is the protection you expected from them?

**The Exposure of Falsehood**

The interrogation serves to humiliate the errant by forcing them to confront the reality of their choices. Everything they worshipped — idols, false gods, worldly powers, personal desires — will be shown to be incapable of helping them. The exposure is total and public: not a private realization but a public declaration.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:93

> **apart from God? Are they helping you or helping themselves?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Final Humiliation**

The question continues: "apart from God? Are they helping you or helping themselves?" The answer is obvious: they cannot help anyone — not the worshippers, not even themselves. The idols, the false gods, the worldly powers that were invoked alongside God — all of them are exposed as utterly powerless. The rhetorical question forces the errant to confess what they already know: their worship was futile, their devotion was misdirected, and their supposed helpers cannot even help themselves.

**The Principle: False Gods Cannot Save**

This is one of the Qur'an's most consistent teachings: nothing worshipped alongside God can provide any help on the Day of Judgment. The principle applies not only to physical idols but to every modern substitute — wealth, status, ideology, personality cults, nationalism. None of them can save their devotees from the accounting before God.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:94

> **Then they shall be toppled into it, they and the errant,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Toppling Into Fire**

The verse describes the errant being "toppled" (*kubkimū) into the Fire. The word *kubkimū* conveys the sense of tumbling headfirst, falling upside down, being hurled in a disorderly mass. This is not a dignified entry; it is a violent, humiliating tumble. The errant — those who worshipped false gods — are thrown into the Fire along with the objects of their false worship.

**Together with Their False Gods**

The toppling includes both the worshippers and the worshipped: "they and the errant" — meaning they and those they followed into error, or they and their idols. The image is of the entire false system — the false gods, the false leaders, the false devotees — all tumbling together into the Fire. The unity they shared in error becomes a shared fate in punishment.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:95

> **and the hosts of Iblīs all together.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Hosts of Satan**

The verse adds: "and the hosts of Iblīs all together" (*wa-junūdu ibrīs ajmaʿūn). The "hosts of Iblīs" include all who followed Satan's lead — both jinn and human beings. The word *junūd* — "hosts" or "armies" — suggests an organized force: Satan has an army, and his army includes those who followed his whisperings, obeyed his temptations, and adopted his rebellion against God.

**The Complete Gathering**

The word *ajmaʿūn* — "all together" — emphasizes totality: the entire party of Satan — every jinn and every human who aligned with him — is gathered and cast into the Fire. There is no exception, no escape, no last-minute reprieve. The hosts of Iblīs are dealt with completely.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:96

> **They will say, as they dispute with one another therein,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Dispute in the Fire**

Inside the Fire, the errant begin to dispute with one another (*yaḥtāṣimūn). The word *ḥaṣama* means to dispute, to argue, to blame one another. This is a profound detail: even in punishment, the errant cannot find unity. The false solidarity that united them in their rejection of truth dissolves into blame, accusation, and mutual recrimination. The party of Satan, which appeared so unified in its rebellion, fractures in its punishment.

**The Psychology of Blame**

The dispute reflects a universal human tendency: when things go wrong, blame shifts. In the Fire, every errant person blames those who led them astray — the leaders blame the followers for following blindly, the followers blame the leaders for leading deceptively. The blame game never ends because no one accepts responsibility for their own choices.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:97

> **"By God, indeed we were in manifest error,"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Admission of Error**

The errant confess: "By God, indeed we were in manifest error" (*wa-qālū wallāhi in kunnā la-fī ḍalālin mubīn). The oath "by God" emphasizes the sincerity of their confession — too late to benefit them, but genuine. They acknowledge that their error was not subtle or hidden but "manifest" — clear, obvious, undeniable. They were not victims of a sophisticated deception; they were lost in plain sight, following a path that was obviously wrong.

**The Clarity of the Error in Retrospect**

The error appears "manifest" only in retrospect — after the punishment has begun. When they were in the world, the error did not appear manifest; it appeared normal, traditional, reasonable. Only when the consequences have arrived does the error become visible for what it was. This is a universal lesson: the consequences of error reveal what the error was.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:98

> **when we made you equal with the Lord of the worlds.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Confession to Their False Gods**

The errant address their false objects of worship: "when we made you equal with the Lord of the worlds." They acknowledge the specific nature of their error: it was not merely error in general but the specific sin of making equals with God — of setting created things alongside the Creator. This is the root sin — the original error from which all other errors flow.

**The Meaning of "Making Equal"**

The word for "made equal" (*nusāwīkum) comes from the same root as *sawāʾ* — "equal." The errant are confessing that they treated the idols, the false gods, the worldly powers as equal to God — giving them the worship, the obedience, and the devotion that belonged to God alone. In retrospect, the absurdity of this equation is manifest: the Creator of everything, infinite and eternal, was placed alongside created things, finite and temporary. The equation was never valid; it was always a catastrophic error.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:99

> **And none caused us to go astray but the guilty."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Blame of the Guilty**

The errant continue their confession and their blame: "And none caused us to go astray but the guilty" (*mā lanā min shafīʿīn wa-lā ṣadīqin ḥamīm). According to ʿIkrimah, "the guilty" refers to the son of Adam (Cain) and Iblīs, who inaugurated disbelief and sin. Others say it refers to the leaders and elders who misled the common people. Either way, the errant are acknowledging that their misguidance had specific sources — specific people or beings who led them astray.

**The Shared Accountability**

The verse does not absolve the followers of responsibility; it identifies the leaders who bore additional responsibility for misleading them. Both the leaders and the followers are in the Fire — but the leaders bear the additional burden of having caused others to go astray. This is the Qur'anic principle of shared accountability: those who mislead others carry both their own sin and a share of the sin of those they led astray.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:100

> **So now we have no intercessors,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Absence of Intercession**

The errant acknowledge that they have "no intercessors" (*fa-mā lanā min shāfiʿīn). In their worldly religion, they believed that their idols, their saints, or their leaders could intercede with God on their behalf. Now, in the Fire, they realize that no one can intercede. The intercession they expected has not materialized; the friends they trusted have abandoned them; the powers they invoked are powerless.

**The Qur'anic Teaching on Intercession**

The Qur'an teaches that intercession exists but is entirely subject to God's permission:

> *"Who is there that can intercede with Him except by His permission?"* (Qur'an 2:255)

The errant's mistake was not believing in intercession per se but believing that their false gods could intercede — when intercession belongs to God alone and is granted only to those He permits.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:101

> **nor any loyal friend.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Absence of True Friendship**

The errant also acknowledge they have "no loyal friend" (*wa-lā ṣadīqin ḥamīm). The word *ḥamīm* — "loyal" or "close" — denotes someone who genuinely cares about the other person, who feels their pain, who would help if they could. The errant find themselves utterly alone — no intercessor, no friend, no companion. The loneliness of the reckoning is itself a form of punishment.

**The Isolation of the Reckoning**

Al-Rāzī and al-Qurṭubī note that the Day of Judgment is a day of profound isolation. Every person stands alone before God — no parent can help their child, no child their parent, no friend their friend. The errant's confession of friendlessness is the realization of this isolation: the relationships they built on worldly grounds have dissolved, and they stand alone with their deeds.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:102

> **If we had another turn, we would be among the believers."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Desire for Return**

The errant express the universal desire of the damned: "If we had another turn, we would be among the believers" (*law anna lanā karratan fanakūna mina l-muʾminīn). They want a second chance — another turn in the world — so that they can live differently. This desire is expressed by the disbelievers throughout the Qur'an (2:167; 6:27; 23:99–100; 32:12) and is always denied. The second chance is not available because the time for choice has passed.

**The Sincerity That Comes Too Late**

The errant's statement is sincere — they genuinely wish they had believed. But sincerity at the moment of punishment does not count as faith. Faith must precede the encounter with punishment; once the punishment is visible, belief is not faith but mere recognition of reality. The verse teaches the most important timing lesson in the Qur'an: the time for faith is now — before the punishment arrives, before the door closes, before it is too late.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:103

> **Truly in that is a sign, but most of them are not believers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The First Half of the Refrain After Abraham**

Abraham's narrative closes, and the sūrah marks the close with the formula it will use after every prophetic account in the chapter.
