This verse delivers its first half: *"Truly in that is a sign, but most of them are not believers."* The second half — the naming of God as the Mighty and the Merciful — follows in the next verse, and the separation is deliberate, since the first half is a statement about human beings and the second a statement about God.

**What Constitutes the Sign**

Abraham's story is the sūrah's longest treatment of a single prophet's reasoning. It gives his confrontation with his father, his demolition of the idols and the argument he puts to his people, his survival of the fire, and his prayer for forgiveness. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr both treat the account as evidence in the technical sense: not a miracle performed to compel assent but a chain of reasoning that remains available for inspection. The sign is the argument itself, and it is why the verse can call it a sign *and* record that most did not believe without contradiction — the evidence was sufficient and the response was nevertheless refusal.

**"Most of Them": A Statement, Not a Complaint**

Al-Rāzī notes that *most of them* is generally understood to reach beyond Abraham's own people to the contemporaries of the Prophet ﷺ, and that the refrain is intended as a comfort to him. The clause is therefore doing double duty. Read as history, it records that Abraham's generation largely refused. Read as address, it tells a man watching his own preaching fail among people he loved that the failure is not a personal anomaly but the standard shape of prophetic history. The comfort offered is not the promise of improvement — the sūrah has just recorded the refusal — but the removal of isolation.

**The Position the Reader Occupies**

The verse leaves the hearer with an uncomfortable question rather than a conclusion. A sign has been given and is still inspectable; the report that most did not believe is a report about a group the reader is not yet told he belongs to. The refrain's second half will supply the divine character against which the refusal is to be read, and the sūrah withholds it for a full verse — long enough for the reader to notice which side of the statistic he is standing on.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:104

> **And truly thy Lord is indeed the Mighty, the Merciful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Half of the Refrain**

The refrain that closes each prophetic narrative in this sūrah is a single sentence spoken in two voices, and the sūrah has kept them apart deliberately. Verse 103 delivers the first half — *"Truly in that is a sign, but most of them are not believers"* — which is a statement about human beings. This verse delivers the second half, and it is a statement about God. The separation matters, because the movement from one to the other is the movement the whole sūrah is training its reader to make: from the observed fact of unbelief to the character of the One whom the unbelievers are refusing.

The first half could easily be read as a verdict of despair. *Most of them are not believers* is, on its face, a report of failure. Left there, it would sit as a bare statistic of refusal. What the second half does is refuse to let the statistic be the last word, and it does so not by disputing the statistic but by naming two divine attributes that remain intact whatever the statistic says.

**Why *al-ʿAzīz* Comes Before *al-Raḥīm***

The order is not incidental. *Al-ʿAzīz*, "the Mighty" or "the Invincible," is the Name that establishes that God's power is not contingent on anyone's acceptance of it. The root *ʿ-z-z* carries the sense of strength that cannot be overcome and of a status that no rival can reach. Placed immediately after the report that most people did not believe, it answers an unspoken question: does widespread refusal diminish God? It does not. The Might is unaffected by the response. Al-Ṭabarī reads the refrain in exactly this register — the sign has been given, the refusal has been recorded, and neither event has altered the divine standing.

Only then does *al-Raḥīm*, "the Merciful," arrive. Had mercy been named first, the sequence might have suggested that mercy depends on being wanted. Named second, it reads as what it is: a mercy that persists after refusal has been documented. The two Names together form the shortest possible statement of the sūrah's theology of history — God is not weakened by rejection and not soured by it.

**Comfort Addressed to a Single Listener**

Al-Rāzī notes that *most of them* is generally understood to refer not only to the ancient peoples but to the contemporaries of the Prophet ﷺ, and that the refrain is intended as a comfort to him. This is worth pressing, because it identifies the actual audience of a line that looks like a general maxim. The sūrah was revealed to a man who was watching his own preaching fail among people he loved, in a city where he had been trusted his whole life. The refrain tells him that the pattern he is living through is not a personal anomaly but the standard shape of prophetic history.

There is a pastoral logic here that is easy to miss. The comfort offered is not the promise that things will improve; the sūrah has just recorded that Abraham's people did not believe either. The comfort is the removal of isolation. A person who believes his failure is unique experiences it twice over — as failure and as evidence that something is wrong with him specifically. The refrain removes the second burden while leaving the first honestly in place.

**The Refrain as Structural Memory**

The sūrah repeats this pair at the close of each narrative — after Moses and Pharaoh, after Abraham, after Noah, after Hūd, after Ṣāliḥ, after Lot, after Shuʿayb — and the repetition does work that no single statement could do. A sign stated once is information. A sign followed seven times by the same closing formula becomes a rhythm the hearer comes to expect, and expectation is what turns a text into something memorisable. The classical commentators observed the sūrah's rhythmic and poetic character precisely here: refrains binding the prophetic accounts together and demonstrating that the prophets' fundamental teaching, and the fundamental refusal it met, were the same.

So this verse is simultaneously the smallest unit in the sūrah and one of its load-bearing elements. Read alone it is eleven words. Read as the seventh return of a formula, it is the sūrah's way of asserting that history is not a sequence of unrelated events but a single argument conducted across generations, with one consistent divine character running through all of it.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:105

> **The people of Noah denied the messengers,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third Prophetic Narrative: Noah**

The sūrah now turns to Noah — the third prophetic narrative and the first of the shorter accounts. Noah's story is introduced with a striking phrase: "The people of Noah denied the messengers" (*kadhdhabat qawmu nūḥi l-mursalīn). The plural "messengers" (*al-mursalīn) is used even though Noah is the specific prophet being discussed. Al-Rāzī explains that the plural is used because rejecting one messenger implies rejecting all messengers — since the message is the same, denying any prophet is effectively denying the entire institution of prophethood.

**The Universal Pattern of Denial**

The phrase establishes the pattern: denial (*takdīb*) is the defining action of Noah's people. They did not merely disagree or doubt; they actively denied — declared the message false, rejected the messenger, and refused to engage with the evidence. This is the first degree of rejection: not ignorance but active denial.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:106

> **when their brother Noah said unto them, "Will you not be reverent?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Their Brother" — The Prophetic Kinship**

Noah is described as "their brother" — meaning he was one of them, from among them, not a stranger or an outsider. This is significant: the prophet always comes from within the community he is sent to. He shares their language, their culture, their experience. He is not an alien force imposing foreign ideas; he is a family member calling his own people to truth.

**The Call to Reverence**

Noah's opening question — "Will you not be reverent?" (*alā tattaqūn) — is the same question Moses used with Pharaoh's people. The call to taqwā is the universal first step of the prophetic message: be mindful of God, protect yourselves from the consequences of disobedience, cultivate the awareness that keeps you on the right path. Taqwā is not fear of God in the sense of being afraid of Him; it is fear of offending Him — the same kind of careful reverence a loving child feels toward a beloved parent.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:107

> **Truly I am a trustworthy messenger unto you.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Trustworthy Messenger**

Noah describes himself as "a trustworthy messenger" (*rasūl amīn). The trustworthiness is key: the people have known Noah their entire lives. He was not a stranger who arrived with an unknown message; he was a known quantity — honest, reliable, truthful. His entire life had been a testimony to his trustworthiness. The people's rejection of him was not based on any evidence of dishonesty; it was based on their refusal to accept the implications of his message.

**The Contrast with the Accusation**

The description of Noah as "trustworthy" contrasts with the accusations his opponents will level against him: that he is followed only by the lowly, that his message is mere tradition, that he should be stoned or expelled. None of these accusations touch his core quality: trustworthiness. His life speaks louder than their words.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:108

> **So reverence God and obey me.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sequence of Obedience**

Noah's command follows the proper sequence: first reverence God, then obey the messenger. The order matters: obedience to the prophet is valid only insofar as it is rooted in reverence for God. If someone obeys the prophet out of social pressure, personal loyalty, or tribal allegiance — without reverence for God — the obedience is hollow. True obedience flows from taqwā; taqwā is the foundation, and obedience to the messenger is the fruit.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:109

> **And I ask not of you any reward for it; my reward lies only with the Lord of the worlds.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Absence of Material Motivation**

Noah declares that he seeks no reward from his people for his message. The prophets do not charge fees; they do not seek wealth, status, or power from those they call to God. Their reward is with God alone. This declaration serves multiple purposes: it establishes the prophets' disinterestedness (they are not motivated by material gain), it removes any excuse for rejection (you cannot claim the prophet is doing this for money), and it models the proper attitude for every caller to truth (do it for God, not for the compensation of people).

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:110

> **So reverence God and obey me."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Repetition as Emphasis**

The command "reverence God and obey me" is repeated here after the declaration of disinterestedness. The repetition is deliberate: having established his credentials (trustworthiness, disinterestedness), Noah reiterates the core demand. The structure of the prophetic message is simple: reverence God, obey the messenger. Everything else — the arguments, the signs, the stories — serves this central demand.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:111

> **They said, "Shall we believe you, when the lowliest follow you?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Snobbery of the Disbelievers**

Noah's people respond with class-based rejection: "Shall we believe you, when the lowliest follow you?" (*a-nuʾminu laka wa-ittabʿaka l-arthalūn). The word *arthalūn* — "the lowliest" — refers to the poor, the weak, the socially insignificant. The disbelievers cannot accept a message whose followers are from the lower classes. Their objection is not to the content of the message but to the social composition of its adherents.

**The Snobbery That Blocks Truth**

This is one of the most common barriers to accepting truth: the refusal to associate with those deemed socially inferior. The disbelievers are saying: if only the poor and lowly follow you, then your message cannot be for us — people of status, wealth, and refinement. The objection reveals that their criterion for truth is not evidence but social prestige: they want a message that comes with elite approval.

**The Quraysh's Parallel Objection**

The Quraysh of Makkah made exactly the same objection to the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ:

> *"Had it been good, they would not have outstripped us in [attaining] it."* (Qur'an 46:11)

They were referring to the poor Companions — ʿAmmār, Ṣuhayb, Bilāl — and saying that no person of status would follow a religion whose adherents were from the lower classes. The Qur'an's response, through Noah and through Muhammad ﷺ, is the same: the truth is not determined by the social status of its adherents but by its own intrinsic validity.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:112

> **He said, "What knowledge have I of what they used to do?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Noah's Disavowal of Responsibility**

Noah's response is masterful: "What knowledge have I of what they used to do?" (*mā ʿilmī bi-mā kānū yaʿmalūn). He is saying: I am not responsible for who follows me. I do not investigate the backgrounds, the social status, or the past histories of those who respond to my call. My task is to deliver the message; their task is to respond. What they did before they came to me is between them and God.

**The Prophet's Limited Mandate**

Noah's statement establishes a fundamental principle: the prophet is not responsible for the inner states, motivations, or histories of his followers. He cannot inspect hearts; he cannot judge intentions; he cannot screen followers by social class. His task is delivery; judgment belongs to God. This principle protects the prophetic mission from the impossible burden of guaranteeing the purity of every adherent.

**The Implicit Critique of the Objectors**

Noah's response also contains an implicit critique: you are asking me to do something that is not my job. You are asking me to judge people's hearts and social standings — but that is God's job, not mine. By making this demand, you are revealing that you do not understand the nature of the prophetic mission.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:113

> **Their reckoning is only by my Lord, were you but aware.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Restriction of Judgment to God**

Noah states explicitly: "Their reckoning is only by my Lord, were you but aware" (*in ḥisābuhum illā ʿalā rabbī law tashʿurūn). The restriction (*inn... illā*) is emphatic: their reckoning is ONLY on God — not on Noah, not on any prophet, not on any human being. The phrase "were you but aware" is a gentle rebuke: if you only understood how the prophetic mission works, you would not make this demand.

**The Democratic Nature of the Divine Call**

By declaring that the reckoning belongs to God alone, Noah implicitly declares that his message is open to all — rich and poor, high and low, powerful and weak. The door of the prophetic call is not guarded by social criteria; it is open to every soul that responds. This is the democratic nature of the divine call: God's message does not come with a class requirement.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:114

> **And I shall not drive away the believers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refusal to Exclude**

Noah declares: "And I shall not drive away the believers" (*wa-mā ana bi-ṭāridi l-muʾminīn). This is a direct refusal of the disbelievers' demand. They wanted Noah to exclude the poor and lowly; Noah refuses. The believers — regardless of their social status — will not be driven away. The prophetic community is inclusive: whoever believes is welcome, regardless of class, race, wealth, or background.

**The Principle of Inclusion**

This verse establishes one of the most important principles in Islamic communal life: no believer is to be excluded from the community on the basis of social status, wealth, or background. The Prophet Muhammad ﷺ was commanded to the same principle when the Quraysh asked him to exclude the poor Companions (6:52; 18:28). The community of faith is open to all who believe — no exceptions.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:115

> **I am but a clear warner."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prophet as Warner**

Noah defines his role with precision: "I am but a clear warner" (*in ana illā nadhīrun mubīn). The word *nadhīr* — "warner" — is one of the two primary functions of the prophet (the other being *bashīr* — "bearer of glad tidings"). Noah's task is to warn — to make the consequences of rejection clear, to communicate the stakes, to ensure that no one can claim ignorance. The warning is "clear" (*mubīn*) — unmistakable, direct, without ambiguity.

**The Limitation Implied in "But"**

The word "but" (*illā) is limiting: "I am BUT a clear warner." Noah is not claiming to be anything more than a warner. He is not a king, a judge, a social reformer (in the political sense), or a military commander. His role is specific and limited: to warn clearly. The outcome of the warning is God's concern, not his.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:116

> **They said, "Truly if you cease not, O Noah, you shall indeed be among the stoned."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Threat of Violence**

The disbelievers escalate from rejection to threat: "Truly if you cease not, O Noah, you shall indeed be among the stoned" (*la-in lam tantahi yā nūḥu la-takūnanna mina l-marjūmīn). The word *marjūm* — "stoned" — can mean literally stoned to death or metaphorically "accursed" or "vilified." Al-Qurṭubī draws attention to the parallel with Abraham's father's threat in 19:46: "If you cease not, I shall surely stone you!" — which is understood as metaphorical stoning (verbal abuse and social ostracism).

**The Escalation from Argument to Violence**

The pattern is consistent: when argument fails, threats begin. The disbelievers cannot refute Noah's message, so they threaten him with violence. This is the tyrant's default: silence the messenger because you cannot silence the message. The threat of stoning is particularly brutal — it represents not just punishment but communal execution, the entire community participating in the elimination of the truth-bearer.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:117

> **He said, "My Lord, verily my people have denied me."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Noah's Complaint to God**

Faced with the threat of violence, Noah turns to God: "My Lord, verily my people have denied me" (*qāla rabbi inna qawmī kadhdabūn). The complaint is not a demand for revenge but a statement of fact presented to the One who can act. Noah is not taking matters into his own hands; he is bringing the situation to God. This is the prophetic pattern in the face of rejection: complain to God, not about God.

**The Sadness of Rejection**

The tone of Noah's complaint is one of deep sadness. He has delivered the message clearly, faithfully, and without seeking any reward. His people have rejected it and now threaten him with violence. The sadness is not about personal hurt but about the spiritual loss of his people — they are rejecting the very message that could save them.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:118

> **So decide between me and them, and deliver me and the believers who are with me!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prayer for Judgment**

Noah asks God to "decide between me and them" (*faftaḥ baynī wa-baynahum fath) — literally, "open between us" — meaning: render judgment, make the distinction clear, resolve the conflict. The word *fath* — "opening" or "judgment" — implies both a legal verdict and a resolution of the impasse. Noah cannot resolve the situation by himself; only God can decide between the truth and the falsehood.

**The Prayer for Deliverance**

Noah also asks for deliverance — for himself and for "the believers who are with me." The deliverance is communal: Noah does not pray only for himself but for the entire believing community. The believers who have responded to his call, the lowly ones whom the disbelievers despise — they are all included in Noah's prayer for rescue.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:119

> **So We delivered him and those who were with him in the full-laden Ark.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Deliverance Through the Ark**

God answers Noah's prayer: "So We delivered him and those who were with him in the full-laden Ark" (*fa-annjaynāhu wa-man maʿahu fi l-fulki l-mashhūn). The word *fulk* — "Ark" — refers to the vessel God commanded Noah to build. The word *mashhūn* — "full-laden" — means loaded, filled to capacity. The Ark carried not only the believers but also pairs of every species of animal (11:40) — a complete preservation of life from the destruction that was about to engulf the earth.

**The Symbolism of the Ark**

The Ark is a symbol of salvation — the vessel through which God's mercy carries the believers through the storm of punishment. The image resonates across religious traditions: the Ark of Noah is a symbol of divine protection, of faith carrying its passengers through the flood of divine judgment.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:120

> **Then afterwards We drowned those who remained.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Drowning of the Disbelievers**

After the deliverance of Noah and the believers, "We drowned those who remained" (*thumma aghraqnā baʿda l-bāqīn). The word "remained" (*al-bāqīn) refers to those who stayed behind — the disbelievers who refused to board the Ark. They "remained" on the earth, confident that they were right, and the earth swallowed them in the flood.

**The Second Drowning in the Sūrah**

This is the second drowning in this sūrah — Pharaoh's army was drowned in the sea (v. 66), and Noah's people were drowned in the flood. The repetition is deliberate: drowning is one of the Qur'an's most powerful images of divine punishment. It conveys totality — the water covers everything, leaves nothing untouched, and gives no second chance. Both drownings serve as eternal signs: the consequences of rejecting the messengers of God.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:121

> **Truly in that is a sign, but most of them are not believers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain After Noah**

Noah's account closes, and the sūrah marks the close with the first half of its recurring formula: *"Truly in that is a sign, but most of them are not believers."* The second half, naming God as the Mighty and the Merciful, follows in the next verse. This is the refrain's third appearance, after Pharaoh and after Abraham, and by now its rhythm is familiar enough that the reader hears the second half arriving before it is spoken.

**Why Noah's Story Qualifies as a Sign**

The sūrah gives Noah only sixteen verses and spends none of them on the flood. It records the call, the refusal, the demand that the lowly followers be driven out, the prayer of separation, and the rescue. The omission is a decision about what the story is for: this is not a narrative of catastrophe but a case filed as evidence. Al-Ṭabarī reads the account as evidence in the technical sense — an argument that remains inspectable rather than a miracle that compels assent — which is what allows the verse to call it a sign and record the refusal in the same breath.

**The Statistic and the Reader**

Al-Rāzī notes that *most of them* is generally taken to reach beyond Noah's own people to the contemporaries of the Prophet ﷺ, and that the refrain is intended as a comfort to him. The clause is doing double duty. As history it records that Noah's generation largely refused. As address it tells a man watching his own mission fail that the failure is not a personal anomaly. The comfort is not the promise of improvement but the removal of isolation — the sūrah has just documented the refusal, and offers no reversal.

**What Is Withheld Until the Next Verse**

The first half, standing alone, is a report of failure and could easily be read as a verdict of despair. The sūrah leaves it there for one full verse. The reader is made to sit with the statistic before being given the divine character against which it is to be weighed, which is the sūrah's way of ensuring that neither half dissolves the other.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:122

> **And truly thy Lord is indeed the Mighty, the Merciful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain After Noah**

Noah's account in this sūrah is compressed into sixteen verses, and the sūrah chooses to spend none of them on the flood. It gives the call, the refusal, the mockery about the lowly followers, the prayer of separation, and the rescue — and then it closes with the refrain. The omission is a decision about what the story is *for*. This is not a narrative of catastrophe for its own sake; it is a case filed as evidence, and verse 122 is the stamp that files it.

What makes the placement pointed is what immediately precedes it. Noah's people did not merely decline his message; they told him to drive out the poorest of his followers as a condition of negotiation. The refusal was therefore not intellectual hesitation but a demand that the messenger compromise the composition of his own community. Against that, the refrain's assertion that God is *the Mighty, the Merciful* is a correction of the terms on which Noah's people thought the negotiation was being conducted.

**Might and Mercy Against a Class Objection**

The objection Noah faced was a class objection: his followers were the contemptible, the ones with no standing. The two Names answer it on both sides. *Al-ʿAzīz* answers the assumption that strength is measured by social rank — the might that matters is not the might of the Quraysh-style aristocracy or its ancient equivalent, and it was never distributed according to pedigree. *Al-Raḥīm* answers the assumption that God's favour tracks human prestige. The mercy that reached those followers reached them because it is mercy, not because they qualified.

Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī both draw the pastoral consequence: the messenger's credibility does not depend on the eminence of his adherents, and a community's worth before God is not an aggregate of its members' social value. This is why the refrain is placed here rather than stated abstractly elsewhere. The sūrah states it at the moment the principle is under attack.

**The Reader's Position**

Read in sequence, the refrain does something to the reader that it does not do to Noah. Noah heard it as reassurance. The reader of the sūrah, arriving at it for the second time, hears it as confirmation of a pattern — and a pattern is a claim about what will happen next. If unbelief is what closed Noah's story, and unbelief is what closes the story the reader's own contemporaries are enacting, then the refrain is not only comfort but warning, and the sūrah does not spell out which reading applies because it wants both to be held at once.

That double address is characteristic of Qur'anic rhetoric and of this sūrah in particular. The same eleven words that steady the prophet unsettle the audience. The Mighty is mighty toward both; the Merciful is merciful toward both; and the difference between the two outcomes lies entirely in the response the first half of the refrain has just described.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:123

> **ʿĀd denied the messengers,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fourth Prophetic Narrative: Hūd and ʿĀd**

The narrative shifts to Hūd and his people, the tribe of ʿĀd. The introduction follows the same pattern: "ʿĀd denied the messengers" (*kadhdhabat ʿādun l-mursalīn). The ʿĀd were an ancient Arab tribe — powerful, tall, and architecturally accomplished — who lived in the region of southern Arabia (modern-day Yemen/Oman). They are mentioned throughout the Qur'an as a paradigm of a civilization destroyed for its arrogance and oppression.

**The Characterization of ʿĀd**

The ʿĀd are characterized in the Qur'an by their pursuit of domination and worldly glory. They built monumental structures (89:6–8), claimed unmatched power (41:15), and oppressed those weaker than themselves. Their sin was not idolatry alone but the arrogance that accompanies absolute worldly power — the conviction that material strength is the ultimate reality.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:124

> **when their brother Hūd said unto them, “Will you not be reverent?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prophetic Pattern Repeated**

These verses follow exactly the same pattern as Noah's narrative (vv. 106–109): brother-prophet, call to taqwā, declaration of trustworthiness, command to reverence God and obey the messenger, disavowal of material reward. The repetition is not redundancy; it is emphasis. The message of every prophet is the same; the pattern of every prophetic mission is the same. What differed was not the message but the people and their response.

**Hūd as "Their Brother"**

Like Noah, Hūd is described as "their brother" — a member of their own tribe, speaking their language, sharing their experience. The message comes from within, not from outside. The ʿĀd cannot claim that Hūd is a foreigner imposing foreign ideas; he is one of them, calling them to the truth that their own nature should recognize.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:125

> **Truly I am a trustworthy messenger unto you.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Same Word, a Different People**

Hūd opens his mission to ʿĀd with the identical claim Noah had made to his own people — *rasūl amīn*, "a trustworthy messenger" — and the sūrah's repetition of the phrase across prophets is the first thing to notice. The word *amīn* is built on the root *ʾ-m-n*, the root of *īmān* and *amān*, faith and security; an *amīn* is one in whom something may be safely deposited. A messenger is called *amīn* because a message is a deposit. He has been entrusted with something that is not his, and the whole question of his character reduces to whether he will deliver it unaltered.

Al-Rāzī draws out why this claim is the strongest available to a prophet. He cannot demonstrate the message directly; revelation is not a public object that can be inspected. What he can offer is a lifetime of verifiable behaviour in front of people who have known him since childhood. Hūd was not a stranger arriving with a doctrine. He was a man of ʿĀd, one of their own, whose dealings they had watched for decades. The claim *I am trustworthy* is therefore not an assertion but a citation of public record.

**Trust as the Only Currency a Prophet Has**

The logic becomes clear when set against what the messengers refuse in the very next verses. Hūd will say that he asks no payment, and the connection between the two statements is exact. A man who charges for a message has an interest in its content; he benefits if it is agreeable and loses if it is not. A man who refuses payment has removed himself from the transaction, and it is that removal which makes the deposit intact. Trustworthiness and the refusal of reward are not two separate virtues but one virtue seen from two sides.

This is also why the accusation of lying, which ʿĀd will shortly make, is so hollow. They do not allege that Hūd has been caught in a falsehood; they allege that he is under some influence and that his followers are contemptible. Both charges concede the point he has just made. Nobody in ʿĀd could plausibly say that Hūd had lied to them before.

**What the Repetition Establishes**

By the second occurrence of the phrase the sūrah has begun to make a structural claim: the qualification for prophethood does not change between peoples. Noah faced a community that mocked his followers; Hūd faces a community proud of its monuments and its strength. Different sins, different civilisations, one identical credential. Al-Rāzī's observation that the path to recognising a messenger is universal and does not vary with historical circumstance is stated here in narrative form rather than as a principle.

For the sūrah's first audience the edge was immediate. The Prophet ﷺ was known among the Quraysh as *al-Amīn* long before the revelation, and al-Qurṭubī notes the parallel explicitly: he had given them no reason to mistrust him, having been truthful all his life. The verse is thus doing double duty — reporting what Hūd said to ʿĀd, and answering, without naming them, the men who were saying the same things in Makkah.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:126

> **So reverence God and obey me.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Commands in a Fixed Order**

The verse joins reverence for God with obedience to the messenger in a single sentence, and the order is not arbitrary. Al-Rāzī's gloss is precise: the call to reverence God precedes the call to obey Hūd because the second depends on the first. Obedience to a messenger is not an independent obligation; it is derivative. It is binding precisely because the messenger carries a message from the One who is to be revered, and it expires the moment the messenger asks for something the revered One has not authorised.

This ordering protects against the corruption to which religious authority is permanently exposed. A leader whose authority rests on his own person can demand anything. A leader whose authority rests on the message can demand only what the message contains. The sūrah states the hierarchy in one line and thereby sets the limit on every claim to religious obedience that will ever be made in its name.

**What *Taqwā* Means Here**

The word rendered *reverence* is *taqwā*, from *w-q-y*, to guard or shield oneself. Al-Rāzī notes that in this context it carries the sense of mindfulness — *"Will you not be mindful of disobedience toward Me?"* — and that reading sharpens the command considerably. *Taqwā* is not fear in the sense of terror; it is the sustained attention of a person who knows that his actions have consequences he cannot undo afterwards. The classical analogy, familiar from the treatment of 2:2, is of a man walking a road where hidden damage lies under the surface: he is not frightened of the road, he is careful on it.

ʿĀd's specific failure makes the command pointed. They built as though permanence were available for purchase and exercised power as though no accounting would follow. Both are failures of *taqwā* in its exact sense — not wickedness so much as inattention, the confident assumption that nothing would ever be settled. The command therefore does not ask them to feel something new; it asks them to stop forgetting.

**Obedience Defined by Its Object**

Al-Qurṭubī observes that the people were to obey Hūd when he enjoined faith upon them, which is to say that the obedience commanded is obedience within a defined scope. The verse does not establish Hūd's authority over ʿĀd's affairs generally; it establishes it over the matter he has been sent about. The distinction is the difference between a messenger and a ruler, and the sūrah is careful to keep it.

Read alongside the verse before it, the pair forms a complete account of prophetic authority: a claim of trustworthiness grounded in public record, and a demand for obedience grounded in the character of the One who sent him. Neither element appeals to the messenger's own standing. Both would collapse if he did.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:127

> **And I ask not of you any reward for it; my reward lies only with the Lord of the worlds.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refusal That Authenticates the Claim**

The third line of the prophetic opening is the one that does the evidentiary work. Having claimed trustworthiness and demanded obedience, Hūd removes the obvious motive for fabrication. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī both treat this as a recurring theme of Qur'anic prophetology: the messengers do not seek enrichment or status in return for the message, and the refusal is offered as evidence rather than as virtue-signalling.

The reasoning is worth stating plainly, because it is the reasoning a merchant society would recognise. A man selling a product profits if the product is believed. His testimony about the product is therefore worth less than the testimony of a man who gains nothing from its acceptance. Hūd is not performing generosity; he is eliminating a conflict of interest, and he says so in commercial terms — *I ask not of you any reward*.

***Rabb al-ʿĀlamīn*: the Scope of the Alternative**

The second clause names the party from whom the reward is expected: *the Lord of the worlds*. The phrase is the same one that opens the sūrah's sister chapter, al-Fātiḥah, and its function here is to widen the frame. Hūd is not declining payment in order to take it elsewhere in secret; he is relocating the transaction out of ʿĀd's jurisdiction entirely. Al-Rāzī reads *al-ʿālamīn* in its widest sense — every level of created existence, everything other than God — so that the reward expected is not merely deferred but drawn from an order that ʿĀd cannot see, audit or obstruct.

That relocation has a practical consequence for the hearer. A man paid by his audience can be pressured by his audience; he will soften what displeases them. A man paid by the Lord of the worlds cannot be pressured at all, which is exactly why ʿĀd's threats and mockery have no leverage over Hūd. The refusal of reward is, functionally, a declaration of independence.

**The Formula's Fifth Element**

Set beside the two verses before it, this completes the four-line opening that the sūrah gives to each of its prophets: *Will you not be reverent? / Truly I am a trustworthy messenger unto you / So reverence God and obey me / And I ask not of you any reward.* The sequence is an argument, not a list. It establishes the hearer's exposure, the messenger's credibility, the demand that follows from both, and the absence of any self-interest behind the demand.

The sūrah repeats this opening at 26:105–9, 123–27, 141–45, 160–64 and 176–80, and the repetition makes the same point the refrain makes: the shape of prophetic mission does not vary with the civilisation it addresses. ʿĀd was wealthy, militarised and architecturally confident, and it was addressed in exactly the terms used with a people of farmers and a people of merchants. The uniformity is the claim.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:128

> **Do you frivolously build on every high place a sign?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Monumental Arrogance**

Hūd challenges his people's architectural obsession: "Do you frivolously build on every high place a sign?" (*a-tabnūna bi-kullu rīʿin āyatan). The word *rīʿ* — "high place" — can also mean a path or road. The ʿĀd built monuments on every hilltop and along every road — not for practical purposes but as displays of power and wealth. Their architecture was an expression of their arrogance: every structure was a declaration of their perceived permanence and dominance.

**Building for Immortality**

Al-Rāzī and Ibn Kathīr observe that the ʿĀd built as though they would live forever. Their monumental architecture was an attempt to achieve immortality through stone — to leave a mark that time could not erase. But the irony is that the very structures they built to proclaim their permanence became the ruins that testified to their destruction. The lesson is universal: no monument can make its builder immortal; only God endures.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:129

> **And do you occupy castles, that haply you may ever abide?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Illusion of Permanence**

Hūd continues: "And do you occupy castles, that haply you may ever abide?" (*wa-tattakhidhūna maṣāniʿa laʿallakum takhladūn). The word *maṣāniʿ* — "castles" or "fortresses" — refers to the ʿĀd's massive structures — fortresses, aqueducts, palaces. The phrase "that haply you may ever abide" (*laʿallakum takhladūn*) exposes their motivation: they built fortresses as if they would live forever — seeking permanence through architecture.

**The Futility of Seeking Permanence in the World**

Al-Rāzī reflects: the desire for permanence is natural to the human soul — but the soul seeks it in the wrong place. True permanence (khalād) is in the Hereafter, not in worldly structures. The ʿĀd tried to build their way to immortality, but the only immortality available is through God. Every civilization that builds for eternity on this earth is repeating the ʿĀd's error: the earth and everything on it will pass away; only the Face of God remains (55:26–27).

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:130

> **And when you strike, do you strike as tyrants?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Violence of the ʿĀd**

Hūd names their third characteristic: "And when you strike, do you strike as tyrants?" (*wa-idhā basaṭtum basaṭtum jabbārīn). The word *jabbārīn* — "tyrants" — comes from the same root as one of God's Names: al-Jabbār (the Compeller). The ʿĀd's use of force was tyrannical — heavy-handed, brutal, oppressive. When they had power over others, they used it to dominate and crush, not to protect and serve.

**The Three Sins**

Al-Rāzī identifies three sins of the ʿĀd: (1) the desire for glory — building monuments; (2) the desire for permanence — building as if immortal; (3) the desire for domination — striking as tyrants. These three vices correspond to the irascible soul's desire for supremacy. For al-Rāzī, these are vices that belong properly to God alone — glory, eternity, and absolute power. When human beings claim them, they are not elevating themselves; they are transgressing their limits.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:131

> **So reverence God and obey me.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Return to the Core Message**

After describing the ʿĀd's three sins, Hūd returns to the core demand: "So reverence God and obey me." The structure of the prophetic message is: diagnosis of the problem, then the prescription. The problem is arrogance, monumentality, and tyranny. The prescription is taqwā and obedience to the messenger. The cure for the disease of self-aggrandizement is humility before God and submission to His guidance.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:132

> **And reverence Him who has succored you with what you know,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Enumeration of Blessings**

Hūd reminds the ʿĀd of God's blessings: cattle, children, gardens, springs. These are the basic elements of a prosperous agricultural society — livestock for sustenance and trade, offspring for continuity and strength, gardens for food and beauty, springs for water and life. The enumeration is deliberate: God has given the ʿĀd everything they need, and they are repaying His generosity with arrogance and tyranny.

**The Logic of Gratitude**

The logic underlying these verses is the logic of gratitude: the One who has given you everything you possess deserves your worship. If the ʿĀd are honest about the source of their blessings — the cattle that graze, the children who play, the gardens that bloom, the springs that flow — they must acknowledge that these are gifts from God. And the appropriate response to gifts is gratitude — expressed through worship and obedience.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:133

> **succored you with cattle and children,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Inventory Begins**

Hūd's argument to ʿĀd takes an unusual form: instead of opening with a warning, he opens with an inventory of what they have been given. The verses list cattle and children, gardens and springs, and the rhetorical force of the list depends entirely on the verb that governs it. They were *succored* — given these things as an act of aid — and the word makes the possessions something other than achievements.

Al-Qurṭubī's gloss on this passage is the key to it: God made the things mentioned here subservient and useful to human beings, and so He should be thanked for them. The verse is therefore not a rebuke about wealth but a correction of attribution. ʿĀd had read its prosperity as evidence of its own competence; Hūd reads it as evidence of a favour it did not confer on itself.

**Cattle and Children as the Two Ancient Capitals**

The pairing is deliberate and would have been immediately legible to the sūrah's first hearers. In an economy without currency in the modern sense, wealth was held in two forms: livestock, which is capital that reproduces, and children, who are labour, defence and continuity. To be *succored* with both is to be given the complete apparatus of a self-sustaining society.

That is why the charge that follows is so severe. ʿĀd was not a marginal people scraping by; it possessed the full complement of what its world counted as security. The failure the verse identifies is not deprivation-driven but abundance-driven — the specific moral blindness that arises when a community has everything it considers necessary and concludes that it therefore needs nothing further.

**Gratitude as the Missing Act**

The thanksgiving al-Qurṭubī names is not a feeling but an acknowledgement with consequences. To recognise that cattle and children were given is to accept that they are held on terms; to treat them as self-acquired is to claim an independence the facts do not support. The Qur'an's treatment of gratitude is consistently of this kind — a cognitive posture that reorganises how a person handles what he has.

Hūd's inventory is the opening move of an argument that will end in destruction, and its gentleness is worth noting. Before any threat, the people are reminded of what has been done for them. The sequence — blessing named, ingratitude identified, warning given — is the standard prophetic order in this sūrah, and it establishes that the punishment was preceded by an appeal to memory rather than by a demand for submission.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:134

> **and gardens and springs.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Half of the Inventory**

The list continues from livestock and offspring to cultivated land and water. The addition matters because it moves from what a community breeds to what it settles. Cattle can be driven; gardens and springs fix a people to a place. By the end of the two verses ʿĀd has been described as possessing every element of durable settlement — food, labour, defence, water and permanent agriculture.

Al-Qurṭubī's principle applies across the whole inventory: these things were made subservient and useful to human beings, and so God should be thanked for them. The word *subservient* carries weight. The springs do not flow because ʿĀd commanded them; the gardens bear because conditions they did not create permit bearing. The community is living inside a system it did not author.

**Why Springs Are Named Separately**

The sūrah mentions springs alongside gardens rather than assuming the reader will supply the connection, and the specification is not redundant. In an arid region a garden without a spring is a description of something that does not exist; naming both identifies the community as one whose agriculture depends on water it cannot produce. The Thamūd inventory later in the sūrah repeats the same pairing, which suggests the formula is deliberate rather than decorative.

The effect on the hearer is to make the blessing concrete enough to be checked. This is not a general claim that God provides; it is an itemised account of a specific settlement's water supply. A community that can verify the list cannot easily dispute the premise.

**Abundance and the Failure of *Taqwā***

Read against the question that opened Hūd's mission — *Will you not be reverent?* — the inventory supplies the answer's context. *Taqwā* is the disposition of someone who does not take his security for granted, and ʿĀd's security was, by the standards of its world, complete. The verses that follow record its pride in building monuments on every height and fortresses as though it would live forever.

The connection between the two is causal in the sūrah's presentation. It is not that ʿĀd had wealth and separately became arrogant; the wealth produced the assumption of permanence, and the assumption of permanence dissolved the attention that *taqwā* requires. The inventory is therefore the diagnosis, not merely the scene-setting — it names the conditions under which the failure of reverence became almost automatic.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:135

> **Truly I fear for you the punishment of a tremendous day!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Warning of Punishment**

Hūd concludes with a warning: "Truly I fear for you the punishment of a tremendous day" (*innī akhāfu ʿalaykum ʿadhāba yawm ʿaẓīm). The "tremendous day" is the Day of Judgment — or, in the immediate context, the specific punishment that God will send upon the ʿĀd. The warning is both eschatological (the final Day) and historical (the imminent destruction). The word "tremendous" (*ʿaẓīm) conveys the enormity of what is coming — a punishment so great that it cannot be described except by the superlative.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:136

> **They said, "It is the same to us whether you exhort us or whether you are not among those who exhort."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Indifference of the Arrogant**

The ʿĀd's response is the ultimate expression of arrogance: complete indifference. "It is the same to us whether you exhort us or whether you are not among those who exhort" (*sawāʾun ʿalaynā wa-ʿaẓta am kunta lam takun mina l-wāʿiẓīn). They are saying: your preaching makes no difference to us. Whether you continue or stop, we will not change. This is the state described in the Qur'an:

> *"Truly it is the same for the disbelievers whether thou warnest them or warnest them not; they do not believe."* (Qur'an 2:6)

**The Hardened Heart**

This indifference is the endpoint of a long process of hardening. The ʿĀd have heard the message, seen the evidence, and decided — not through reasoned evaluation but through the entrenchment of their arrogance — that nothing will change their behavior. The heart that reaches this state is sealed; it cannot be opened by argument or warning. Only punishment can penetrate it — and by then, it is too late.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:137

> **This is naught but the ethos of those of old,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Appeal to Normalcy**

The ʿĀd describe Hūd's message as "the ethos of those of old" (*hādhā illā khuluqu l-awwalīn). The word *khuluq* — "ethos" or "custom" — means the ways, the practices, the conventions of previous generations. They are saying: what you are calling us to is nothing new; it is just the old ways. This is the argument of normalcy: what we are doing is normal, what you are asking is unusual. The normal must be right; the unusual must be wrong.

**The Rejection of the New**

This is one of the most common forms of resistance to truth: the appeal to what is familiar and the rejection of what is unfamiliar. The ʿĀd do not say Hūd's message is wrong; they say it is old — implying that what is old is irrelevant, outmoded, or superseded by their current way of life. The argument is not logical but psychological: the comfort of the familiar versus the disruption of the new.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:138

> **and we shall not be punished."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Denial of Accountability**

The ʿĀd conclude with the most dangerous denial of all: "we shall not be punished" (*wa-mā naḥnu bi-muʿadhdhabīn). This is not merely a rejection of Hūd's message; it is a rejection of the entire concept of accountability. They do not believe they will be held responsible for their actions. This is the denial of the Hereafter — the conviction that death is the end and there is no judgment beyond it.

**The Theological Root of All Sin**

Al-Rāzī observes that the denial of punishment is the theological root of all sin. If there is no punishment, then there is no consequence for wrongdoing. If there is no consequence, then there is no reason to restrain oneself. The ʿĀd's belief that they would not be punished was the foundation of their entire corrupt behavior: their monumental arrogance, their tyrannical violence, their rejection of the prophet — all of it rested on the conviction that there would be no accounting. Remove that conviction, and the entire edifice of sin collapses.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:139

> **So they denied him, and We destroyed them. Truly in that is a sign, but most of them are not believers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Destruction of ʿĀd**

The narrative concludes with stark brevity: *"So they denied him, and We destroyed them"* (*fa-kadhdhabūhu fa-ahlaknāhum*). The destruction of ʿĀd is described in other sūrahs as a screaming wind (*rīḥ ṣarṣar*) that raged against them for seven nights and eight days (69:6–7), leaving them like hollow trunks of palm trees (69:7). The mighty builders who thought they would live forever were destroyed by a wind — the most ephemeral of natural forces. Their fortresses could not protect them; their monuments became their tombstones.

**The Sign Lies in the Manner of Destruction**

Al-Rāzī's treatment of ʿĀd centres on a people convinced that their permanence was secured by their construction: fortresses built as though they would last forever, monuments raised on every height for amusement. The wind answers that conviction at its exact point. What they had made solid was removed by air, the least substantial of elements, and the sūrah notes elsewhere that their dwellings were left standing as visible remains. The sign is therefore not merely that they were destroyed but that they were destroyed in a manner contradicting the specific claim their civilisation had made about itself.

**The First Half of the Refrain**

The verse then turns to the formula: *"Truly in that is a sign, but most of them are not believers."* The second half, naming God as the Mighty and the Merciful, follows in the next verse, and its placement after ʿĀd is pointed. This was the people who believed that might belongs to whoever can build the largest monument; the refrain's coming assertion that the Lord is the Mighty reassigns the word at the precise moment their civilisation has been removed.

**The Pattern Completed**

Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī read these narratives as one argument conducted across generations rather than as separate histories. The pattern here is complete: the messenger was known to his people, the demand was stated, the refusal was given, the destruction followed, and the sign remains inspectable. What the refrain supplies is the divine character that persists unchanged through the whole sequence — not contingent on acceptance of the message, and not diminished by its refusal.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:140

> **And truly thy Lord is, indeed, the Mighty, the Merciful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain After Hūd and the People of ʿĀd**

Hūd's narrative introduces something the earlier accounts had not: a people destroyed not by a moral scandal but by prosperity itself. ʿĀd built monuments on every height for amusement, constructed fortresses as though they would live forever, and exercised power with the cruelty of those who have never been checked. Their sin, in the sūrah's own diagnosis, was the pursuit of domination and worldly glory. The refrain that closes their story is therefore positioned after a very specific kind of failure.

This explains a small but significant variation in the wording. Where the refrain elsewhere reads *"And truly thy Lord is indeed the Mighty, the Merciful,"* here the source text inserts an additional particle — *"And truly thy Lord is, indeed, the Mighty, the Merciful"* — and the doubled emphasis lands on precisely the point ʿĀd got wrong. They had built a civilisation on the belief that might belongs to whoever can construct the largest monument. The refrain repeats the word *might* back to them with extra weight, reassigning it.

**Might Reassigned**

The argument the sūrah makes through this refrain is that ʿĀd mistook an effect for a cause. Their buildings were real; their power was real; both were effects of a might that was never theirs. Al-Rāzī develops the point in his treatment of the verse: the one who borrows strength and then claims it as his own has not merely made an error of attribution but has set himself against the lender. The destruction that follows is not an arbitrary punishment but the withdrawal of what was lent.

This is why the refrain pairs might with mercy rather than with justice. Had the second Name been *al-Qahhār* or *al-Muntaqim*, the verse would read as a threat. Paired with *al-Raḥīm*, it reads as an offer that was on the table right up to the destruction — the mercy was available to ʿĀd, and ʿĀd's tragedy is that it was offered to people who had convinced themselves they did not need it.

**The Refrain's Cumulative Weight**

By the fourth occurrence the refrain has ceased to be a coda and has become an argument. The reader has now seen the same sentence close four different histories: a tyrant drowned, a community of idolaters confuted, a people destroyed by their own contempt for the poor, and a civilisation levelled for its monuments. The consistency of the closing formula is what licenses the generalisation the sūrah is making — that these are not four stories but one story told four times.

For the sūrah's original audience this had an immediate edge. Makkah was a commercial city proud of its buildings, its caravans and its standing among the Arabs. The refrain, arriving after ʿĀd, asks the listener to recognise the resemblance. The sūrah never says so directly; the refrain does the work by being where it is.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:141

> **The Thamūd denied the messengers,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fifth Prophetic Narrative: Ṣāliḥ and Thamūd**

The narrative now turns to Ṣāliḥ and the tribe of Thamūd — the fifth prophetic account and the fourth using the standard pattern. The Thamūd were a later Arab tribe who succeeded the ʿĀd in the same general region. They are known for their rock-carved dwellings (15:80–84; 89:9) — houses hewn from mountains, a feat of engineering that demonstrated both their skill and their material attachment.

**The Pattern Continues**

The standard prophetic pattern is repeated: brother-prophet, call to taqwā, trustworthiness declared, reverence and obedience commanded, no material reward sought. The repetition by this point is deliberate: the reader is meant to notice that every prophet says the same thing, every people respond the same way, and the same God administers the same justice. The pattern is the message.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:142

> **when their brother Ṣāliḥ said unto them, “Will you not be reverent?”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fourth Narrative and Its Signature Question**

The sūrah now turns to Thamūd, and the opening line is identical in form to the openings given to Noah, Hūd and the others: their brother asks them whether they will not be reverent. The word for *brother* — *akh* — carries the weight al-Rāzī assigns to it elsewhere in the sūrah: he was one of them, of their lineage and their town, not an outsider importing a foreign doctrine. The question is asked by a man whose genealogy his audience could recite.

The interrogative form deserves notice. Ṣāliḥ does not open with an accusation or a command but with a question, and the commentators read the form as an invitation rather than a rebuke — the hearer is addressed as someone who might still answer. The same rhetorical strategy appears in the sūrah's treatment of Abraham and recurs wherever the Qur'an invites rather than indicts.

**Thamūd's Particular Situation**

What distinguishes Thamūd from ʿĀd in this sūrah is the precision of its material comfort. The verses that follow describe them as left secure amid gardens and springs, sown fields and date palms with ripe stalks, carving houses out of the mountains with practised skill. They are not a people of crude power like ʿĀd but of settled, cultivated, technically accomplished prosperity — and the question *Will you not be reverent?* is aimed at precisely that condition.

Al-Rāzī's observation on the following verses is that the question implies they were living as though they would be left in the earth indefinitely, as though no abode of recompense awaited them. Comfort produces a specific epistemic failure: it makes permanence feel like a fact rather than a hope. *Taqwā* is the counter-disposition, and the opening question is therefore the right one for this people — not *will you believe* but *will you be careful*, will you stop assuming.

**The Brother's Standing**

The insistence on brotherhood has a strategic function that the commentators mark. A prophet who is one of his people cannot be dismissed as ignorant of their ways, and he cannot claim to have arrived with no knowledge of what they value. Ṣāliḥ knew the houses they carved and the palms they cultivated; his question comes from inside that world, not from outside it. That is what makes the refusal that follows a refusal of a known person rather than of an abstraction.

The pattern matters for the sūrah's overall argument. Every prophet it names is sent to his own people in their own language and setting, and every refusal is therefore local, personal and specific. The sūrah is not describing humanity's abstract rejection of truth; it is describing neighbours rejecting a neighbour, seven times over.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:143

> **Truly I am a trustworthy messenger unto you.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Trust Claimed Among People Who Knew the Claimant**

Ṣāliḥ makes the same claim Noah and Hūd made, and in his case the claim is unusually concrete. Thamūd was a settled community with long institutional memory; Ṣāliḥ's character was not a matter of report but of direct acquaintance. The Qur'an elsewhere records their own admission of his prior standing — that they had hoped much of him before his message — which is precisely the admission that makes the *amīn* claim unanswerable.

Al-Rāzī's analysis of the term applies here with full force: a messenger is called trustworthy because the message is a deposit, and the question of his character is the question of whether he will deliver it unaltered. A man known for decades to have handled other people's affairs honestly has already answered that question in a domain where it could be tested. Ṣāliḥ's trustworthiness in commercial and social dealings is evidence bearing on his trustworthiness with revelation, and it is the only evidence of that kind available to any prophet.

**The Claim in the Face of a Coming Sign**

The placement of this verse immediately before the demand for obedience, and shortly before the sign of the she-camel, gives it a forward reference. Ṣāliḥ is establishing the credibility that the sign will then test. A sign given to a person already distrusted proves nothing, because its interpretation can be contested; a sign given to a man whose word has never failed carries the weight of that record with it.

This is why the sūrah's prophetic openings always place the trust claim before the demand and before the sign. The order is evidential. Credibility is established first, from public record; then the demand follows from it; then, where a sign is given, it lands on ground already prepared.

**Uniform Credentials Across Unequal Civilisations**

Noah addressed a people who mocked his followers for their poverty. Hūd addressed a people proud of fortresses and monuments. Ṣāliḥ addresses a people distinguished by agricultural wealth and rock-cut architecture. Three very different societies, and in each case the messenger's credential is the same: that he is known, local and honest. Nothing about the credential is calibrated to the audience's sophistication.

The uniformity is the sūrah's point. Al-Rāzī notes that the plural *messengers* is used even where a single prophet is discussed, because the path to recognising a messenger is universal and does not change with historical circumstance. The verse is a narrative instance of that principle: whatever the civilisation, the test of a messenger is his character among people who can verify it.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:144

> **So reverence God and obey me.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Demand, Restated Without Alteration**

The formula's third line returns unchanged, and its unchanging character is itself informative. Reverence for God, then obedience to the messenger: the same order, the same dependency, the same limit on the messenger's authority that al-Rāzī identifies when he notes that the call to reverence precedes the call to obey because the second rests on the first. Ṣāliḥ is not asking for allegiance to himself. He is asking for the obedience that his message, and nothing else, authorises.

In Thamūd's context the demand has a specific shape. The commands that follow concern the she-camel — that she be left to drink, that she not be harmed — and the obedience required is obedience in a matter that costs them something tangible. A shared water source in an arid settlement is not a symbolic concession. The verse therefore asks for the kind of obedience that can be verified, in a domain where non-compliance is immediately visible.

**Reverence Against Settled Comfort**

*Taqwā* in this setting is the disposition of a person who does not take his security for granted. Thamūd had gardens, springs, sown fields and houses carved from rock — the material conditions in which the sense of permanence is hardest to dislodge. The command to revere God is, in effect, a command to keep an uncertainty in view: that the water may not flow, that the rock may not hold, that the settlement is contingent.

Al-Rāzī's reading of the adjacent verses is that Thamūd lived as though no abode of recompense existed. Reverence is the corrective to that specific error, which is why the sūrah opens every prophet's mission with it. It is not a demand for emotional intensity but for a sustained, deliberate awareness that the present arrangement of things is neither permanent nor self-authorising.

**Obedience's Boundary**

Al-Qurṭubī's observation that the people were to obey the messenger when he enjoined faith applies here with equal force, and the she-camel episode illustrates the boundary. Ṣāliḥ's authority extends to what God has commanded through him; it does not extend to Thamūd's property, its internal arrangements or its choices beyond that. When the camel is hamstrung, the transgression is against a divine command conveyed by the messenger, not against the messenger's personal authority.

That distinction is what keeps the sūrah's account of obedience from becoming an account of clerical power. The messenger is obeyed because he is a conduit; when the conduit is violated, the violation runs upward. The verse states the arrangement in six words and leaves the limit intact.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:145

> **And I ask not of you any reward for it; my reward lies only with the Lord of the worlds.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refusal Where It Costs Most**

Of the four prophets given this opening, Ṣāliḥ's refusal of reward falls in the community where it is hardest to interpret as mere rhetoric. Thamūd was an agricultural and commercial society; wealth there was concrete, measured in water, grain and livestock. A man who declined payment in such a setting was declining something with a known market value, in front of people who could price it.

That concreteness is what makes the refusal evidentially useful. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī treat the messengers' refusal of enrichment as a standing theme, and its force is always the same: the messenger removes himself from the transaction so that the message stands on its own. In a society that understood exchange precisely, the removal was unmistakable.

**Reward Relocated Beyond the Settlement**

*My reward lies only with the Lord of the worlds* does what the phrase does in the parallel openings — it moves the payment out of the audience's control. Al-Rāzī's wide reading of *al-ʿālamīn* as every level of created existence means that the reward Ṣāliḥ expects comes from an order Thamūd cannot observe, tax or withhold. The practical effect is that no threat they make can touch his compensation, and no favour they offer can alter his message.

This is the structural reason the refusal is placed where it is. A messenger paid by his hearers is, in the last analysis, their employee, and employees modulate their message. A messenger paid elsewhere is not, and the sūrah makes sure the hearers hear that before the sign arrives.

**The Completed Opening**

With this verse the four-line formula is complete for the fourth time: the question about reverence, the claim of trustworthiness, the demand for reverence and obedience, and the refusal of payment. The sūrah will give the same opening to Lot and to Shuʿayb, and by then the pattern is unmistakable — a fixed grammar of prophetic mission, repeated across societies with nothing in common but the refusal.

The grammar is worth stating as an argument, because that is how the sūrah uses it. Who is speaking? A man of known honesty, one of you. What does he want? Your reverence for God and obedience to a message that is not his. What does it cost you to test him? Nothing he has asked you to pay. Every element is designed so that the only remaining reason to refuse is a reason the hearer would rather not state aloud.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:146

> **Will you be left among that which is here, secure,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Warning Against Complacency**

Ṣāliḥ challenges the Thamūd with a pointed question: "Will you be left among that which is here, secure?" (*a-tutrakūna fīmā hāhunā āminīn). The question exposes the Thamūd's fundamental error: they believe they will be left in peace — that their material comfort will continue indefinitely, that there will be no reckoning, no change, no consequence. The question is rhetorical: the answer is no. You will not be left in security forever.

**The Catalogue of Blessings**

Ṣāliḥ enumerates the Thamūd's blessings: gardens, springs, sown fields, date palms with ripe stalks. The description is vivid and abundant — a civilization at the height of its agricultural prosperity. The "ripe stalks" (*haḍīm) of the date palms suggest fruit so heavy that the stalks nearly snap under the weight — an image of abundance pushed to its limit. The Thamūd have every material blessing; the question is whether they are grateful or merely complacent.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:147

> **amid gardens and springs,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Same Inventory, a Different People**

Ṣāliḥ's question to Thamūd begins with the same catalogue Hūd had used: gardens, springs, sown fields, date palms. The sūrah's reuse of the inventory across two narratives is a deliberate structural echo, and it makes a comparative claim. ʿĀd and Thamūd were different peoples in different places with different skills — one of monuments, one of rock-cut houses and agriculture — and they failed in the same way for the same reason.

Al-Qurṭubī's principle governs both cases: the things listed were made subservient and useful to human beings, and so God should be thanked for them. The repetition of the principle across narratives is what converts it from an observation about one people into a statement about how abundance functions generally.

**The Interrogative Frame**

The verses are cast as a question — *Will you be left secure in that which is here?* — and the interrogative form is where the argument lives. Ṣāliḥ does not assert that Thamūd will be destroyed; he asks whether they suppose they will be left as they are, and leaves the supposition exposed for them to examine. Al-Rāzī reads the question as indicating that they were living as if they expected to be left on the earth, as if no abode of recompense awaited them.

That is a precise psychological claim. The error is not disbelief in a future reckoning as a proposition but the practical assumption, embedded in daily conduct, that the present arrangement will continue. The question is designed to surface an assumption the hearers have not stated and may not know they hold.

**Gardens and Springs as the Ground of Security**

Naming gardens and springs first, before the fields and palms, establishes the basis of the settlement's confidence: water and cultivated land. Everything else in the inventory depends on these. By identifying them explicitly, the verse identifies exactly what the community is trusting — and therefore exactly what it would lose if the trust were misplaced.

The rhetorical strategy is economical. Rather than threatening, Ṣāliḥ lists what they value and asks whether they have considered its contingency. The question does its work by making the listener perform the calculation himself, which is why the sūrah gives the inventory in the form of a query rather than a warning.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:148

> **sown fields, date palms with ripe stalks?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Date Palm Singled Out**

The inventory closes on a detail specific enough to be a portrait rather than a formula. Al-Rāzī notes that although the previous verse mentions gardens generally, the explicit mention of date palms is taken by some to indicate the superiority of the date palm over other fruit — a judgement that reflects both its nutritional centrality and its economic value in the societies the Qur'an addresses.

The specification has an argumentative function as well. A general reference to gardens could be dismissed as conventional religious rhetoric. A reference to date palms with ripe stalks describes something the hearers could see from where they stood, in a season they could name. The argument is anchored in observable fact.

***Haḍīm*: the Weight of Ripeness**

The word rendered *ripe* is *haḍīm*, and the commentators give two readings. Al-Ṭabarī records that some understand it to mean slender, while others take it to mean that the fragile stalks are crushed or crumbled, so that they nearly snap under the weight of the ripe fruits piled one upon another. The second reading is the more vivid and the more purposeful.

It describes abundance at the precise point where it becomes a strain on the thing bearing it. The stalk is not merely loaded but deformed by its load — bending, compressing, near to breaking. As an image inside an argument about prosperity, it is exact: it shows a condition that is simultaneously maximum yield and maximum stress, and it asks the hearer whether he has mistaken the second for the first.

**The Question Completed**

With this verse the interrogative that began at 146 is complete, and its full force is available. *Will you be left secure amid gardens and springs, sown fields, date palms with ripe stalks?* Al-Rāzī's reading is that the question exposes a people living as though they expected to be left on the earth indefinitely, as though there were no abode of recompense.

The sūrah's answer arrives within a few verses: the punishment seized them, and the refrain closed the narrative. The inventory was not scene-setting but evidence. It established that Thamūd had everything its world counted as security, that the security was borrowed, and that the community had taken the borrowing for ownership — which is the same failure ʿĀd made, stated in a different material vocabulary.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:149

> **And you skillfully hew dwellings in the mountains.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Pride in Craftsmanship**

The Thamūd are characterized by their architectural achievement: "you skillfully hew dwellings in the mountains" (*wa-tanḥitūna mina l-jibāli buyūtan fārihīn). The word *fāriḥīn* — "skillfully" — also carries the meaning of "exulting" or "exuberantly" — suggesting that the Thamūd carved their dwellings with pride and joy. Their architecture was not merely functional; it was an expression of their confidence in their own permanence.

**Al-Rāzī's Analysis**

Al-Rāzī observes the contrast between the two tribes in this sūrah: the ʿĀd were overcome by their desire for domination and glory, while the Thamūd were seduced by the material comforts of the world. The ʿĀd represent the vices of the irascible soul (anger, pride, aggression); the Thamūd represent the vices of the concupiscent soul (attachment to comfort, materialism, complacency). Between them, the sūrah describes the two main categories of human failing: the excess of aggression and the excess of attachment.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:150

> **So reverence God and obey me.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Warning Against the Prodigal**

Ṣāliḥ warns the Thamūd: "obey not the commands of the prodigal" (*wa-lā tuṭīʿūna amra l-musrifīn). The "prodigal" (*musrifīn) are those who exceed bounds, who waste, who go to excess. The Thamūd's leaders — the notables who benefited from the status quo — are the "prodigal" whose commands Ṣāliḥ warns against following. Their leadership leads to "corruption upon the earth" (*fasād fi l-arḍ) and a failure to "set matters aright" (*yuṣliḥūn).

**The Two Opposing Concepts: Corruption and Rectification**

The verse presents two opposing concepts: *fasād* (corruption) and *iṣlāḥ* (setting aright). Corruption is the work of the prodigal — those who exceed bounds and damage the social and moral order. Setting aright is the alternative — the work of those who repair, restore, and maintain justice. The prophetic message always involves this choice: follow the corrupt and perpetuate corruption, or follow the prophet and participate in rectification.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:151

> **And obey not the commands of the prodigal,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Command About Whom to Listen To**

Ṣāliḥ's instruction is unusual in form: it does not prohibit an act but prohibits an obedience. The vice named is *isrāf*, prodigality or wastefulness, and the command is not *do not waste* but *do not obey those who waste*. The shift places the danger in the social transmission of a disposition rather than in the disposition alone.

Al-Rāzī's gloss makes the mechanism explicit: the warning against being prodigal means that one should not be seduced by those who seek the things of this world excessively, and should seek instead contentment. The word *seduced* is the operative one. Prodigality spreads not by argument but by example — by the visible behaviour of people whose standing makes their conduct look like success.

**Prodigality in Qur'anic Usage**

The commentators locate this verse within a sustained Qur'anic treatment of wastefulness, pointing to 3:147, 7:80–81, 25:67 and especially 10:12. The consistency across those passages is that *isrāf* is treated not as a private failing of budgeting but as a distortion of desire — the pursuit of more than the situation requires, undertaken without reference to an end.

In Thamūd's context the vice has a specific social shape. This was a community of carved houses and cultivated wealth whose elites set the standard of consumption. To obey the commands of the prodigal is to accept that standard as normative, and once accepted it becomes unexaminable: the measure of enough is set by those who have decided there is none.

**Why the Command Is Framed as Disobedience**

The verse pairs this prohibition with the positive command in verse 144 — *reverence God and obey me* — and the pairing defines a choice between two authorities. Either the community's practice is set by the messenger, or it is set by its most extravagant members. There is no third option in which consumption standards are simply neutral.

That is the verse's real claim, and it is a social rather than a personal one.
