Every community takes its norms of sufficiency from somewhere, and the sūrah insists that the choice of source is a moral decision. Obedience to the prodigal is not a matter of personal taste; it is the adoption of a standard that the messenger has been sent to correct.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:152

> **who work corruption upon the earth, and do not set matters aright.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

***Fasād* and *Iṣlāḥ* as a Paired Opposition**

The verse completes Ṣāliḥ's warning by naming both poles: those who work corruption upon the earth — *fasād* — and the negation of setting matters aright — *iṣlāḥ*. The commentators direct the reader to 2:220 for the Qur'an's treatment of this pairing, and the pairing is structural rather than incidental. *Fasād* is not merely harm; it is the undoing of an order that was capable of being sound, and *iṣlāḥ* is the active maintenance of that order.

The double formulation therefore excludes a middle position. One does not refrain from corruption and thereby satisfy the command; the verse requires positive setting-aright as well. Al-Rāzī's reading of the surrounding verses connects this directly to contentment — *qanāʿah* — as the disposition that makes restraint possible.

**Corruption at Increasing Scale**

The commentators extend the reference beyond commercial fraud. The acts described as behaving wickedly upon the earth and working corruption are usually understood to include highway robbery and piracy, raiding, and the destruction of crops. Read alongside the injunctions against short measure in the following verses, a sequence emerges: the dishonest scale, the seized caravan, the destroyed field.

That sequence is the sūrah's social analysis. These are not unrelated crimes but one appetite operating with diminishing restraint. The appetite begins where the other party is present and can be shorted, and ends where the other party is absent and can simply be robbed. Naming them together identifies a trajectory rather than a list.

**The Verse as Diagnosis of a Whole Community**

Ṣāliḥ's closing clause is spoken to a people whose material achievements the sūrah has just catalogued in admiring detail. The gardens, springs and rock-cut houses are real accomplishments; the charge is that the same community works corruption and does not set matters aright. Technical capacity and moral order are treated as independent variables.

This is the sharpest social observation in the narrative. A civilisation can be highly competent at producing and entirely disordered at distributing, and its competence will make the disorder more costly rather than less. The sūrah's sequence — inventory, question, command, refusal, destruction — is an argument that the second term cannot substitute for the first.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:153

> **They said, "You are but one of the bewitched."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Accusation of Sorcery**

The Thamūd's response follows the pattern of every disbelieving people: they accuse the prophet of being "bewitched" (*musahhar*). The accusation implies that Ṣāliḥ's message is not the product of divine revelation but of supernatural deception — that he is under the influence of sorcery or jinn. This is the same accusation leveled against Moses and against the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ.

**The Strategy of Reattribution**

The accusation of sorcery is a strategy of reattribution: rather than acknowledging the divine origin of the prophet's message, the disbelievers attribute it to a different supernatural source — one that is illegitimate, dangerous, or insane. By recharacterizing the prophet as "bewitched," they can dismiss everything he says without having to engage with its substance.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:154

> **You are naught but a human being like us; so bring a sign, if you are among the truthful."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Objection to the Prophet's Humanity**

The Thamūd add a second objection: "You are naught but a human being like us" (*mā anta illā basharun mithlunā). This objection — that the prophet is merely a human being, not an angel or a superhuman figure — is one of the most common in the Qur'an (11:27; 14:10; 21:3; 23:24). The disbelievers' logic: if the message is divine, its bearer should be more than human. The fact that he eats food, walks in markets, and shares the human condition is, in their view, evidence against his claims.

**The Qur'anic Response**

The Qur'an's response to this objection is consistent: the prophets are human beings precisely because they are sent to human beings. An angel messenger would be unintelligible to humans; a human messenger can communicate in human language, model human behavior, and demonstrate that the divine message is livable in a human body. The humanity of the prophet is not a deficiency; it is a requirement of the mission.

**The Demand for a Sign**

The Thamūd demand a sign — a miracle to authenticate Ṣāliḥ's claims. The demand is not inherently unreasonable; the prophets are sent with signs. But the Thamūd's demand comes with an implicit condition: we will only believe if you produce what we ask for, in the way we ask for it. This is not genuine openness to evidence; it is a challenge designed to maintain control over the terms of engagement.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:155

> **He said, "This is a she-camel; she shall have drink and you shall drink on an appointed day."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sign of the She-Camel**

Ṣāliḥ produces the sign: a she-camel, miraculously emerging from a rock. The she-camel is described in detail in other sūrahs (7:73; 11:64; 91:13). She is not an ordinary camel; she is a miracle — a sign from God that authenticates Ṣāliḥ's prophethood. The specific test is that the she-camel has a right to water on an appointed day — she and the people alternate drinking days, each taking their turn.

**The Symbolism of the She-Camel**

The she-camel is a sign that operates on multiple levels. First, it is a miracle of creation — a camel emerging from solid rock demonstrates God's power over matter. Second, it is a test of obedience — the people must respect the she-camel's right to water, sharing the resource with a creature that is not theirs. Third, it is a symbol of divine provision — God can provide water in ways the people do not expect. The she-camel is a sign that requires not just observation but obedience.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:156

> **And touch her not with harm, lest you be taken by the punishment of a tremendous day."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Warning**

Ṣāliḥ warns the Thamūd: "touch her not with harm" (*wa-lā tamassūhā bi-sūʾin). The warning is specific: do not harm the she-camel. The consequence is equally specific: "lest you be taken by the punishment of a tremendous day." The punishment for harming the she-camel is not a human-administered penalty but a divine one — the "tremendous day" of God's wrath.

**The Test of Obedience**

The she-camel is a test of the Thamūd's willingness to obey a specific divine command. The command is simple: do not touch the she-camel with harm. The simplicity is deliberate: God is testing whether the Thamūd can obey even a straightforward, specific command. If they cannot obey this, they cannot be trusted with anything greater.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:157

> **But they hamstrung her, and then became remorseful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Crime**

Despite the warning, the Thamūd hamstring the she-camel — kill her by cutting her leg tendons. The act is one of supreme defiance: they know who she is, they know the warning, and they kill her anyway. The hamstringming is a deliberate, public act of rebellion — designed to demonstrate their rejection of Ṣāliḥ's authority and their contempt for God's sign.

**The Remorse That Comes Too Late**

The verse adds a devastating detail: "and then became remorseful" (*fa-aṣbahū nādimīn). After the act, they felt remorse — but the remorse came after the deed, not before. Remorse after the point of no return is not repentance; it is the anguish of consequences already set in motion. The Thamūd regretted their action when they realized what it would cost them — not when they were deciding whether to do it. This is the pattern of every sin committed against better judgment: the regret arrives only after the irreversible has been done.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:158

> **So the punishment seized them. Truly in that is a sign, but most of them are not believers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Punishment**

The punishment comes swiftly. Other sūrahs describe it as a "cry" (*ṣayḥah*) — a single blast from the sky that destroys the entire tribe (11:67; 54:31). The Thamūd who hewed dwellings in the mountains found that their mountain homes became their tombs. The punishment is described as "seizing" them — the word *akhadhahum* implies a sudden, overpowering grasp from which there is no escape.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:159

> **And truly thy Lord is the Mighty, the Merciful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain After Ṣāliḥ**

The refrain concludes the Thamūd narrative: God is the Mighty, the Merciful. The Mighty who seized the Thamūd for their crime; the Merciful who sent Ṣāliḥ to warn them before the punishment came. Both attributes are manifest in the story: the might that punished and the mercy that preceded the punishment with warning.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:160

> **The people of Lot denied the messengers,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sixth Prophetic Narrative: Lot**

The narrative of Lot follows the same standard pattern as the preceding prophets: his people deny the messengers; he calls them to reverence; he declares his trustworthiness; he commands obedience to God and to himself; he disclaims any desire for reward. The consistency of this pattern across all six prophetic narratives in the sūrah reinforces the central thesis: the message was always the same, the rejection always followed the same logic, and the outcome always confirmed the same truth.

**Lot Among His People**

Lot is described as "their brother" — a member of the community he is sent to reform. Historically, Lot was Abraham's nephew (or, in some traditions, a fellow believer who separated from Abraham's company). He was sent to the cities of the plain — cities whose names are preserved in Biblical tradition (Sodom and Gomorrah) but which the Qur'an refers to simply as "the overthrown cities" or "the town."

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:161

> **when their brother Lot said unto them, “Will you not be reverent?”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Brother Who Was Not a Kinsman**

The sūrah calls Lot *their brother*, and the phrase is doing careful work. Lot was not ethnically of that people; the tradition places him as Abraham's nephew, sent to the cities and dwelling among them. The commentators resolve the description in the way the Qur'an generally uses it: *brother* marks the man who lives inside a community, speaks its language and shares its daily life, whether or not the genealogy runs through it. He is one of them in the only sense that matters for a messenger — he cannot be dismissed as a foreigner passing judgement from a distance.

Al-Rāzī's treatment of the term elsewhere in the sūrah establishes the point: *brother* means he was known to them. For Lot the acquaintance was domestic. He lived in their town, married among them, and his family's position is central to the narrative that follows. The question he opens with is asked from inside a household, not from outside a border.

**The Question Before the Indictment**

As with the other prophets, the opening is interrogative: *Will you not be reverent?* The form matters more here than elsewhere in the sūrah, because what follows is the harshest moral charge the chapter brings. Beginning with an invitation rather than an accusation is a deliberate ordering — the hearer is first addressed as someone capable of answering, and only then confronted with what he has done. Al-Ṭabarī reads the *taqwā* question in these openings as *"Will you not be mindful of disobedience toward Me?"*, which frames the coming charge as a failure of attention before it is a failure of desire.

That framing is not a softening. It identifies the mechanism. A community that has normalised a transgression has usually done so by ceasing to notice it, and the question is aimed at the noticing rather than at the act.

***Taqwā* in a Community Without Restraint**

The verses that follow describe a people who approached men instead of women, who transgressed knowingly, and whose assembly responded to the prophet by demanding his expulsion for being a man who sought purity. The last detail is diagnostic: the community did not merely permit the transgression but treated restraint as the deviance. In such a setting *taqwā* — the guarded, deliberate attention to consequences — has no social support at all.

This is why the refrain that closes the narrative lands where it does. The sūrah records that the community was destroyed and that Lot and his family were saved, except his wife among those who remained behind, and then it says that the Lord is the Mighty, the Merciful. The mercy was extended through a prophet living in the town, repeatedly, and the destruction followed the refusal of that extension.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:162

> **Truly I am a trustworthy messenger unto you,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Trust Claimed by a Resident**

Lot's claim to trustworthiness differs in kind from Hūd's or Ṣāliḥ's. Those prophets appealed to a lifetime among their own kin. Lot appeals to years of residence among a people not his own — the harder case, since a resident alien has fewer structural protections and more incentive to accommodate. That he did not accommodate is the substance of the claim.

The root *ʾ-m-n* carries the sense of a deposit safely kept. A messenger is *amīn* because the message is entrusted to him, and al-Rāzī's point applies here exactly: the question of the messenger's character is the question of whether he will deliver the deposit unaltered. Lot's years in the town are the evidence, and they are evidence precisely because he had every worldly reason to soften what he said.

**The Claim Tested by Isolation**

What makes this instance of the formula distinctive is how completely it was vindicated by events. Lot's household, apart from his family, contained no believers; the Qur'an records that he found not more than one house of those who submitted. A messenger whose message converted almost nobody, and who nonetheless did not modify it, has demonstrated trustworthiness in the strongest available form — the deposit was delivered intact into a situation where delivery brought him no advantage whatever.

The sūrah's account therefore uses the *amīn* claim retrospectively. It is stated at the opening as a claim; by the narrative's end it has been tested against total rejection and found accurate. The reader who reaches the refrain has seen the claim verified not by success but by persistence.

**Against the Charge of Impurity**

The assembly's eventual response to Lot was to expel him and his household *because they are people who seek to be pure*. The accusation inverts the trust claim: what Lot offers as fidelity to a deposit, his opponents describe as a defect of disposition. The verse's placement before that exchange is deliberate — the sūrah lets the messenger state his credential, then lets the community answer it with a slur, and leaves the reader to compare the two.

Al-Qurṭubī notes the recurring Qur'anic pattern in which a community's charge against its prophet reveals the community rather than the prophet. Here the charge is that purity itself is objectionable, which is as complete a statement of moral inversion as the sūrah anywhere makes.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:163

> **so reverence God and obey me.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Demand in a Case Where Obedience Is Costly**

The third line of the formula is unchanged, and the order it encodes is unchanged with it: reverence for God first, obedience to the messenger second, the second resting on the first. What differs in Lot's case is what obedience would have required. It was not a ritual adjustment or a commercial reform but the abandonment of a practice the community had made constitutive of its identity — and it required that from people who had already decided the practice was normal.

Al-Rāzī's principle that the call to reverence precedes the call to obey because the second depends on the first is doing real work here. Lot could not have sustained a demand for obedience on his own authority against an entire town. The demand held only because it pointed beyond him.

**Reverence as the Absent Disposition**

*Taqwā* in this narrative is best understood as the disposition the community had lost: the capacity to hold back from an act because of what it leads to. The verses describe the transgression as undertaken knowingly, which rules out ignorance as the mechanism. What was missing was the guard — the internal check that awareness of consequences normally installs.

The Qur'anic account pairs this with a second failure of *taqwā*, on the part of Lot's wife, who was among those who remained behind. The sūrah thus shows the same disposition absent in two very different people: a community that made transgression public policy, and an individual inside the prophet's own household who did not share his commitment. The demand for reverence is addressed to both, and its refusal takes both forms.

**Obedience's Limit Preserved**

Al-Qurṭubī's observation that the people were to obey the messenger when he enjoined faith applies here with unusual clarity, because Lot's command was narrow. He did not ask for political authority, judicial office or control of the town's affairs; he asked that a specific practice cease. The refusal was therefore not a refusal of governance but of a single, identifiable restraint — which is what makes the community's response, demanding the prophet's expulsion rather than debating the practice, so revealing.

The verse keeps the limit visible. Obedience is owed to the message; where the message ends, the claim ends. Lot never asserts more, and the sūrah never grants him more.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:164

> **And I ask not of you any reward for it; my reward lies only with the Lord of the worlds.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Refusal in a Hostile Town**

The fourth line closes Lot's opening, and in his case it answers an accusation that was never voiced. A man who persists in condemning a community's central practice, while living among them and depending on them, invites the suspicion that he is negotiating — that his severity is a bid for status, payment or influence. The refusal forecloses the reading before it can be offered.

Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī both treat the messengers' refusal of enrichment as evidence rather than as virtue. The logic is transactional and would have been familiar to any hearer: a paid advocate is interested in his own argument, and an unpaid one is not. Lot's isolation makes the point sharper, since he had no constituency to monetise.

***Rabb al-ʿĀlamīn* Against a Closed Society**

*The Lord of the worlds* relocates the reward outside the town's jurisdiction entirely. Al-Rāzī's wide reading of *al-ʿālamīn* — every level of created existence — means that the compensation Lot expects cannot be granted, withheld or audited by the people he is addressing. For a resident alien in a hostile settlement, that relocation is not a theological abstraction but the only possible basis for independence.

It also explains his endurance. A man whose reward is deferred to an order his opponents cannot touch has nothing they can threaten. The subsequent expulsion decree, which they issue in the very next verses, has no leverage over him for exactly this reason.

**The Opening Complete Before the Destruction**

With this verse Lot's four-line opening is complete: the question about reverence, the claim of trustworthiness, the demand for reverence and obedience, and the refusal of payment. The narrative then moves quickly to the community's answer, the angels' arrival, the expulsion of Lot's household in the night, and the overturning of the cities with the rain of the warned.

The speed is itself an argument. The sūrah spends four verses establishing that the messenger was known, honest, disinterested and moderate in his demand, and then records the destruction in two. The disproportion tells the reader where the weight of responsibility lies. The refrain that follows — *the Mighty, the Merciful* — closes a case in which mercy had been extended at length and refused completely.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:165

> **Among all creatures do you come unto males,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Unique Sin**

Lot's people are charged with a sin not previously attributed to any other community in the Qur'an: "Among all creatures do you come unto males" (*a-taʾtūna l-dhukrāna mina l-ʿālamīn). The phrase "among all creatures" emphasizes the unprecedented nature of their behavior: no nation before them had institutionalized same-sex relations as a matter of public practice. The word *taʾtūna* — "you come unto" — is deliberately indirect, describing the act as an approach or a coming-to, while making clear the object: males.

**The Violation of Natural Order**

Al-Rāzī and other commentators understand this verse as describing a violation of the natural order that God established in creation — the complementarity of male and female, designed for reproduction, companionship, and the continuation of the human species. By abandoning this order, Lot's people were not merely committing individual sins but overturning the created structure of human sexuality.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:166

> **leaving your spouses your Lord created for you? Nay, but you are a transgressing people."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Abandonment of the Natural Partner**

Lot's challenge continues: "leaving your spouses your Lord created for you?" (*wa-tadharūna mā khalaqa lakum rabbukum min azwājikum). The spouses (*azwāj*) are what God "created for you" — the natural partners designed for human beings. By abandoning these natural partners and pursuing same-sex relations, Lot's people are rejecting God's creation in favor of their own desires.

**"You Are a Transgressing People"**

Lot concludes with a verdict: "Nay, but you are a transgressing people" (*bal antum qawmun ʿādūn). The word *ʿādūn* — "transgressing" — means exceeding bounds, going beyond limits. The people have transgressed not only moral boundaries but the limits of their own nature — acting in ways that contradict the design embedded in their very creation.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:167

> **They said, "If you cease not, O Lot, you will be among those who are expelled."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Threat of Expulsion**

Lot's people respond with a threat identical in structure to Noah's: "If you cease not, O Lot, you will be among those who are expelled" (*la-in lam tantahi yā lūṭu la-takūnanna mina l-mukhrājīn). The threat is of expulsion — removal from their community, exile from their city. This is the response of a society that has normalized deviation: the one who objects to the norm is not engaged with; he is expelled. The reformer is treated as the problem, not the corruption he is exposing.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:168

> **He said, "Truly I am of those who detest what you do."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Lot's Moral Clarity**

Lot declares: "Truly I am of those who detest what you do" (*innī li-ʿamalikum mina l-qālīn). The word *qālīn* — "detesters" — conveys strong aversion, repugnance, rejection. Lot is not merely disapproving; he is deeply repulsed by what his people are doing. His moral instinct is clear and unclouded: this behavior is an abomination.

**The Importance of Moral Instinct**

Lot's declaration teaches the importance of maintaining a clear moral instinct. The people have normalized their behavior to the point where it seems ordinary to them. Lot refuses to accept the normalization; he maintains the instinctive revulsion that the behavior deserves. This is a lesson for every age: when society normalizes what was previously recognized as wrong, the moral voice must continue to express the revulsion — not out of hatred for the people, but out of commitment to the truth about the act.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:169

> **My Lord! Deliver me and my family from what they do."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prayer for Deliverance**

Lot turns to God with a prayer: "My Lord! Deliver me and my family from what they do" (*rabbi najjinī wa-ahlī mimmā yaʿmalūn). The prayer is both personal ("deliver me") and communal ("and my family"). Lot is asking to be saved from the moral environment of his people — not just from physical harm but from the contagion of their corruption.

**The Prayer for the Family**

The inclusion of "my family" in the prayer is significant: Lot is concerned not only for himself but for his household. He wants his family to be protected from the influence and the consequences of the people's behavior. This is the prayer of every righteous parent in a corrupt society: protect my children from the moral environment that surrounds them.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:170

> **So We delivered him and his family all together,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Collective Deliverance**

God answers the prayer: "So We delivered him and his family all together" (*fa-njaynāhu wa-ahlahu ajmaʿīn). The deliverance is complete: Lot and his entire household are saved. The word *ajmaʿīn* — "all together" — emphasizes totality. This is the pattern of divine deliverance: when God saves, He saves completely.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:171

> **save for an old woman among those who lagged behind.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Exception: Lot's Wife**

One person is excluded from the deliverance: "an old woman among those who lagged behind" (*illā ʿajūzan fī l-ghābirīn). This is Lot's wife, who remained loyal to the people of the city and did not join Lot in departure. The word *ghābirīn* — "those who lagged behind" — implies that she stayed by choice, aligned with the people rather than with her husband's mission.

**The Lesson About Family and Faith**

The exception demonstrates that family ties do not guarantee salvation. Lot was a prophet; his wife was still condemned. Blood relationship to a righteous person does not automatically confer righteousness. Each person is accountable for their own choices. This is a comfort to every righteous person whose family members do not share their faith: your righteousness does not save them; their salvation depends on their own choices.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:172

> **Then We destroyed the others.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Destruction by Rain**

The remaining people — those who were not saved — are destroyed by a rain. Other sūrahs describe this as a rain of stones of baked clay (11:82; 15:74), a rain of fire and sulfur. The rain is not a gentle rainfall but a devastating barrage — a punishment that comes from the sky as thoroughly as the sin came from the violation of the natural order.

**"Evil Was the Rain of the Warned"**

The verse concludes with a judgment on the punishment itself: "Evil was the rain of the warned!" (*biʾsa maṭaru l-mun dharīn). The word "evil" (*biʾsa) here does not mean morally wrong — the punishment is just — but terrible, dreadful, severe. The rain that fell upon Lot's people was a rain of punishment, and its nature was terrifying. The "warned" refers to the people who were warned by Lot but did not heed the warning.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:173

> **And We rained a rain upon them. Evil was the rain of the warned!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Destruction in Two Sentences**

After fourteen verses of narrative, the sūrah disposes of Lot's people in a single line and then comments on it. The economy is deliberate. The sūrah has already spent its length on the preaching, the refusal and the demand for expulsion; the destruction is recorded without elaboration, and the only expansion given is the exclamation about the rain.

The commentators connect this rain to the parallel reference in Sūrah al-Furqān — *"And indeed they passed by the town upon which fell the evil rain"* (25:40) — and note the description of the rain as one of fire and sulfur, with al-Ṭabarī citing the corresponding biblical account. What the Qur'anic narration adds is not detail but emphasis on a single word: *warned*.

**The Weight of *al-Mundharīn***

*Evil was the rain of the warned* makes the warning the defining feature of the event. The rain was not evil because it was severe; severe rain falls on the un-warned constantly. It was evil because it fell on people who had been told in advance, repeatedly, by a man living in their town, and who had responded by demanding his expulsion for seeking purity.

This connects directly to the sūrah's wider principle, stated at verses 208–9 and echoed at 15:4, 17:15, 20:134 and 28:59: that God does not destroy a people without first sending warners. The rain of the warned is the corollary of that principle — where the warning has been given and refused, the destruction carries a weight it would not otherwise carry.

**The Exclamation as Reader's Instruction**

The sentence's second half is not narrative but evaluation, and it is addressed to the reader rather than to the characters. The sūrah tells the audience how to feel about the event it has just reported, which is unusual and purposeful: it forecloses the reading in which the destruction is mere catastrophe, natural disaster or arbitrary violence.

The exclamation also sets up the refrain that follows immediately. *Evil was the rain of the warned!* is followed by *And truly thy Lord is indeed the Mighty, the Merciful.* The juxtaposition is the sūrah's whole theology of judgement in two lines — the punishment was deserved because the mercy was extended first, and the mercy was extended first because it is who God is.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:174

> **Truly in that is a sign, but most of them are not believers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain After Lot**

Lot's narrative closes with the first half of the formula: *"Truly in that is a sign, but most of them are not believers."* The second half follows in the next verse. Of the sūrah's prophetic accounts this is the shortest and the harshest, and the refrain arrives immediately after a destruction described in a single line — *"And We rained a rain upon them. Evil was the rain of the warned!"*

**What Makes This Case a Sign**

The account is not primarily a record of punishment but of a warning that was given at length and refused completely. Lot lived inside the town, was known to it, asked no payment, and made a single narrow demand. The community's answer was not to argue the demand but to demand his expulsion for seeking purity. Al-Qurṭubī notes the recurring Qur'anic pattern in which a community's accusation reveals the accusers, and here the accusation — that purity itself is objectionable — is as complete a statement of moral inversion as the sūrah anywhere makes.

**The Institutional Dimension**

The commentators draw out that the transgression had become public policy rather than private failing: the assembly, not merely individuals, responded to the prophet. This is what gives the narrative its particular weight. A society can contain individual wrongdoing without becoming corrupt; it becomes corrupt when the wrongdoing acquires institutional defence and treats restraint as the deviance. The sign is the point at which that inversion becomes visible — a community expelling its prophet on the ground that he is too clean.

**Withholding the Second Half**

The first half, standing alone, reports a destruction and a refusal. The sūrah leaves it there for a full verse before naming God as the Mighty and the Merciful, and the delay is deliberate. Placing *al-Raḥīm* immediately after the rain of the warned makes a claim about the relationship between warning and punishment: the mercy was not withheld and then replaced by severity, the mercy *was* the warning, extended repeatedly through a man living in the town. The rain fell only after that extension had been used up.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:175

> **And truly thy Lord is indeed the Mighty, the Merciful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain After Lot**

Lot's narrative is the shortest of the prophetic accounts and the most compressed in its account of the punishment: *"And We rained a rain upon them. Evil was the rain of the warned!"* The sūrah then closes with the refrain. The juxtaposition is jarring by design — a verse describing a rain of destruction followed immediately by the assertion that the Lord is the Merciful — and the jarring is where the meaning sits.

The commentators connect this rain to the parallel reference in Sūrah al-Furqān, *"And indeed they passed by the town upon which fell the evil rain"* (25:40), and note the identification with the rain of fire and sulfur in the biblical account. What the Qur'anic narration adds is the word *warned*. The rain is described as evil not because it was indiscriminate but because it fell on people who had been told in advance, repeatedly, by a man living among them.

**Mercy Named at the Point of Judgement**

To place *al-Raḥīm* directly after the destruction of Lot's people is to make a claim about the relationship between warning and punishment. The mercy was not withheld and then replaced by severity; the mercy was the warning. Every verse of Lot's preaching — the call to reverence, the offer of no reward, the refusal to demand payment — was mercy extended in time, giving the hearers room to turn. The rain fell only after that room had been used up.

This is the reading al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī draw from the parallel passages on divine destruction: that God does not destroy a people until messengers have been sent to them (cf. 15:4; 17:15; 20:134; 28:59). The refrain, positioned here, is the sūrah's shorthand for that principle. It says in two Names what those verses say in sentences.

**The Two Halves Held Together**

A reader who takes only the first half of the refrain from verse 174 concludes that Lot's people are simply an example of unbelief. A reader who takes only this verse concludes that God is merciful. The sūrah's structure insists on both, in order, with no connective between them. The effect is that neither half can be used to dissolve the other: the mercy cannot be invoked to soften the account of the rain, and the rain cannot be invoked to cancel the mercy.

That is the sūrah's central discipline, and by its fifth repetition it has become the reader's own habit of reading. Every prophetic history closes the same way, and the reader has learned to expect the turn from human failure to divine character. What the refrain ultimately teaches is not a proposition about God but a way of holding history — seeing the refusal clearly and refusing to let the refusal be the final statement about the One who was refused.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:176

> **The inhabitants of the thicket denied the messengers,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Seventh Prophetic Narrative: Shuʿayb**

The seventh and final prophetic narrative in the sūrah is that of Shuʿayb and the "inhabitants of the thicket" (*aṣḥāb al-aykah). The "thicket" (*aykah) refers to a tangled grove of trees — possibly a specific type of tree associated with the area. The inhabitants of the thicket are often identified as the people of Midian, to whom Shuʿayb was also sent (29:36), though some commentators distinguish between the people of Midian and the inhabitants of the thicket as two separate groups.

**The Final Pattern**

This is the last of the seven prophetic narratives, and it follows the standard pattern for the final time. The repetition — now completed seven times — has driven the point home: the message is one, the pattern is consistent, and God's justice operates the same way with every community.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:177

> **when Shuʿayb said unto them, “Will you not be reverent?”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prophet of the Thicket**

The sūrah's final prophetic narrative concerns the *Aykah*, the thicket. The commentators explain the term as a tangled or twisted tree, or a group of trees, and note its appearance at 15:78, 26:176, 38:13 and 50:14; the inhabitants are associated in the tradition with Madyan, and al-Ṭabarī's treatment of Shuʿayb at 7:85 bears on the identification. What matters for this sūrah is the setting the name supplies: a community defined by its vegetation, its water and, above all, its trade.

Shuʿayb opens with the same question as every other prophet in the chapter — *Will you not be reverent?* — and the sūrah's insistence on the formula is now in its fifth repetition. The reader has learned what the question means and where it leads, which changes how it lands.

**A Question Aimed at Commerce**

The verses that follow identify the transgression precisely: short measure, dishonest balance, the diminishment of other people's goods, and corruption worked upon the earth. This is the sūrah's only sustained treatment of economic injustice, and the *taqwā* question is aimed at it directly. Al-Rāzī reads *taqwā* in these openings as mindfulness of disobedience, and in a market setting that mindfulness has a very concrete content — the awareness that a transaction has a second party whose interests are not present in the weighing.

The failure is therefore not greed in the abstract but a specific defect of attention. The merchant who shortens the measure is attending to his own margin and not to the person across the counter. The question asks him to widen his attention, and it asks before any command is given.

**The Brother Among Merchants**

The sūrah calls Shuʿayb *their brother*, and in a trading town the description carries commercial weight. He was not an outside reformer proposing rules to a market he did not participate in; he was a man whose dealings his neighbours had observed. That is what makes the demand for honest weights credible rather than merely exhortatory.

The pattern by now is fixed: every prophet in this sūrah is one of his own people, known to them, addressing a specific and verifiable failure. The uniformity is the sūrah's argument about how revelation actually reaches human beings — not through strangers bearing general principles, but through neighbours naming particular practices.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:178

> **Truly I am a trustworthy messenger unto you.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Trust in the Domain Where Trust Is Measured**

The *amīn* claim reaches its most literal application with Shuʿayb. In a commercial community, trustworthiness is not a general moral quality but a professional one with a public record: does this man's measure hold, does his balance level, does his word match his contract? Shuʿayb's claim appeals to that record, and it is the strongest form the claim takes anywhere in the sūrah because the domain in which it is asserted is the domain in which it can be tested daily.

Al-Rāzī's analysis of *amīn* as one in whom a deposit may safely be left applies with particular force here. The message is a deposit; so is the grain in a merchant's scale. A man habitually dishonest in the second cannot credibly claim fidelity in the first, and the community is well placed to know which it is.

**The Claim and the Charge to Come**

The verses following this one record the community's answer: that Shuʿayb is bewitched, that he is only a human being like them, and that they consider him among the liars. The *amīn* claim is placed immediately before those charges, and the sūrah's ordering lets the reader compare them. A lifetime of honest dealing is answered with an allegation of sorcery — a charge that cannot be tested, offered against a man whose character could be.

Al-Qurṭubī notes the recurring pattern in which a community's accusation reveals the accusers. Here it does so with unusual clarity: the community abandons the one ground on which it could have argued, Shuʿayb's known conduct, and retreats to an unverifiable claim about his mental state.

**The Fifth Repetition of a Fixed Credential**

By this point the sūrah has given the identical trust claim to Noah, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Lot and Shuʿayb. The credential does not vary with the audience's wealth, its technology or its particular sin. Al-Rāzī's principle — that the path to recognising a messenger is universal and does not change with historical circumstance — is demonstrated by the repetition rather than argued.

For the sūrah's first hearers the application was immediate. The Prophet ﷺ was known among the Quraysh as *al-Amīn* before the revelation, and al-Qurṭubī draws the parallel explicitly: he had given them no reason to mistrust him. The verse reports what Shuʿayb said to the people of the thicket and, without naming anyone, answers the men making the same charges in Makkah.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:179

> **So reverence God and obey me.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Obedience Specified as Commercial Restraint**

The third line of the formula returns unchanged, and in Shuʿayb's case the obedience demanded has an exact content given in the verses that follow: weigh with the right balance, do not diminish people's goods, do not behave wickedly upon the earth working corruption. The demand is not for belief in the abstract but for a change in how weights are handled — one of the most falsifiable commands in the Qur'an.

Al-Rāzī's ordering principle holds: the call to reverence precedes the call to obey because the second depends on the first. In a market this is practically true as well as theologically. A merchant who does not regard the transaction as answerable to God has no reason to level the balance when no one is watching, and no regulator can supply that reason.

**The Link Between Fraud and *Fasād***

The verse's demand is immediately followed by the prohibition against behaving wickedly upon the earth and working corruption — *fasād*. The commentators connect the two: the acts referred to include highway robbery, piracy, raiding and the destruction of crops, and they are treated as extensions of the same disposition that shortens the measure. Al-Rāzī's gloss on the related passages is that the warning against prodigality means one should not be seduced by those who seek the world excessively, but should seek contentment instead.

That connection is the sūrah's sharpest social observation. Fraudulent weighing, violent seizure and ecological destruction are not separate vices but one appetite operating at increasing scale. The command to revere God is addressed to the appetite at its root, which is why it comes first.

**Contentment as the Formed Disposition**

*Taqwā* here issues in what the commentators name as *qanāʿah*, contentment — the settled conviction that one's portion need not be enlarged at another's expense. This is the positive content of the demand, and it explains why the sūrah frames a commercial injunction as a matter of reverence rather than of regulation. A balance can be inspected; contentment cannot. Only the first disposition makes the second reliable.

The verse's brevity conceals a complete moral programme. Reverence God — adopt the stance of a person who is answerable. Obey me — implement it in the specific practice I have been sent to correct. Everything the following verses elaborate is contained in those two clauses.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:180

> **And I ask not of you any reward for it; my reward lies only with the Lord of the worlds.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refusal That Reframes the Market**

No refusal of payment is more pointed than one addressed to a community whose sin is taking more than its share. Shuʿayb is asked to accept a message that will cost him margin, and he declines to charge for delivering it. The refusal is not merely evidence of sincerity; it is a demonstration of the disposition he is commanding. He asks for honest measure and practises unpaid delivery.

Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī treat the messengers' refusal of enrichment as a standing Qur'anic theme with evidential force, and here the theme and the message coincide. The messenger's conduct is an instance of his instruction, which is the strongest possible answer to a community inclined to treat the instruction as self-serving.

**Reward Outside the Ledger**

*My reward lies only with the Lord of the worlds* removes the transaction from the ledger entirely. Al-Rāzī's wide reading of *al-ʿālamīn* — every level of created existence — means the compensation Shuʿayb expects is not denominated in the currency his opponents control. In a town where every relationship was mediated by exchange, the statement would have been immediately intelligible as a withdrawal from the system.

The withdrawal is what makes his command enforceable in conscience. A reformer who profits from reform can be suspected of manufacturing grievances. One who has declined payment cannot, and the sūrah places the refusal immediately before the community's counter-accusations so that the reader holds the two together.

**The Last of the Openings**

This is the fifth and final completion of the four-line prophetic formula in the sūrah. From Pharaoh's court to the thicket, the grammar has not varied: a question about reverence, a claim of known trustworthiness, a demand for reverence and obedience, and a refusal of payment. Five societies — a tyranny, an idolatrous community, an architecturally proud civilisation, a sexually corrupt town, a fraudulent market — and one identical credential.

The formal consistency is the sūrah's argument in its most compact form. The variety of human refusal is enormous; the shape of the address that meets it does not change. After this verse the narratives end, the refrain closes the series for the seventh time, and the sūrah turns to defend the origin of its own text and to address its Prophet directly.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:181

> **Give full measure, and be not among those who fall short.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Specific Sin: Economic Corruption**

Unlike the other peoples in this sūrah, whose primary sins were idolatry, arrogance, or moral deviance, the inhabitants of the thicket are specifically charged with economic corruption: cheating in measures and weights. "Give full measure" (*awfū l-kayl) — do not shortchange your customers. "Weigh with the right balance" (*wazinū bi-l-qisṭāsi l-mustaqīm) — use honest scales. "Diminish not people's goods" (*wa-lā tabkhasū l-nāsa ashyaʾahum) — do not take what belongs to others.

**The Gravity of Economic Sin**

The focus on economic sin in this narrative is significant. While the ʿĀd were destroyed for arrogance and the people of Lot for moral corruption, the inhabitants of the thicket are destroyed for financial dishonesty. This teaches that economic corruption — cheating, fraud, exploitation — is not a minor sin but a grave one that can bring divine punishment. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the acts referred to by "behave wickedly upon the earth, working corruption" include highway robbery, piracy, raiding, and the destruction of crops — all forms of predatory behavior that undermine social trust.

**The Relevance to Modern Commerce**

The lesson is directly applicable to modern economic life: honesty in trade is not merely a matter of good business practice; it is a religious obligation with consequences. Every business person who cheats on weights and measures, every corporation that defrauds its customers, every system that enables exploitation — all of them are engaging in the same behavior that brought divine punishment upon the inhabitants of the thicket.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:182

> **Weigh with the right balance,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command in Its Most Testable Form**

Shuʿayb's central instruction is given in the vocabulary of the marketplace: *weigh with the right balance*. The commentators note that these verses instruct people not to cheat in matters of buying and selling, and the concreteness is the point. Of all the commands the sūrah records, this is the one most immediately falsifiable — a balance either levels or it does not, and any party to the transaction can check.

That falsifiability gives the command a moral weight that vaguer injunctions lack. A person can rationalise an attitude; he cannot rationalise a short weight in front of the customer watching it. The verse is therefore addressed to the exact point where intention becomes visible.

**The Balance as a Theological Instrument**

The Qur'an's use of weighing imagery is extensive — the scales of the Day of Judgement, the weighing of deeds, the balance established in creation — and the commentators read the commercial injunction as continuous with that imagery rather than separate from it. The balance in the market and the balance in the reckoning are the same instrument at different scales.

This is why a commercial command appears inside a prophetic mission at all. It is not an appendix of social legislation; it is the local instance of a cosmic principle. To level the balance is to align one's conduct with the way things are actually measured, and the sūrah treats the refusal to do so as a form of practical disbelief.

**From Measure to Disposition**

The command's aim is not merely correct arithmetic. Al-Rāzī's gloss on the related passages identifies contentment — *qanāʿah* — as the disposition the injunctions are forming, and notes that contentment is impossible in a person who believes he must take whatever he can get. The balance is the outward test; the disposition is the inward cause.

That is why the verse is preceded by the call to reverence. Without *taqwā*, a merchant levels the balance only when observed, and the command has achieved nothing durable. With it, the levelling is not a compliance behaviour but an expression of a settled conviction that the transaction is answerable to Someone who is present in it.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:183

> **and diminish not people's goods, nor behave wickedly upon the earth, working corruption.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From the Scale to the Goods Themselves**

The prohibition widens from the instrument of measurement to the substance measured. *Diminish not people's goods* covers every method of taking more than one's share, whether or not a balance is involved — short measure, adulteration, concealed defect, delayed payment, contract terms that transfer value invisibly. The commentators treat these verses as instructions against cheating in buying and selling, and the breadth of the wording supports that reading.

The phrase *people's goods* is worth pausing on. The goods are described as belonging to the other party before the transaction begins. The verse does not frame commerce as a contest in which each party takes what he can; it frames it as a transfer of something already owned, in which the taker is under constraint.

**The Extension to Violence and Destruction**

The second clause — *nor behave wickedly upon the earth, working corruption* — carries the commentators' wider reference. Al-Rāzī records that the acts described usually include highway robbery and piracy, raiding, and the destruction of crops. The verse thus spans the range from the shorted scale to the raided caravan and the burned field.

The span is the argument. These are not separate offences requiring separate prohibitions but gradations of a single disposition, and the sūrah names them together to show that the appetite which shortens a measure does not stop at the measure. On *fasād* and *iṣlāḥ* as an opposing pair, the commentators point to 2:220.

**The Community's Answer and Its Meaning**

The verses that follow record the response of the people of the thicket: that Shuʿayb is bewitched, that he is only a human being like them, that they deem him among the liars, and that they do not see him as strong among them. Not one of these answers the commercial charge. The community does not defend its weights; it attacks the man.

Al-Qurṭubī notes the recurring pattern in which a community's accusation reveals the accusers. Here the revelation is precise: a people confronted with a falsifiable claim about its own conduct responds with unverifiable claims about the claimant's mind and status. The refusal to engage the balance is itself the diagnosis, and the destruction that closes the narrative follows the refrain's seventh and final return.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:184

> **And reverence Him Who created you and the generations of old."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Appeal to the Creator**

Shuʿayb's call to reverence is grounded in creation: "reverence Him Who created you and the generations of old" (*w-ittaqu l-ladhī khalaqakum wa-l-jibillata l-awwalīn). The word *jibillah* — "generations" or "multitudes of creatures" — encompasses all the peoples who have lived before. The appeal is to the Creator of everything — the same God who created the current generation and all previous generations. The logic is the same as Abraham's: the Creator deserves worship because creation implies the Creator, and the Creator's right over the created is absolute.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:185

> **They said, “You are but one of the bewitched.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Standard Objections**

The inhabitants of the thicket repeat the same two objections used by every disbelieving community: (1) "You are bewitched" — the accusation of supernatural deception; (2) "You are but a human being like us" — the objection to the prophet's humanity. They add a third: "we deem you to be among the liars" — a direct accusation of dishonesty.

**The Escalation**

The addition of the "liars" accusation represents an escalation: from sorcery to humanity to dishonesty. Each objection is a different strategy for avoiding engagement with the message. None of them address the substance of Shuʿayb's call to honest dealing and reverence for God.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:186

> **You are naught but a human being like us, and indeed we deem you to be among the liars,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Objection That Concedes the Point**

The people of the thicket open their reply by stating a fact — that Shuʿayb is a human being like them — and then drawing an inference from it. The commentators note that this phrase appears repeatedly in connection with the prophets, at 11:27, 14:10, 21:3 and 23:24 and 33–34, and that it is spoken as an accusation by those who refuse to follow a mere human being.

What makes the objection self-defeating is that the same phrase is used by prophets about themselves, at 18:110 and 41:6, as a way of emphasising their humanity and the powerlessness they share with others before God. The premise is agreed; only the conclusion is disputed. The community assumes that a human being cannot carry a divine message, and the Qur'an's own prophets assert their humanity precisely to show that he can.

**The Charge of Lying Against a Known Man**

The second clause — *we deem you to be among the liars* — is where the accusation collapses. Shuʿayb had opened by claiming trustworthiness, and in a commercial town that claim rested on a public record of honest dealing. The community does not cite an instance of his dishonesty; it states a verdict about his character without evidence.

Al-Qurṭubī's observation about accusations that reveal the accusers applies directly. A charge of lying made against a man whose commercial integrity was daily verifiable, and unsupported by any instance, tells the reader more about the people making it than about the man it concerns. The sūrah places the *amīn* claim before the charge precisely so the two can be compared.

**The Sequence of a Refusal**

Read with the verses around it, the community's answer follows a recognisable order: he is bewitched, he is a human being like us, he is a liar, we do not see him as strong among us, and finally the demand that he produce a sign if he is truthful. Each move retreats further from the substantive charge, which concerned weights and measures.

That retreat is the sūrah's diagnosis. A community that cannot answer an argument about its own conduct escalates into claims about the arguer's mind, species, honesty and social standing. The final demand for a sign is not a genuine request for evidence but a way of postponing the question already asked, and the sūrah's answer to it is the destruction and the refrain.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:187

> **so make fragments from the sky fall upon us, if you are among the truthful."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Challenge for a Miracle**

The inhabitants of the thicket demand a specific miracle: "make fragments from the sky fall upon us" (*fa-asqiṭ kisafan mina l-samāʾ). The word *kisaf* — "fragments" — can mean pieces of sky, chunks of celestial matter, or simply a punishment from above. The demand is a challenge: if you are truly a prophet, produce a dramatic miracle — bring the sky down on us.

**The Mockery Behind the Challenge**

The challenge is not a genuine request for evidence; it is mockery. The demand is designed to be impossible — or so they think. They are saying: prove yourself by doing something truly spectacular — something so dramatic that even we cannot deny it. The irony is that they do not realize that the "dramatic miracle" they are asking for is precisely what will destroy them.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:188

> **He said, "My Lord knows best what you do."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Shuʿayb's Response**

Shuʿayb does not engage with the challenge; he deflects it to God: "My Lord knows best what you do" (*rabbi aʿlamu bi-mā taʿmalūn). He is saying: I am not the one who will punish you; God knows your deeds and He will deal with you in His time. The response reflects the prophetic posture: the prophet delivers the message; the punishment is God's prerogative.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:189

> **So they denied him, and the punishment of the day of gloom seized them. Truly it was the punishment of a tremendous day.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Punishment of the Day of Gloom**

The inhabitants of the thicket are destroyed by "the punishment of the day of gloom" (*ʿadhāba yawmi l-ẓullah). The "gloom" (*ẓullah) refers to a cloud or shadow — described in some accounts as a cloud that initially provided relief from a terrible heat, under which the people gathered for comfort, only to be destroyed by fire from within it. The punishment is doubly devastating: what appeared to be relief was actually destruction.

**The Deception of Apparent Comfort**

The narrative contains a powerful lesson: sometimes what appears to be comfort is actually the prelude to destruction. The cloud provided shade from the heat, and the people gathered under it gratefully — not knowing that it was the vehicle of their annihilation. This is a metaphor for every temptation that appears attractive but carries hidden destruction within it.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:190

> **Truly in that is a sign, but most of them are not believers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Final Refrain**

This is the seventh and first half of the sūrah's closing refrain. After it, and after the second half in the next verse, the sequence of prophetic narratives ends and the sūrah turns to defend the origin of its own text before addressing the Prophet ﷺ directly. The refrain's final appearance therefore works as a hinge: it closes the series, and by closing it in the familiar formula signals that the series is complete.

**Seven Histories, One Closing Sentence**

Counting back through the chapter, the refrain has closed the accounts of Pharaoh, Abraham, Noah, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Lot and Shuʿayb. Seven peoples, seven different forms of refusal — tyranny, idolatry, contempt for the poor, pride in civilisation, sexual transgression, commercial fraud — and one identical closing formula. The formal consistency is the sūrah's argument in miniature: the variety of human refusal is enormous, and the divine character it is set against does not vary.

**Repetition as Structure Rather Than Ornament**

The classical commentators marked this sūrah out for its rhythmic and poetic character, and the refrains binding the prophetic accounts together are the reason. A sign stated once is information; a sign followed seven times by the same closing formula becomes a rhythm the hearer learns to expect, and expectation is what converts a text into something memorisable. By the seventh occurrence the reader hears the second half arriving before it is spoken — which is precisely the effect the repetition was built to produce.

**What the Refrain Does Not Say**

The formula never promises that the next community will believe, and it never softens the report that most did not. Its comfort is the removal of isolation rather than the promise of improvement: the prophet hearing it is told that his situation is the standard shape of prophetic history, not a personal anomaly. The sūrah then leaves the histories and turns to the present, where the same pattern is still running. sin and every mode of rejection.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:191

> **And truly thy Lord is indeed the Mighty, the Merciful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain After Shuʿayb and the Final Turn**

This is the seventh and last occurrence of the refrain, and it closes the last of the prophetic narratives. After it the sūrah changes register entirely, turning from history to a defence of the Qur'an's own origin and then to its closing address to the Prophet ﷺ. The refrain's final appearance therefore functions as a hinge: it ends the series and, by ending it in the familiar formula, signals that the series is complete and that something different is beginning.

Shuʿayb's account is also the one most directly concerned with commerce — short measures, dishonest balances, the diminishment of other people's goods. Its closing refrain thus follows the sūrah's most sustained treatment of economic injustice, which sharpens what the two Names are doing here.

**Might and Mercy as the Ground of Economic Justice**

The connection is closer than it first appears. The fraud Shuʿayb confronted rests on an assumption about power: that the strong party in a transaction can take more than its share because the weak party cannot prevent it. Short measure is, in the end, a claim about who is mighty. The refrain answers that claim at the level where it is actually made. The might that decides what is taken and what is given is not the merchant's.

And the mercy is what makes the command something other than a threat. The commentators note that the injunctions against cheating in buying and selling are addressed to a community that had the power to ignore them; the reason given for compliance is not fear alone but gratitude to a Lord whose mercy is already extended. Al-Rāzī's gloss on the related passages is that contentment — *qanāʿah* — is the disposition the commands are forming, and contentment is impossible in a person who believes he must take what he can get.

**Seven Returns of One Sentence**

Counting back through the sūrah, the refrain has now closed the stories of Pharaoh, Abraham, Noah, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Lot and Shuʿayb. Seven histories, seven different peoples, seven different forms of refusal — tyranny, idolatry, contempt for the poor, pride in civilisation, sexual transgression, commercial fraud — and one closing sentence for all of them. That formal choice is the sūrah's argument in miniature: the variety of human refusal is enormous, and the divine character it is set against does not vary.

The classical commentators marked this sūrah out for its rhythmic structure precisely because of these refrains, and the rhythm is not decoration. Repetition at this scale creates the expectation that makes the final occurrence feel like a completion. The reader who reaches verse 191 hears the formula and knows the histories are done — and knows it without being told, which is what a refrain is for.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:192

> **And truly it is a revelation of the Lord of the worlds,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Transition: The Quran's Divine Origin**

With the completion of the seven prophetic narratives, the sūrah enters its final section — a powerful affirmation of the Qur'an's divine origin. "And truly it is a revelation of the Lord of the worlds" (*wa-innahu la-tanzīlu rabbi l-ʿālamīn). The word *tanzīl* — "revelation" or "sending down" — emphasizes the verticality of the Qur'an's origin: it came down from above, from the Lord of all that exists. It did not originate on earth; it descended from Heaven.

**The Connection to the Prophetic Narratives**

The placement of this section immediately after the seven prophetic narratives is deliberate. The narratives have demonstrated that the message of every prophet was the same; now the sūrah declares that the Qur'an — the final revelation brought by the final Prophet — is from the same Source. The Lord of the worlds who sent Moses, Abraham, Noah, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Lot, and Shuʿayb is the same Lord who sent down the Qur'an. The continuity is unbroken.

**"The Lord of the Worlds" as Source**

The attribution to "the Lord of the worlds" is not incidental. It is the same title used by Moses before Pharaoh (vv. 16, 24), by Abraham in his declaration of faith (v. 77), and by every prophet in this sūrah. The Qur'an's source is not a tribal god, a local deity, or a national protector; it is the Lord of everything that exists — the universal Sovereign whose message is relevant to all peoples in all times.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:193

> **brought down by the Trustworthy Spirit,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Trustworthy Spirit**

The Qur'an was brought down "by the Trustworthy Spirit" (*nazala bihi l-rūḥu l-amīn). The "Trustworthy Spirit" (*al-rūḥ al-amīn) is identified by the vast majority of commentators as the Archangel Gabriel — the angel of revelation, the intermediary between God and the prophets. Gabriel is called a "Spirit" (*rūḥ) because he is created from light or pure spirit, without the material dimension that characterizes human beings. He is "Trustworthy" (*amīn) because of his absolute faithfulness in conveying God's message without addition, subtraction, or distortion.

**The Significance of the Angelic Intermediary**

The mention of Gabriel serves multiple functions. First, it establishes the chain of transmission: God → Gabriel → Muhammad ﷺ → humanity. The message passes through a reliable intermediary, ensuring its integrity. Second, it distinguishes the Qur'an from other forms of supernatural communication: it did not come from satans (vv. 210–212), from jinn, or from any other source — it came through the most trustworthy of angels. Third, it connects this sūrah to its unique vocabulary: the Trustworthy Spirit is mentioned only here in the entire Qur'an, making this verse the definitive statement about the angelic mediation of revelation.

**The Connection to the Prophetic Theme**

The sūrah has just completed seven narratives about trustworthy prophets (*rusul umanaʾ*); now it introduces the Trustworthy Spirit. The parallelism is deliberate: just as every prophet in the sūrah was described as "trustworthy" (*amīn*), the one who brought the final revelation is also "trustworthy." The trustworthiness extends from the human messengers through the angelic intermediary all the way back to God Himself — the entire chain is reliable.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:194

> **upon thine heart—that thou mayest be among the warners—**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Upon the Heart**

The revelation was brought "upon thine heart" (*ʿalā qalbika). The Qur'an describes the reception of revelation as occurring on the heart — not the mind, not the brain, but the heart. Al-Rāzī discusses this at length: the heart in Qur'anic usage is the seat of understanding, the center of consciousness, the organ of spiritual perception. The revelation was not merely intellectual information; it was a transformative event that occurred at the deepest level of the Prophet's being.

**The Heart as the Seat of Knowledge**

Al-Rāzī explains that the Qur'anic meaning of the heart is the seat of understanding and knowledge, upon which the faculties of the soul depend. In contrast to those who identify the brain as the seat of intelligence, the Qur'an positions the heart as the center of comprehension — not denying the brain's physiological role but affirming that the heart has a spiritual and subtle reality of which the biological heart is only a symbol and reflection.

**"That Thou Mayest Be Among the Warners"**

The purpose of the revelation descending upon the Prophet's heart is stated: "that thou mayest be among the warners" (*li-takūna mina l-mun dharīn). The Prophet is not just a recipient of revelation; he is commissioned as a warner — one who communicates the warning to others. The revelation upon his heart transforms him from a recipient into a messenger, from a student into a teacher, from a servant who listens into a prophet who speaks.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:195

> **in a clear, Arabic tongue.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Arabic Language of the Qur'an**

The revelation came "in a clear, Arabic tongue" (*bi-lisānin ʿarabiyyin mubīn). The language of the Qur'an is Arabic — specifically, the clear, eloquent Arabic of the Prophet's people. This is not incidental; it is essential. A message must be in the language of its recipients to be understood. The Qur'an was sent to an Arab audience first, and its Arabic is part of its miraculous nature — its eloquence, its precision, its rhetorical power are all properties of the Arabic language at its highest.

**The Significance of Linguistic Clarity**

The word *mubīn* — "clear" — emphasizes that the Qur'an's Arabic is not obscure or difficult but accessible and comprehensible. It is Arabic at its most eloquent and most clear — a combination that is itself miraculous. The Arabic of the Qur'an is simultaneously the most linguistically sophisticated speech in the language and the most accessible to any Arabic speaker willing to understand.

**The Relevance to the People**

The fact that the Qur'an is in Arabic is mentioned elsewhere as a sign of its accessibility to the people to whom it was first revealed:

> *"We have sent it down as an Arabic Qur'an so that you may understand."* (Qur'an 12:2)

The language is not a barrier; it is a bridge. The message comes in the listener's own tongue — making the excuse of incomprehension unavailable.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:196

> **It is indeed in the scriptures of those of old.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Consistency with Previous Scriptures**

The verse declares: "It is indeed in the scriptures of those of old" (*wa-innahu la-fī zuburi l-awwalīn). The "scriptures" (*zubur) refer to the earlier revealed books — the Torah, the Psalms, the Gospel, and the scrolls of Abraham and Moses. The Qur'an's message is not new; it is consistent with what was revealed before. The mention of the earlier scriptures serves as a confirmation: the same God who sent those books has sent this one, and the message is continuous.

**The Prophet Mentioned in Earlier Scriptures**

Some commentators understand the verse as referring not only to the message but to the Prophet himself — that the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ is mentioned by name or description in the earlier scriptures. This interpretation is supported by verses such as 7:157: "Those who follow the Messenger, the unlettered prophet, whom they find written down with them in the Torah and the Gospel."

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:197

> **Is it not a sign for them that the learned of the Children of Israel know it?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Testimony of the Learned**

The verse poses a rhetorical question: is it not a sign that "the learned of the Children of Israel" (*ʿulamāʾu banī isrāʾīl) recognize the Qur'an's truth? The "learned" refers to the Jewish and Christian scholars who, because of their knowledge of the earlier scriptures, could recognize the Qur'an's consistency with them. Some commentators identify specific individuals — such as ʿAbdullāh ibn Salām, the learned Jewish scholar of Madinah who converted to Islam.

**The Scholarly Recognition as Evidence**

The testimony of the learned is presented as a sign — evidence that the Qur'an is from God. Those who know the earlier scriptures best are in the strongest position to recognize whether the new revelation is consistent with them. The fact that the most knowledgeable among the People of the Book recognized the Qur'an's authenticity is, the verse argues, a powerful sign for anyone willing to consider it.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:198

> **And had We sent it down upon some non-Arab,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Hypothetical Non-Arab Messenger**

The verse presents a hypothetical: "And had We sent it down upon some non-Arab" (*wa-law nazzalnāhu ʿalā baʿḍi l-aʿjamīn). The word *aʿjam* — "non-Arab" — refers to someone who does not speak Arabic, a foreigner. The hypothetical is: what if the Qur'an had been brought by someone who could not speak Arabic?

**The Absurdity of the Hypothetical**

The point of the hypothetical is to demonstrate its absurdity. A message in clear Arabic, brought by someone who cannot speak Arabic, would make no sense. The language of the message and the language of the messenger must match. This is why God sent the Qur'an through an Arab prophet in Arabic — the coherence is essential to the message's credibility.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:199

> **and he recited it unto them, they would not have believed in it.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Incredulity That Would Follow**

The hypothetical concludes: "they would not have believed in it" (*mā kānū bihī yuʾminūn). Even if a non-Arab brought the Qur'an and recited it to the Arabs, they would not have believed — because the mismatch between the messenger's language and the message's language would be too great. The point is that the Arabs' rejection of the Qur'an cannot be attributed to its language or its messenger; both are perfectly suited to them. The rejection is not about the form; it is about the heart.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:200

> **Thus do We place it into the hearts of the guilty;**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Quran in the Hearts of the Guilty**

The verse states a mysterious principle: "Thus do We place it into the hearts of the guilty" (*kadhālika salaknāhu fī qulūbi l-mujrimīn). The "it" can refer to the Qur'an itself or to the denial of it. Some commentators understand the verse as meaning that God causes the denial of the Qur'an to be placed in the hearts of those who are already guilty — their prior rejection and wrongdoing creates a spiritual condition in which belief becomes impossible.

**The Principle of Sealed Hearts**

The verse touches on one of the most debated topics in Islamic theology: the relationship between divine decree and human responsibility. The "placement" in the hearts of the guilty is understood by some as God's sealing of hearts that have already chosen to reject — a consequence, not a cause. The guilty have made their choices; God's "placement" is the natural outcome of those choices. Others understand it as God's predestining certain souls for disbelief — a position that emphasizes divine sovereignty.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:201

> **they will not believe in it till they see the painful punishment;**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Belief That Comes Too Late**

The verse states: "they will not believe in it till they see the painful punishment" (*wa-lan yuʾminū bihī ḥattā yaraw l-ʿadhāba l-alīm). This is the belief that comes only when the punishment is visible — a belief that does not avail, because it is not faith but mere recognition of reality. When the punishment is in front of your eyes, believing is not faith; it is the acknowledgment of an unavoidable fact.

**The Condition for True Faith**

The verse implies that true faith must precede the sight of punishment. Faith that requires visible evidence of the Hereafter is not faith at all. The test of this world is precisely that the Hereafter is unseen — believed on the basis of God's word, not on the basis of visible proof. Those who wait until they see the punishment before believing have failed the test.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:202

> **so it will come upon them suddenly, while they are unaware.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Suddenness of the Punishment**

The punishment will come "suddenly, while they are unaware" (*bayghatah wa-hum lā yashʿurūn). The word *bayghatah* — "suddenly" — conveys the complete unexpectedness of the punishment. It arrives without warning, without preamble, without giving the guilty time to prepare. The unawareness (*lā yashʿurūn) emphasizes that they will not even realize what is happening until it is upon them.

**The Lesson About Preparedness**

The suddenness of the punishment is a standing warning: do not assume that because punishment has not yet come, it will not come. The guilty often feel secure precisely because their punishment has been delayed — but the delay is not a cancellation. It is a period of grace during which repentance is possible; when it ends, the punishment arrives without notice.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:203

> **Then they will say, "Will we be granted respite?" Do they seek to hasten Our Punishment?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Request for Respite**

When the punishment arrives, the guilty will ask for a delay: "Will we be granted respite?" (*a-fa-bi-ʿadhābinā yastaʿjilūn). The request is the universal cry of those who face consequences they did not expect: "Give me more time!" But the time for respite was in the world — when the warnings were delivered, when the prophets called, when the signs were displayed. Now, the time for respite has passed.

**The Irony of Hastening**

The verse notes the irony: in the world, the disbelievers mockingly challenged the prophets to bring the punishment: "Bring it on, if you are truthful!" Now, facing the punishment, they want it delayed. The wish has been reversed: those who hastened now beg for delay. The reversal is itself a form of punishment — the realization that the attitude they adopted in the world has led to the very outcome they now dread.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:204

> **Do they seek to hasten Our Punishment?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Mockery Turned Into a Question**

The sūrah now addresses its own audience directly, and it does so with a question that quotes their mockery back at them. The commentators note that the disbelievers would mockingly seek to hasten God's punishment in this life, as recorded at verse 187, and that at the moment of truth they will instead seek to have it delayed. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Rāzī both read the verse against that reversal.

The rhetorical force lies in the tense. The demand to hasten the punishment is made in confidence, from a position of apparent safety, by people who do not believe it can arrive. The question asks them to consider the demand as something they have actually made — a request on file, as it were, which may be granted.

**The Reversal the Verse Anticipates**

Al-Rāzī's point about the moment of truth is the verse's real subject.
