The same people who challenged the punishment to come will later ask for delay, and the commentators connect this to the Qur'an's repeated depiction of those who wish for a second chance at life in order to do good — as at 2:167 and 39:58. The desire for respite is universal once the possibility of reversal has closed.

This gives the question its weight. It is not a taunt but a correction of a miscalculation: the challenge to hasten the punishment assumes that the challenger controls the timing, and the verse identifies that assumption as the error. The timing was never in the challenger's hands.

**From History to the Present Audience**

The placement is significant. The verse follows the seven prophetic narratives and the seventh refrain, and it turns from what happened to ancient peoples to what the current audience is doing. The transition asserts that the situation is continuous — the same challenge, the same reversal, the same outcome.

The sūrah has spent its length establishing the pattern; this verse applies it. The people who mocked are being told that they are inside a structure they have already seen described seven times, and that the structure does not stop for those who decline to notice it.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:205

> **Consider: if We grant them enjoyment for years,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Futility of Worldly Enjoyment**

These verses pose a rhetorical question: even if God gives the disbelievers years of enjoyment — health, wealth, comfort, pleasure — what will it avail them when the promised punishment comes? The answer is implicit: nothing. The years of enjoyment will not serve as a defense, a ransom, or a mitigation. When the punishment arrives, everything enjoyed before becomes irrelevant.

**The Added Ruin of Entrenchment**

Al-Ṭabarī adds a darker dimension: the years of enjoyment may actually add to the disbelievers' ruin, because each year without repentance deepens the entrenchment in disbelief. The longer the delay, the harder the heart; the more comfortable the life, the greater the reluctance to change. The enjoyment becomes not a mercy but a trap — a gilded cage that makes the eventual punishment more devastating because the fall from comfort to destruction is so much greater.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:206

> **and there comes upon them that which they were promised,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Conditional Completed**

The verse supplies the second half of a supposition begun in the preceding line: *if We let them enjoy for years, and then there comes upon them that which they were promised*. The structure is a thought experiment, and its purpose is to test whether duration changes the outcome.

Al-Qurṭubī reads the passage as addressed to the idolaters of Makkah, noting that even if the promised punishment were not to arrive for a long time, the things of the world they enjoyed would be of no use to them in the Hereafter. The verse therefore concedes the premise its hearers most want granted — that the delay might be long, perhaps indefinitely — in order to show that the concession changes nothing.

**What *That Which They Were Promised* Means**

The phrase is deliberately general. It covers the punishment the mockers had challenged God to send, and it covers the reckoning the sūrah has described throughout. The generality is purposeful: the verse does not need to specify the form of the arrival, because the argument turns on the fact of arrival rather than on its manner.

This connects to the challenge in verse 204. The mockers demanded the punishment be hastened because they did not believe it would come. The verse grants that it may come late and shows that lateness is not absence. The promise stands whether or not the timescale suits those it addresses.

**The Insignificance of the Interim**

The verse's function is to reduce the value of the intervening years to zero at the moment of arrival. Whatever was enjoyed during the delay is not carried across; it is not even a mitigating memory. The commentators' further observation is that in the case of the Makkan idolaters the enjoyment would only add to their ruin, since they would have become entrenched in what they were doing.

That is the sharper half of the argument. The delay is not neutral time in which anything might be reconsidered; it is time in which the disposition hardens. The verse therefore removes both comforts available to the hearer — that the punishment may not come, and that if it comes late there will have been some benefit in the meantime.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:207

> **of what avail to them will be that which they were made to enjoy?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question That Answers the Thought Experiment**

The supposition opened two verses earlier now receives its answer, and the answer is itself a question — the form the sūrah prefers when the conclusion is one the hearer must reach himself. *Of what avail to them will be that which they were made to enjoy?* The expected answer is *none*, and arriving at it is the work the verse assigns.

Al-Qurṭubī's reading is explicit: even if the promised punishment were delayed for years, the worldly things they enjoyed would be of no use to them in the Hereafter. The verse is not asserting that enjoyment is worthless in itself; it is asserting that it does not transfer. Whatever value it had was confined to the period in which it was experienced.

**The Verb *Muttiʿū*: Enjoyment as Something Granted**

The word used is from the root *m-t-ʿ*, to be given provision or enjoyment for a time. The form is passive — *that which they were made to enjoy* — and the passivity matters. The enjoyment is described as something conferred, not acquired, which connects back to the inventories given to ʿĀd and Thamūd and to al-Qurṭubī's principle that the things listed were made subservient to human beings and so God should be thanked for them.

A granted enjoyment carries an implied term. It was given for a period, and the verse asks what remains of it once the period ends. The question is not rhetorical in the dismissive sense; it is a genuine accounting question, and the answer follows from the nature of the grant.

**Enjoyment That Increases Ruin**

The commentators add a second consideration: in the case of those addressed, the enjoyment would only add to their ruin, because they would have become entrenched in their conduct. The delay does not preserve an opportunity; it deepens a habit. By the time the promised arrives, the person who enjoyed has become someone for whom the enjoyment has had a formative effect, and not a benign one.

This closes the sūrah's argument about prosperity. Abundance is not evidence of favour, delay is not evidence of safety, and the interim is not neutral time. The three claims together answer the challenge of verse 204 completely: the mockers asked for the punishment to be hastened because they thought its absence proved its unreality, and the sūrah shows that its absence proves nothing at all.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:208

> **And never did We destroy a town save that it had warners,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Justice of Divine Destruction**

These verses establish a fundamental principle of divine justice: "never did We destroy a town save that it had warners" (*mā ahlaknā min qaryatin illā lahā mun dhirūn). God does not destroy any community without first sending warners — prophets, messengers, signs, reminders. The destruction is never arbitrary, never unjust, never without due process. Every destroyed community had its chance: the message was delivered, the evidence was presented, the warning was given.

**"We Did No Wrong"**

The declaration "We did no wrong" (*wa-mā kunnā ẓālimīn) is God's testimony to His own justice. The destructions described in this sūrah — Pharaoh, ʿĀd, Thamūd, Lot's people, the inhabitants of the thicket — were all just. No community was destroyed without warning; no punishment was administered without the opportunity for repentance. The divine justice is absolute: God wrongs no one, and the punishment of every community is its own fault.

**The Principle of Warning Before Punishment**

This principle — warning before punishment — is one of the most important in the Qur'an. It appears in multiple passages (15:4; 17:15; 20:134; 28:59) and establishes that God's justice operates with full due process. No soul can say on the Day of Judgment: "I was not warned." Every community has been warned — through prophets, through scripture, through the natural signs, through the conscience. The warning is universal; the accountability is individual.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:209

> **as a reminder; and We did no wrong.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Function of the Warners**

The verse completes the sūrah's statement of why messengers are sent, and the word *reminder* — *dhikrā* — defines their function precisely. They do not bring new information about a God previously unknown; they recall what has been obscured. This is consistent with the sūrah's whole treatment of the prophets, each of whom addresses a specific failure in a community that already possesses the conceptual apparatus to recognise it.

The commentators note an alternative reading in which *as a reminder* is not a clause modifying *warners* but the second half of a phrase whose first half is implicit — *they had warners, [and the warning was] a reminder*. Both readings give the same result: the sending of warners is an act of disclosure, undertaken before any punishment.

***We Did No Wrong*: The Principle of Prior Warning**

The closing clause states the principle that governs every destruction the sūrah has narrated. The commentators direct the reader to the parallel statements at 15:4, 17:15, 20:134 and 28:59, all of which establish that God did not destroy any people without first giving them the opportunity to be righteous. The claim *We did no wrong* is therefore not a bare assertion but the conclusion of an argument the sūrah has just spent two hundred verses supplying.

Each of the seven narratives is evidence. In every case a messenger was sent, lived among the people, was known to them, asked no payment, stated his demand clearly, and was refused. The destruction in each case followed that sequence. The verse generalises from the seven instances to the principle, and the generalisation is earned.

**The Answer to the Charge of Arbitrary Violence**

The clause also answers an accusation the sūrah anticipates: that the destructions it has described are acts of unprovoked power. The word *wrong* — *ẓulm* — is the operative term, denoting the placing of a thing where it does not belong, the taking of what is not due. To say *We did no wrong* is to claim that each punishment was proportionate, preceded and deserved.

Set against verse 173 — *Evil was the rain of the warned* — the two statements form the sūrah's complete theodicy. The rain was evil because the warning had been given; God did no wrong because the warning had been given. The mercy and the justice are not in tension; they are both functions of the same prior fact.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:210

> **And the satans did not descend with it,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Exclusion of Satanic Origin**

The sūrah now turns to address a specific accusation: that the Qur'an was brought by satans. The response is absolute: "the satans did not descend with it" (*wa-mā tanazzalat bihī l-shayāṭīn). The Qur'an did not come from satans; it is not satanic in origin. The denial is threefold: (1) satans did not bring it; (2) it is not fitting for them to bring it; (3) they are not able to bring it.

**Why It Is Not Fitting**

The Qur'an is not fitting for satans because of its content: satans bring corruption, misguidance, and evil; the Qur'an enjoins right, forbids wrong, and is a light and guidance. The nature of the message is incompatible with its supposed satanic origin. A message that calls to God, to morality, to justice, to the Hereafter cannot come from beings whose nature is rebellion against God and corruption of humanity.

**Why They Are Not Able**

The satans are "debarred from hearing" (*innahum ʿani l-samʿi la-maḥjūbūn) — they cannot access the divine communication. The heavens are guarded against them:

> *"We reached out to Heaven and found it filled with mighty sentries and flaming stars."* (Qur'an 72:8)

The satans can only eavesdrop — catching fragments of celestial discourse and passing them to their human allies, adding lies to whatever truth they overhear (see vv. 221–223). They cannot bring a complete, coherent, divinely-originated message like the Qur'an. The very structure and consistency of the Qur'an is evidence against its satanic origin.

**The Relevance to the Accusation Against the Prophet**

The Quraysh accused the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ of being influenced by satans or jinn — the same accusation leveled against every prophet. These verses dismantle the accusation with surgical precision: satans could not have brought this message, because the message is incompatible with their nature, beyond their capacity, and guarded against their access. The accusation is not merely false; it is logically impossible.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:211

> **nor is it fitting for them, nor are they able.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Exclusions in One Line**

The verse continues the sūrah's defence of the Qur'an's origin by excluding the satans from it on two distinct grounds. The first is that it is not *fitting* for them — a claim about suitability, about what kind of being could carry what kind of message. The second is that they are not *able* — a claim about capacity. The two are not redundant; they rule out the possibility from opposite directions.

Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī develop the first: the satans bring corruption and misguidance to God's servants, whereas the Qur'an enjoins right and forbids wrong and is a light and guidance. A message whose content is moral correction cannot originate in a source whose activity is moral subversion, because the content would contradict the source's nature.

**The Argument from Inability**

The second exclusion is the more technical. The commentators note that the satans would not be able to bring such a thing even if they desired to do so, which addresses the possibility that a hostile source might nevertheless produce a true text. The exclusion is not merely that they would not want to but that they could not.

Ibn Kathīr supports the suitability of a non-human bearer of revelation by citing 59:21 — *"Had We made this Qur'an descend upon a mountain, thou wouldst have seen it humbled, rent asunder by the fear of God"* — establishing that the message has a weight that determines what can bear it. The capacity argument and the suitability argument converge on the same conclusion from different premises.

**Why the Sūrah Argues This Here**

The placement is the closing movement of the chapter's defence of its own origin, begun at verse 193 with the assertion that the Qur'an was brought down by the Trustworthy Spirit upon the Prophet's heart. Having stated the positive claim, the sūrah now removes the alternative explanations available to its opponents — that the text came from poets, from soothsayers, or from the satans who were thought to inspire them.

The argument matters because the accusation was live. Pre-Islamic Arabian culture attributed inspired speech to jinn and satans, and the Qur'an's opponents used that framework to explain the Prophet's ﷺ recitation. The verse closes that explanation on both grounds at once: the satans neither would nor could produce this.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:212

> **Indeed, they are debarred from hearing [it].**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Final Exclusion**

The verse completes the argument by removing the last available route. It is not enough to establish that the satans would not bring the message and could not; a listener might still suppose they overheard it and passed it on. The verse forecloses that possibility: they are debarred from hearing it.

Ibn Kathīr connects the statement to the jinn's own testimony at 72:8 — *"We reached out to Heaven and found it filled with mighty sentries and flaming stars"* — and the commentators point to the parallel treatments at 15:17–18 and 37:6–10, which describe the heavens as guarded against eavesdropping. The exclusion is thus presented not as a theological inference but as a reported feature of the unseen order.

**The Threefold Argument Complete**

Read with the two preceding verses, the sūrah has now excluded the satans in three ways: it is not fitting for them, they are not able, and they cannot hear. Each exclusion answers a different objection — that they might be the source by nature, that they might be the source by power, and that they might be intermediaries rather than sources.

The completeness is characteristic of Qur'anic argumentation on this subject. The claim being defended is central enough that partial defence would be insufficient: if the Qur'an's origin were contestable, nothing else in it would stand. The sūrah therefore closes every route at once rather than the most plausible one.

**The Sūrah's Closing Movement**

These verses sit immediately before the sūrah's final address to the Prophet ﷺ, and their function is preparatory. Having established that the message is neither poetic nor demonic in origin, the sūrah can turn to its practical conclusion — the instruction to warn the nearest clan, to lower the wing to the believers, and to trust the One who sees him when he stands and when he turns among those who prostrate.

The order is deliberate. Assurance about the message's origin precedes the command to deliver it. A messenger who doubts his commission cannot lower his wing to anyone; the sūrah removes the doubt before issuing the instruction, and it removes it with an argument rather than an assertion.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:213

> **So call not upon another god along with God, lest thou be among the punished.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Warning to the Prophet**

The verse addresses the Prophet directly: "call not upon another god along with God" (*fa-lā tadʿu maʿa Llāhi ilāhan ākhar). This command, while addressed to the Prophet, is understood by all commentators as applying to all believers. The warning is absolute: do not associate any partner with God. The consequence is equally absolute: "lest thou be among the punished" (*fa-takūna mina l-muʿadh dhabīn). Shirk — associating partners with God — is the only sin that places a person among the punished.

**The Universality of the Warning**

Although the verse is in the second-person singular (addressing the Prophet), its warning extends to every human being. The Prophet is addressed not because he is in danger of shirk — the prophets are protected from it — but because the address to the most revered of human beings emphasizes the gravity of the sin. If even the Prophet is warned against shirk, how much more must every other person be warned.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:214

> **And warn thy tribe, thy nearest kin,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command to Warn the Family**

God commands the Prophet to warn even his own family: "And warn thy tribe, thy nearest kin" (*wa-ʾandhir ʿashīrataka l-aqrabīn). This verse was fulfilled when the Prophet gathered his closest relatives and addressed them directly. The account is preserved in multiple traditions: the Prophet called the Banū Hāshim to a meal and said:

> *"Purchase your souls from God! I can avail thee naught against God."*

He addressed his daughter Fāṭimah, his uncle ʿAbbās, and his aunt Ṣafiyyah by name, making clear that even family ties would not save them from God's judgment.

**The Universality of the Warning**

The command to warn one's nearest kin teaches that the prophetic message does not exempt anyone — not even the Prophet's own family. The warning is universal: your blood relationship to the Messenger of God will not save you if you do not believe. Each soul is individually accountable.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:215

> **and lower thy wing to the believers who follow thee.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command to Gentleness**

While the Prophet is commanded to warn forcefully, he is also commanded to be gentle with the believers: "lower thy wing to the believers who follow thee" (*wa-khfiḍ janāḥa-ka li-mani ttabaʿaka mina l-muʾminīn). The image is of a bird lowering its wing — a gesture of tenderness, protection, and humility. The Prophet is to be firm with the deniers and gentle with the believers — harsh with falsehood and soft with truth.

**The Symbolism of the Wing**

Al-Rāzī explains the symbolism: as a bird lowers its wing when it wishes to descend gently, so the Prophet should deal gently with the believers — not with the harshness of a warrior but with the tenderness of a caregiver. The "wing" (*janāḥ) can also mean "side" — suggesting that the Prophet should incline his side toward the believers, dealing with them in a posture of closeness and compassion.

**The Balance of Firmness and Gentleness**

The combination of verses 214 and 215 teaches the prophetic balance: firmness in warning the deniers, gentleness in nurturing the believers. The Prophet is not one-dimensional; he adapts his approach to the needs of the audience. This is a model for every leader, teacher, and parent: be firm with those who need correction and gentle with those who need encouragement.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:216

> **And should they disobey thee, say, "Truly I am quit of that which you do."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Disavowal of Disobedience**

The verse addresses the scenario where those who should follow the Prophet refuse to do so: "say, 'Truly I am quit of that which you do'" (*fa-in ʿaṣawka fa-qul innī barīʾun mimmā taʿmalūn). The word *barīʾ* — "quit" or "innocent" — means completely disassociated. The Prophet declares that he is not responsible for the disobedience of those who reject him. His task was delivery; their response is their own accountability.

**The Principle of Prophetic Responsibility**

This verse reiterates the principle stated throughout the sūrah: the prophet is responsible for delivery, not for the outcome. If the people disobey, the prophet is not accountable for their disobedience. He has warned; he has called; he has exemplified. What they do with the message is between them and God.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:217

> **And trust in the Mighty, the Merciful,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command to Trust**

The Prophet is commanded to "trust in the Mighty, the Merciful" (*wa-tawakkal ʿalā l-ʿazīzi l-raḥīm). The concept of *tawakkul* — trust in God — is a cornerstone of Islamic spirituality. It means placing one's reliance entirely on God, combining effort with trust, doing one's part and leaving the outcome to God. The pairing of "Mighty" and "Merciful" here is significant: trust in God is based on both His power (He is able to help) and His compassion (He is willing to help).

**The Foundation of Trust**

Trust in God is not blind optimism; it is based on knowledge of God's attributes. The Mighty — nothing can thwart His will. The Merciful — His compassion exceeds His wrath. The One who is both powerful enough and compassionate enough to care for His servant is the only legitimate object of trust.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:218

> **Who sees thee when thou standest [to pray],**

**Expanded Commentary**

**God's Awareness of the Prophet**

The verse describes God's intimate awareness of the Prophet: "Who sees thee when thou standest [to pray]" (*alladhī yarāka ḥīna taqūm). The "standing" refers to standing in prayer — particularly the night vigil (*tahajjud), which the Prophet maintained for long periods. God sees the Prophet in his most private moments of devotion — when no one else is watching, when the worship is between the servant and his Lord alone.

**The Comfort of Divine Observation**

The verse is a comfort: the God whom the Prophet worships sees him. The worship is not one-directional; it is witnessed by the One to whom it is directed. This awareness — that God sees you when you worship — transforms prayer from a lonely act into an intimate encounter. The Prophet's standing in the night, his bowing, his prostration — all of it is seen, known, and appreciated by God.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:219

> **and thy movement amid those who prostrate.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**God's Awareness of the Community**

The verse extends God's awareness to include the Prophet's movement among the worshippers: "and thy movement amid those who prostrate" (*wa-taqallubaka fī l-sājidīn). The word *taqallub* — "movement" or "turning" — refers to the Prophet's movements during prayer — his bowing, prostrating, and turning. It also refers to his movement among the believers — his leadership of the community in worship.

**The Prophet as Prayer Leader**

Al-Rāzī explains that this verse refers to the Prophet's role as leader of prayer — his movements of bowing and prostrating along with the other worshippers. God sees not only the Prophet standing alone but also his movement among the congregation — leading them, guiding them, embodying the worship they follow. The Prophet's leadership in prayer is itself an act of worship seen by God.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:220

> **Truly He is the Hearing, the Knowing.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Divine Attributes**

The verse concludes with two of God's Names: "Truly He is the Hearing, the Knowing" (*innahu huwa l-samīʿu l-ʿalīm). God hears everything — every prayer, every complaint, every whisper. God knows everything — every intention, every secret, every hidden reality. The pairing of Hearing and Knowing is significant: God not only hears the words but knows the hearts behind them. The Prophet's worship, his struggles, his leadership — all of it is heard and known by God.

**The Foundation of Trust**

The description of God as Hearing and Knowing is the foundation for the trust commanded in verse 217. You can trust the One who hears every word you speak and knows every thought in your heart — because He will not ignore your needs or misunderstand your situation.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:221

> **Shall I inform thee of those upon whom the satans descend?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Transition to the Topic of Poets**

The sūrah now enters its final section — a discussion of those upon whom satans descend, leading to the topic of poets. The rhetorical question "Shall I inform thee?" introduces a new theme: the contrast between divine revelation (brought by the Trustworthy Spirit) and satanic inspiration (received by soothsayers and certain poets).

**The Satanic Descent**

The "descent" of satans (*tanazzalu) refers to the way satans descend upon certain individuals — soothsayers, poets, and others who are open to their influence. The descent is not physical but spiritual: satans cast their whispers into the hearts of those who are receptive to them. The individuals upon whom they descend are described in the following verses.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:222

> **They descend upon every sinful liar,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Character of Those Who Receive Satanic Inspiration**

The satans descend upon "every sinful liar" (*kull aṯṯāmin kad dhāb). The two qualities — sinful and lying — define the recipients of satanic inspiration. They are sinful (*aṯhīm*) — morally corrupt, given to wrongdoing — and lying (*kad dhāb*) — habitually dishonest in their claims. The commentators identify these as the soothsayers (*kuhhān*) — individuals who claimed to have knowledge of the unseen by receiving information from satans.

**The Nature of Satanic Communication**

Al-Rāzī explains that the satans overhear fragments of celestial discourse from the angels and convey them to their human allies — the soothsayers. The soothsayers then add to these fragments a hundred lies, presenting the result as genuine knowledge of the unseen. The result is a mixture of truth and falsehood that is fundamentally deceptive.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:223

> **eavesdropping, and most of them are liars.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Mechanism of Satanic Knowledge**

The satans are "eavesdropping" (*yasta miʿūn) — listening in on the celestial discourse of the angels. The eavesdropping is described in more detail elsewhere:

> *"We reached out to Heaven and found it filled with mighty sentries and flaming stars."* (Qur'an 72:8)

The satans attempt to steal information from the heavens; they catch fragments; and they pass these fragments to their allies, who embellish them with lies.

**"Most of Them Are Liars"**

The phrase "most of them are liars" can mean that most of the soothsayers lie about what they hear from the satans — adding their own fabrications to the fragments they receive. Or it can mean that most of the satans lie to the soothsayers — giving them false information along with the occasional true fragment. Either way, the result is the same: the information that reaches the soothsayer's clients is overwhelmingly false, with just enough truth to make the lie plausible.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:224

> **And as for the poets, the errant follow them.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Poets and Their Followers**

The sūrah now turns to the poets: "And as for the poets, the errant follow them" (*wa-l-shuʿarāʾu yattabiʿuhumu l-ghāwūn). In pre-Islamic Arabia, poets (*shuʿarāʾ*) occupied a unique position. They were not merely literary artists; they were believed to possess a kind of supernatural inspiration — their words were thought to come from a source beyond the ordinary. The poet was associated with the soothsayer (*kāhin*) and the possessed (*majnūn*) as part of a spectrum of supernaturally-affected individuals.

**The "Errant" Who Follow**

The "errant" (*ghāwūn) who follow the poets are those who have gone astray — whether satans, misguided people, or the poets' own followers who spread their compositions. The verse establishes a connection between the poets and the errant: the poets' words lead the errant further astray, and the errant are the ones who most eagerly consume and propagate the poets' compositions.

**The Pre-Islamic Context**

In pre-Islamic Arabia, poetry was the primary medium of public communication, cultural memory, and tribal propaganda. Poets composed in praise of their tribe's virtues, satirized their enemies, and shaped public opinion through their words. Their influence was enormous — a poet's verse could start a war or end one, build up a reputation or destroy it. The Qur'an's address to the poets is thus an address to the most powerful communicators of the age.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:225

> **Hast thou not considered that they wander in every valley,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Wandering of the Poets**

The verse asks the Prophet to consider the poets' characteristic behavior: "they wander in every valley" (*allam tar annahum fī kulli wādin yahīmūn). The "wandering in every valley" is both literal and metaphorical. Literally, the poets roamed from tribe to tribe, composing for whoever paid them, without loyalty to any truth or principle. Metaphorically, they wandered through every topic, every mood, every excess — without the discipline of principle or the anchor of truth.

**The Lack of Direction**

Al-Rāzī observes that the poets' wandering reflects a fundamental lack of direction. They go wherever the wind of inspiration takes them — praising someone today and vilifying the same person tomorrow, depending on who is paying. They are not guided by truth but by whim; not anchored in principle but in profit. The "every valley" represents the undisciplined, directionless nature of their speech.

**The Contrast with the Qur'an**

The wandering of the poets is implicitly contrasted with the Qur'an's consistency. The Qur'an does not wander; it proceeds with purpose, consistency, and direction. Its message is the same from beginning to end; its moral vision does not shift with the audience. The contrast is between the directionless wandering of human speech and the purposeful guidance of divine speech.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:226

> **and that they say what they do not do?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Hypocrisy of the Poets**

The final criticism of the poets: "they say what they do not do" (*wa-annahum yaqūlūna mā lā yafʿalūn). The poets speak eloquently about virtues they do not practice, compose beautiful verses about values they do not embody. They praise generosity while being stingy, compose about courage while being cowardly, write about honor while behaving dishonorably. The gap between their speech and their actions is the fundamental flaw in their enterprise.

**The Principle of Embodied Speech**

The verse teaches a profound principle: words are only as valuable as the life behind them. Speech that is not embodied in action is hollow — beautiful on the surface but empty within. The Qur'an consistently emphasizes the connection between speech and action:

> *"O you who believe, why do you say what you do not do? Most hateful it is to God that you say what you do not do."* (Qur'an 61:2–3)

The poets, by this standard, are the antithesis of the prophetic model. The prophets did what they said; the poets say what they do not do. The prophets' speech was backed by their lives; the poets' speech was contradicted by their lives.

---
## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:227

> **Not so those who believe and perform righteous deeds, and remember God much, and help one another after having been wronged. And those who do wrong shall know to what homecoming they will return!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Exception: The Believing Poets**

The sūrah concludes with a crucial exception: "Not so those who believe and perform righteous deeds, and remember God much, and help one another after having been wronged." The exception carves out a category of poets who are fundamentally different from the errant wanderers described in the previous verses. These are poets who believe, who do righteous deeds, who remember God abundantly, and who use their poetic gifts to defend the truth after having been wronged.

**The Four Qualities of the Believing Poets**

The believing poets are distinguished by four qualities: (1) they believe — their faith is genuine and deep; (2) they perform righteous deeds — their actions match their words; (3) they remember God much — their hearts are anchored in divine consciousness; (4) they help one another after having been wronged — they use their talents to defend the community of believers against the attacks of its enemies. These four qualities are the exact opposite of the poets criticized in the previous verses: the errant wanderers were faithless, inactive, forgetful of God, and committed to no cause; the believing poets are faithful, active, God-conscious, and committed to the defense of truth.

**The Poets of the Prophetic Community**

The exception refers historically to the poets among the Prophet's Companions — such as Ḥassān ibn Thābit, ʿAbdullāh ibn al-Rawāḥah, and Kaʿb ibn Zuhayr — who used their poetic talents in the service of Islam. Ḥassān ibn Thābit was the Prophet's own poet, composing verses that defended the Muslims and responded to the satirical attacks of the Quraysh. The Prophet ﷺ said to Ḥassān:

> *"Versify against them, for Gabriel is with thee."*

The Prophet's endorsement demonstrates that poetry — when used in the service of truth, by believing poets who remember God — is not merely permitted but praised.

**The Principle: Speech Is Judged by Its Source and Purpose**

The sūrah's final teaching about poets establishes a universal principle: speech is not judged by its form but by its source and purpose. The poetry of the errant is condemned because it comes from a faithless, directionless, self-serving source. The poetry of the believers is praised because it comes from a faithful, God-conscious, truth-serving source. The same art form — poetry — can be either condemned or commended depending on the spiritual state of its practitioner and the purpose to which it is put.

**"Remember God Much"**

The quality of remembering God much (*dhakarū Llāha kathīran) is not a matter of quantity but of quality. The Sufi commentators explain that "much" does not refer to the number of times God is mentioned but to the depth and constancy of the remembrance — a state in which the consciousness of God pervades every moment and is not disrupted by habit or forgetfulness. The believing poets remember God not only in their verses but in their hearts — and this inner remembrance shapes every word they speak.

**"Help One Another After Having Been Wronged"**

The fourth quality — helping one another after having been wronged — is a reference to the use of poetry as a defensive weapon. The Quraysh used poetry to mock, satirize, and attack the believers. The believing poets responded in kind — not out of aggression but out of defense. The commentators note that the Prophet himself commanded this response, saying to ʿAbdullāh ibn al-Rawāḥah on the occasion of the conquest of Makkah:

> *"Leave him be, O ʿUmar, for it goes through them faster than an arrow."*

Poetry in the service of truth is "an arrow" — effective, precise, and penetrating.

**The Final Warning**

The sūrah concludes with a warning: "And those who do wrong shall know to what homecoming they will return!" The word "homecoming" (*munqalib*) means the place of return — the ultimate destination after death. The wrongdoers — whether poets, tyrants, or ordinary deniers — will all return to God and face the consequences of their choices. The sūrah that began with the disconnected letters and the signs of the clear Book ends with a reminder that every soul will return to its Lord — and the quality of that return depends on the quality of the life lived.

**The Sūrah's Final Message**

The sūrah's overarching message is now complete. Through seven prophetic narratives, it has demonstrated that the message of God is one, the rejection of it follows a predictable pattern, and the justice of God is certain. Through its discussion of the Qur'an's origin, it has established that the final revelation is from God — not from satans, not from poets, not from any human source. And through its conclusion about poets, it has drawn the final distinction: speech is either from God or from elsewhere; the believing poet who remembers God and serves the truth is the exception that proves the rule. The sūrah closes with the universal truth that underlies everything: the wrongdoers will return to God, and their homecoming will not be pleasant.

---
**[End of the commentary on Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ]**
